#6909. 5 Ways Warmth Has Shaped Interior Design in 2026, According to Experts

Warmth is more than a palette—it’s the secret to a home you never want to leave.

After years of cool neutrals and minimalist interiors, there’s been a shift toward warmer, more inviting spaces that feel layered, personal, and lived-in.

“Warmth has become more than just a color palette or a particular material. It's about spaces that feel authentic, comfortable, and reflective of the people who live in them,” says Laura Gerber, founder of ODE Design.

At the end of the day, a warm home should feel inviting and effortless, with materials and details that make people want to spend time there. If you’re looking to add some warmth to your home, take inspiration from these designer-approved ideas.

Wood’s organic grain brings instant warmth to both modern and traditional homes, adding depth without making a space feel overly formal.

“We’re incorporating more white oak, walnut, and rift-cut cabinetry than we have in years, along with wood ceilings and paneled walls,” explains Rachel Simpson, senior interior designer at Revive Design and Renovation.

There’s been a shift from stark white countertops toward natural quartzite, travertine, and marble with warmer veining. These materials have natural variation and movement that bring character into a space.

“Honed finishes, in particular, feel softer and more organic than high-polish surfaces. They allow the beauty of the stone to speak for itself without feeling overly formal or glossy,” explains Simpson.

These days, people really want their homes to feel like a sanctuary.

“In a recent project, the client envisioned a grown-up treehouse nestled among the evergreens. We wanted to create a warm yet contemporary mountain retreat that felt luxurious but also comfortable and welcoming,” says Charles Greenfeld, founder and principal designer at Ethan Charles Design.

Rather than relying on one or two dramatic statement pieces, the interiors were designed to be experienced as a whole, with each detail contributing to that warm aesthetic.

Creating warmth is really about creating layers of thoughtfully curated design elements. This helps to create a home you want to enter and never leave.

“Well-proportioned rugs, textures on walls, loads of art, draperies, and lighting overhead and at eye level are just some of the things we can add to our spaces to make them feel cozy and accessible,” says Ashley Powell, cofounder and interior designer of Roseberry Allen.

Designers have lately been drawn to rooms where the colors are close to one another, but the materials do different things.

“A woven rug, a textured wall, and a piece of wood with a visible grain each catch the light differently,” says Jess Klein, founder and principal of Jess Klein Studio.

That variation adds visual depth and makes the room feel warmer and more inviting, even when the color palette is understated.

#6907. How Tiffany & Co. Made a Bird on a Rock Box

Can inspiration be infinite? Yes, if you know where to look.

Above: Tiffany & Co. Singing Bird on a Clock and Bird on a Rock brooch, 800-843-3269.

How does a yellow cockatoo flitting near an open window in Guadeloupe end up a jewelry icon? That bird knew what it was doing when it perched outside the home of Jean Schlumberger, one of Tiffany & Co’s best-known jewelry designers. Nature was always the apple of Schlumberger’s eye, and in his vision that bird would become a diamond-studded feathered friend nestled in lapis lazuli and pinned on Bunny Mellon’s beret (he would go on to make for her a full, whimsical bestiary. Theirs was a partnership of hundreds of jelly fish, camels, and seahorses, plus the beloved fruit and flowers inspired by Mellon’s own garden).

That was 1965. How to keep reaching new heights? A partnership between Tiffany & Co. and the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) in Paris offers inspiration: An archive of thousands of Schlumberger’s sketches, now being restored and digitized, reveal a mind constantly at work and a vision without boundaries. And so it continues, in spessartine and chrysoprase, in rubellites and mother-of-pearl, and turquoise. The next reincarnation of the bird, though, might be one of the most exciting horological spectacles of the year: a glimmering, singing automata atop a burst of sun, a singing gold and titanium bird on a clock. Though, we must note, it does not play music: instead, it tweets. Look, and listen.

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Editor-in-Chief Stellene Volandes is a jewelry expert, and the author of Jeweler: Masters and Mavericks of Modern Design (Rizzoli).

#6906. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6905. A Kitchen in Bloom: How Tiffany Howell and Miele Seductively Reimagine the Heart of the Home

Eschewing conventional design for an immersive, romantic kitchen, this wildly glamorous space proves that high-tech convenience can foster intimacy, slowness, and the art of living well.

When Tiffany Howell of Night Palm Interiors designs a home, her extraordinary vision transcends conventional norms, veering into an ethereal realm—an approach she calls “the poetry of place.” In Howell’s mind film, fashion, and music are as essential as decorative art, and, when designing a home, she uses all these inspirations to create theatrical spaces that ignite all the senses— layering texture, sound, scent, light, and form to evoke deep emotion. When executed with this level of intention, a room becomes an environment you don't merely look at but fundamentally feel.

When Miele asked Howell to create a dream kitchen for an installation at Art Basel, she seized the opportunity to reimagine what a kitchen can be. Set against the vibrant creative energy of Miami's premier art fair, the commission gave her a blank canvas to challenge utilitarian conventions and treat the culinary space as an ambitious work of living art. “I wanted an elevated sexiness, a beautiful, sensory experience,” she says. “I created the space with characters in mind that I’d invite to a dinner party in this kitchen.” After all, the kitchen is a home’s heartbeat, an interactive space with multiple purposes.

“I do think the kitchen is really one of the most tender rooms in the house,” Howell says. She leaned into that romance by installing black cabinetry with blossoming branches and birds in iridescent greens and pinks. The cabinets are an homage to her love of 1920s chinoiserie furniture and fashion, seen through a very glamourous, Los Angeles lens. That California focus also meant bringing the outdoors in. The grand conservatory space harnesses diffused, natural light with the promise of the scent of intoxicating jasmine wafting into the room. Howell’s ideal playlist sets the tone with jazz greats, and ambient synth-pop.

“I wanted to create a kitchen that’s a completely sensory experience,” Howell explains. “The sound of a crackling warm fireplace, the aroma of a meal cooked for loved ones, or of making coffee in the morning.” For Howell, these aren't incidental details; they are the sensory architecture that turns raw space into lived emotion. In Howell’s view, this kitchen is “also a place where you can take a nap, read a book, or write in your journal. Every moment in this room can become a ritualistic experience of living well.”

When it came to the practical needs of a kitchen, Howell used Miele as a muse, an invitation to eschew the pale minimalism so common in luxury kitchens. “The appliances are so elegant, they kind of act as the jewelry of the room,” she says. “And their functionality and ease of operation give you back time to live better, relax with a drink, hang out with family and friends, and create the stories that make up a life.”

Consider the Miele Built-In Coffee System and wine cooler in matching dark finishes which are nearly camouflaged so the technology reads more as furniture than appliance. That tension between heirloom design and high-tech styling becomes a unified part of a lived-in, well collected home. Accent pieces including a vintage Fortuny pendant lamp over the kitchen island, and upholstered chairs in the conservatory with detailing reminiscent of antique lingerie, create a moody environment well suited to Miele's black-finished lines that resemble rich obsidian.

The 36” Full Surface Induction Cooktop features a frameless, flush design that disappears into a dark-veined countertop. Transforming the entire glass surface into one expansive cooking area, it fits up to six pots or pans at once and with Intelligent Pan Recognition and SmartSelect White controls seamlessly transfer power settings wherever pans are moved. On the same wall is the 30” Handleless Combi-Steam Oven XXL, with DualSteam technology that emanates ASMR sounds of steam cooking, and a black glass façade that resembles a framed panel or window. From the cool, flush glide of the induction cooktop to the whisper-quiet hum of precision steam, the technology engages the senses rather than interrupting them, turning modern convenience into an intuitive, tactile ritual. Rather than breaking up the wall with a stack of conspicuous appliances, these seamless integrations keep the architecture artful. And for added convenience, Miele’s elegant touch controls are enhanced with WIFI connectivity for an easier user experience.

Howell’s concealment of technology is exhibited in her use of the 42” French Door Refrigerator with its elongated custom handles and a dark lacquered paneled front that echoes the floral chinoiserie motifs—an unconventional solution that turns a mundane appliance into a piece of decorative art. And once the refrigerator doors are open, the Brilliant Light Pro illumination makes it easy to survey the food inside the large capacity interior.

“How do you nurture yourself and others in the kitchen?” Howell asks. Her answer: a design that creates a restorative space evoking intimacy and slowness, where technology can encourage a lightness and give us back more time. By composing every sight, surface, scent, and sound into a single harmonious narrative, Howell proves that a kitchen shouldn't just be an aesthetic statement—it should be something you feel in your bones. “What excites me is building a new world with more possibilities,” Howell says. “I don’t think I could design any other way.”

#6902. The One Kitchen Feature Designers Always Splurge on—and Why It’s Worth It

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: Meghan Jay selected a brass chandelier with ribbed glass orbs to modernize this traditional kitchen.

The kitchen is the hardest-working room in your house, but it’s also one of the most expensive spaces to renovate. Depending on the size and scope of your redo, reimagining this space can cost up to six figures, so it’s important to be wise about allocating your budget. While some people may overspend on high-end appliances or really luxurious countertops, you should never overlook the room’s lighting. Turns out, that’s where designers always splurge in the kitchen.

“Lighting has such a powerful ability to change how a room feels,” explains Meghan Jay, an interior designer in Winnetka, Illinois. “You can have the most beautiful cabinetry, stone, and hardware, but if the lighting isn't right, the space can feel flat or even cold.”

Meghan Meehan of Hudson East Design in New Jersey agrees and says the right fixtures can provide height, dimensionality, and lots of mood to the space. “Don't think of lighting as the finishing touch,” she adds. “Think of it as one of the main design elements that makes the entire kitchen.”

Ahead, here’s why spending a little more on your kitchen lighting is worth it—and exactly how much you should budget for.

Every kitchen deserves to glow at full wattage, but it will cost you. After all, illuminating a kitchen isn’t as simple as adding a budget-friendly portable lamp and calling it a day: The best and brightest kitchens usually include several sources such as sconces, recessed ceiling fixtures, and under-cabinet lighting to illuminate meal-prepping zones.

“It needs to provide the right illumination for cooking and everyday tasks, but it also creates warmth and ambiance when you're entertaining or gathering with family,” Meehan explains. As a rule of thumb, she recommends homeowners allocate at least 8 to 12 percent of their kitchen renovation budget to lighting.

That said, anyone who wants to wow with some statement fixtures might wind up spending more. Jay, for example, suggests setting aside $3,000 to $7,000 for decorative wares such as pendant lights above an island.

Admittedly, spending thousands of dollars on lighting is bound to elicit some sticker shock, and some people may wonder if it’s worth it. However, Lauren Wodicka insists it’s worth the investment. “Unlike a sofa, rug, or an appliance, lighting doesn’t experience the same daily wear and tear,” the Northern Virginia designer reasons. “A beautiful, well-made fixture can last for generations and become the focal point of a room.”

Oftentimes, less expensive fixtures can read cheap. “You may notice overly shiny or artificial-looking brass finishes, visible seams, thin metal, or cheaper-looking glass,” Jay says. “Higher-quality fixtures often have more substantial materials, beautiful patinas, and thoughtful details that become more apparent the closer you are.”

If you want to get the best bang for your buck, devise a shopping strategy. Santa Monica-based designer Christine Markatos Lowe first selects functional lighting and then spends the rest of her budget on “decorative pieces with personality.” But make no mistake, a personable light fixture should still have staying power: Lowe often reaches for timeless silhouettes from retailers like Urban Electric and Ann-Morris Lighting and is a sucker for details like handblown glass or “beautifully finished metals.”

“The key to finding the right balance between statement lighting and a cohesive kitchen design is to make sure every element feels intentional,” Lowe explains. “It’s all about allowing each material or element to have its moment without overwhelming the space.”Wodicka—who calls Soane, Hudson Valley Lighting, and Visual Comfort her go-tos—says less is more. “Not every kitchen island needs two or three lighting pendants,” she explains. “Sometimes one beautiful fixture can make a stronger statement.”

Designers often say lighting is the jewelry of a room, so layering different materials, sizes, and finishes adds more visual intrigue. Wodicka wants kitchen lights to complement each other—and the fixtures in surrounding rooms—but they don’t have to be identical. “A light fixture doesn't necessarily have to match the cabinet hardware or plumbing fixtures,” Jay adds. “I often prefer mixing finishes to create a more collected, layered look.”

If you want to keep your fixtures cohesive—without identical finishes—stick with a particular style. For example, a sconce with pleated fabric shades will look out of place next to a midcentury-style pendant that looks like it belongs on the set of Mad Men.

For the perfect finishing touch, Meehan recommends putting all lights on dimmers, which is a simple detail that can make a huge difference. “This allows for functional lighting when cooking, but softer light to warm the space when you are entertaining,” she adds. And, if a small detail can make your investment work even harder, even better.

#6900. The “Scottish Country House” Look Is the Cozy Interior Trend Designers Say to Know This Season

You don't need a Highland cottage to nail this heritage-inspired look.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

You don't need a sprawling stone cottage in the Highlands, a sweeping countryside view, or a roaring fireplace to bring a little Scottish country-house charm into your home. In fact, the appeal of this cozy, heritage-inspired aesthetic is that it can work just as beautifully in a city apartment as it does in a centuries-old estate.

The secret is mixing those old-world details with pieces that feel fresh and livable, so your home doesn't end up looking like a period drama set. A tartan runner on the dining table, a cashmere throw draped over your favorite reading chair, or a few leather accents on the coffee table can bring just the right amount of countryside character to an otherwise modern space. Strathberry's new tartan homeware collection is another lovely entry point, giving the traditional pattern a contemporary spin that feels right at home in all kinds of interiors.

Whether you're fully embracing your countryside daydreams or just looking to make your space feel a little warmer and more layered this winter, there are plenty of ways to bring the look home. Read on for the Scottish country-house-inspired touches that make this aesthetic so charming—and how to work them into your own space.

If there's one pattern that deserves a starring role in a Scottish country-inspired home, it's tartan. And you don't have to limit it to a throw blanket or a few accent pillows. Designer Kaitlin Smith recommends “using plaid for a wallpaper or even as an accent pillow” to bring that distinctly Scottish feel into a space.

For a bolder take, try a tartan wallpaper in a powder room, entryway, or tucked-away reading nook. If you'd rather ease into the look, a single plaid pillow or upholstered accent piece can make just as much of a statement. With so many different tartan patterns and colorways to choose from, you can find one that brings a little heritage-inspired character to your home without making the whole room feel like a theme.

A Scottish country house should feel like it's been lived in for generations, not styled within an inch of its life. That's where woven baskets come in.

Noel Gatts, a designer and HGTV personality, recommends introducing “some slouchy woven baskets” throughout the home, whether at the entryway for shoes and umbrellas or in a living room or bedroom to hold extra blankets. Their slightly relaxed shape and natural texture add to that collected, lived-in feeling while giving everyday clutter somewhere to go.

Try a larger basket beside the front door, a smaller one tucked under a console or an oversized version next to your favorite reading chair. It's a practical detail that also brings in the natural materials that make this aesthetic feel so grounded. Plus, having a cozy throw within arm's reach is never a bad idea.

You can practically picture a pot of tea warming by the fire in a Scottish country house, and Gatts recommends leaning into that feeling with a thoughtful arrangement of crockery and candles.

“Feature a fireplace or dining table with tea pots, earthenware, and an installation of candles and/or candlesticks,” she suggests. This is a lovely opportunity to bring out those pieces you might otherwise save for special occasions, from vintage teapots and handmade ceramic bowls to mismatched candlesticks and well-loved serving dishes. Grouping them together creates a charming focal point without making the space feel overly formal.

Candlelight is especially important here, adding a soft, welcoming glow that feels worlds away from bright overhead lighting. And if you don't have a fireplace, a dining table, mantel or console can give you the same cozy effect.

A Scottish country house shouldn't feel too coordinated, so don't be afraid to bring a few different patterns into the mix. As designer Sarah Cole suggests, “try pairing a wool plaid with a botanical print, a small geometric pattern or a traditional rug.”

The combination adds visual interest and gives a room that layered, lived-in quality that makes country-house interiors so inviting. You can keep the look cohesive by pulling a few warm, autumnal colors throughout the patterns—like russets, burgundies, forest greens and ochres—while varying their scale so they don't compete with one another. It's a simple way to make the space feel more personal and collected over time.

The Scottish country-house look has a wonderful appreciation for pieces that feel like they've been around awhile. Rather than filling every surface with brand-new accessories, Gatts suggests bringing in “found objects with a sense of history” made from weathered wood, pottery, or patinated brass. These are the kinds of details that give a room its personality, whether it's an old wooden box on a coffee table, a vintage ceramic vessel or a brass object with a little wear and patina.

Don't worry about everything matching, either. A mix of materials and finishes makes the space feel more authentic and less like you ordered the entire room in one shopping trip. Gatts recommends using these pieces on a coffee table for “the perfect antique flair,” but a bookshelf, sideboard, or entryway console would work just as well.

Finally, a few well-chosen antique details can give a room that Highland lodge feeling without requiring a full redesign. Jill Wheeler of Birch Hill Interiors recommends adding “a touch of polished brass like antique candlesticks or wall sconces to give the space a refined look.” Try styling a pair of vintage brass candlesticks on a mantel, placing one on a coffee table, or swapping in brass wall sconces beside a favorite reading chair.

Wheeler also suggests incorporating antiques on a mantel, coffee table, or built-in, noting that “antiques can help bring the outside in and tell a story.” The mix of aged finishes and meaningful finds adds a sense of history and character that makes a Scottish country-inspired space feel genuinely lived in.

#6898. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6895. Sometimes, succeeding in high school takes longer than four years

Every summer, approximately 87% of American high school seniors graduate four years after they started ninth grade. That leaves about 500,000 students nationwide who don’t complete high school with their cohort each year. But why do these half a million students not graduate in four years?

#6892. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

#6884. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6882. Designers Reveal the Fastest Ways They've Upgraded a Kitchen Under $100

No renovation needed for these designer-approved swaps.

As you whip up a weeknight meal or prep for a dinner party, you might find yourself daydreaming—and envisioning the kitchen you wish you had. While your current cook space might be perfectly functional, it just doesn’t have the aesthetics you long for.

Alas, the kitchen is widely regarded as the most expensive room to renovate. As Houzz reports, the average cost to remodel a kitchen is currently $26,500. But if you’re happy with things like cabinets and appliances (or a renovation is far off in the future), and you want to focus on just the visuals, you can give the space a glow-up with as little as $100.

It can indeed be done, and ahead, designers are sharing their best budget-friendly upgrades.

Rugs are such thrift-able items. A good rug is basically a necessity in a kitchen, and it can easily be swapped with a new one that refreshes the visuals.

Brooke Mennen-Talsma, principal interior designer at Brooke MT Interiors, shares that in the above kitchen she designed, she added a thrifted runner that she discovered for just $40, adding a focal point to an arched pantry.

She says to not underestimate the power of secondhand, and a lot of her favorite pieces in her portfolio are thrifted items.

Switching in fresh hardware for your cabinets is something that can be done in an afternoon, and it can be gentle on the wallet, too. You can also choose to add backplates to your existing hardware, as Sherrell Neal of Sherrell Design Studio recommends. She calls it a “quick upgrade that can stay under $100,” depending on the size and number of cabinets.

While new lighting in a kitchen can quickly add up, you can add light in other, more economical ways.

For instance, as Kira Young, founder and principal designer at High Tide Design, says, you can simply go with a “cute countertop lamp with a pretty little shade.” She advises specifically purchasing a rechargeable lamp, with no messy cords and no need for an outlet. It can also be easily moved anywhere.

Homeowners are increasingly opting for collected, and even pleasantly cluttered, looks in their homes. But if you really want your kitchen visuals to look polished and pleasing, Heather Kirk, principal interior designer at Kirk Riley Design, suggests highlighting a “hero piece,” or a favorite item that shines and steals focus.

Think strong statement pieces that offer both beauty and function, “like a cheerful tea kettle on the stove or a dramatic footed bowl on the counter,” as Kirk recommends.

Emily Spanos, creative director of Emily June Designs, says that in one kitchen she designed, she painted only the lower cabinets a bright turquoise and kept the walls and upper cabinets a soft white. Paint is one of the cheapest ways to quickly upgrade a home, and it’s something that can effectively be used in the kitchen.

“Painting one element costs a fraction of painting everything, but still highlights what you want the eye to fall on,” Spanos says. “A gallon of paint and a weekend’s work keeps you under budget.”

In addition to going with lower cabinets, you could consider painting an island, pantry door, the trim, or the inside of a glass-front cabinet.

To allow your eye to have multiple things to admire in your kitchen, go with a “layered look,” as designers call it. Think: linens, decor, rugs, and artwork.

Spanos says that she has accomplished this look for under $100 in a few projects she’s worked on. In one art-filled home, she carried the creative feel into the kitchen by displaying art on a couple shelves above the sink area. In another kitchen, she laid down a black-and-white geometric rug for visual interest, which tied into the matte black hardware.

She says, “A few quality tea towels, a small rug or runner, and a piece of art can all be found affordably, especially if you shop vintage or, even better, your own home.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6881. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6880. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

#6878. What Watch Will Robert Pattinson Wear as James Bond? It's Complicated.

Timepieces are almost as much a part of 007’s wardrobe as his dinner jackets.

Perhaps you've heard? There's a new James Bond in town!

Robert Pattinson has been announced as the next 007, and while most of the speculation has understandably focused on how the former vampire/current Batman might approach Britain's foremost secret agent, watch aficionados are asking another question: What will he wear on his wrist?

Watches, after all, are almost as much a part of Bond's wardrobe as his dinner jackets, Aston Martins, and vodka martinis. Sean Connery famously wore a Rolex Submariner, Roger Moore experimented with Seiko's digital watches, and Omega has been 007's watch of choice since Pierce Brosnan strapped on a trusty Seamaster in GoldenEye in 1995. That brand remains, of course, a paid partner of the franchise.

Yesterday's reveal showed no watch on the newly anointed 007's wrist, and Pattinson's own taste in watches is rather different from the rugged, sporty timepieces traditionally associated with Bond.

In fact, he doesn't seem particularly keen on big watches at all.

"Whenever a watch gets too thick and it keeps your cuff up, I don't really understand the appeal," he told one interviewer as recently September. "A lot of guys really like that, but I don't think I can really pull that off. I need to only have a half reveal."

And when asked what he looks for in a watch, he was even more specific.

"I definitely like really thin watches. That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap."

Which is quite a contrast with the substantial divers we've become accustomed to seeing on Bond's wrist.

The fact is, until recently, Pattinson wasn't much of a watch enthusiast at all. That changed at this year's Oscars, when he wore a then-unreleased rose-gold Jaeger-LeCoultre Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Other actors apparently began asking him about it at the afterparty, and something clicked.

He subsequently became an ambassador for Jaeger-LeCoultre, although his relationship with the brand goes back much further. As a teenager, he borrowed his mother's Reverso to impress his schoolmates. More recently, he's revealed that Bruce Wayne will wear a Reverso in the next Batman film.

On screen, Pattinson's watch choices have been interesting. He wore a Chanel J12 in David Cronenberg's Cosmopolis, while Christopher Nolan's Tenet, in which he played a mysterious secret agent, featured a Bond-adjacent Omega De Ville.

Meanwhile, the announcement has prompted some entertaining activity on Instagram. Within hours of Pattinson being named Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre posted pictures of its new ambassador wearing the Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Omega, meanwhile, posted an image of its James Bond Seamaster. And today, Jaeger-LeCoultre has been back with more Pattinson pictures.

It's a little social-media tit-for-tat, although there's no reason the two relationships can't coexist. For now, at least, there's a straightforward distinction: Pattinson can wear whatever he likes. Bond has his own watch.

#6877. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6875. Before & After: Two Best Friends Turned a Stripped-Down Tudor Into a Colorful, Antique-Filled Dream Home

Designer Anne Hulcher Tollett understood her client’s signature aesthetic better than anyone.

Anne Hulcher Tollett can’t remember a life without her best friend, Van. They met in a playpen as toddlers and quickly became an integral part of each other's lives, with many childhood memories taking place in Van’s house growing up. Tollett has been with her best friend through every milestone, including as her maid of honor at her wedding to her husband, Sean, and when the couple put an offer on a suburbanized Tudor home in Richmond, Virginia.

The five-bedroom house had good bones and an intuitive flow, but it needed help getting some of its charm back. When Van and Sean bought it for their family of four (plus their dog, Jelly Bean, and a small flock of chickens), it was whitewashed and lacked architectural character. In short, it wasn’t up to par with Van’s eclectic style, according to Tollett, but as the principal designer of Hanover Avenue, she reassured her bestie that she could easily take it from suburban chic to warm, layered, and personality-filled without having to completely rework the interior.

“Van’s aesthetic is diametrically opposed to ‘simple’ or ‘builder-grade,’ and although this house had been meticulously renovated and maintained by the previous owners, some of the charm she loves had been removed,” says Tollett. The designer and her team focused on lighter renovations and cosmetic upgrades, walking her best friend through every decision, from wallpaper choices to reworking the staircase to highlight the amazing treasures Van has collected on her travels over the years.

Throughout the renovation, Tollett and Van leaned into their sisterly shorthand, resulting in a project with very few disruptions. “Our biggest challenge was creating unexpected quirky moments without taking down walls or undoing all of the thoughtful and well-built upgrades that came with the house,” Tollett explains. “We found places on tables, walls, and bookshelves to highlight everything Van and her family have collected over the years. It makes the house feel like theirs and not the product of some design firm.”

Walls were covered with grasscloth, patterns were layered on throw pillows and vintage rugs, and anything that wasn’t antique or pre-owned was chosen to add a touch of patina to the space, allowing Van’s collections to truly sing.

Once Tollett was finished with the renovation, the house told the story of their friendship and a life well lived. Because the designer knows Van like the back of her hand, she was able to create a retreat that feels like a true reflection of who she is and how her family lives without editing out any of Van’s eccentricities. “We understood her design sensibility and took something well-built but fairly plain and made it fun, pretty, and interesting at every turn, which is exactly how anyone would describe Van!” Tollett adds. Besides, who better to design your house than the person who knows you better than yourself?

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne Hulcher Tollett of Hanover AvenueLocation: Richmond, VirginiaThe Space: A 4,600-square-foot Tudor home with five bedrooms, four full bathrooms, and two half-baths

The barrel back and turquoise chairs were finds that Tollett sourced for Van’s previous home, proving that timeless profiles and accessible sizes can work in many different spaces throughout their lifetimes.

“A room like this with a vaulted ceiling and white walls creates a wonderful backdrop for bold pops of color and patterns because it can visually hold its own against furnishings with personality,” she says. “Van had the beautiful sideboard, and we loved pairing it with the tattooed table.”

Possibly one of the most remarkable makeovers in the entire house, Tollett coated the entryway in deep blue paint, topping it off with darling wallpaper on the ceiling for added dimension. “This is a great example of how a can of paint, a roll of wallpaper, and some strategically placed treasures can transform a room,” she says.

Large-scale grasscloth wallpaper instantly gave the once-all-white box character and rhythm, setting a serene backdrop for the rest of the unique finds in this dining room. The chandelier hanging over the table had long been on Tollett’s list to use in a project, and Van’s home was the perfect spot.

“The dining room is right near the front door, so it’s hung to look like it's being blown by the entrance breeze—a true classic with a twist!” the designer says.

Above the antique bar (whose natural patina plays so well with the wallpaper), are two framed photos of puppets, which Van found during one of her globe-trotting trips. She carried them around in her backpack, and Tollett loved the story behind them so much that she had them framed. On the opposite wall, behind the piano, is a portrait of Van’s grandmother in a vibrant green dress.

The kitchen was well-maintained when Tollett got her hands on it, but it didn’t feel all that fun, and it needed more than simple cosmetic refreshes like other areas of the home.

“We removed the big-box island lighting and reworked the storage, hood, and casework on the range wall,” Tollett explains. “We did the same on the wall opposite the range,” she adds, which was in fantastic shape, so they kept it.

To give the cook space more character, the designer installed a custom Delftware tile backsplash. The island was also in good shape, so Tollett upgraded the hardware, faucet, face frames, and lighting above it, opting for three Pinch pendant lights which bring an interesting, natural texture to the space.

“Open concept kitchens are tricky because they are seen from every angle, so we knew we needed to improve the storage to hide clutter,” she adds. Opposite the range wall, Tollett removed the open shelves, creating closed casework instead and topping everything off with pretty hardware and colors that play well with the adjacent dining and living rooms.

Tollett knew she was going to wrap this powder room in a beautiful butterfly-and-bird wallpaper, so she custom-matched a paint to fully drench the room in a whimsical emerald green hue. They even had their wonderful wallpaper installers cut out a few extra birds and butterflies to extend the design onto the ceiling, creating a more immersive experience. “We wanted the powder room to pack an equal punch to the bold, blue entryway it connects to,” she explains.

One of the mantras Tollett and her team at Hanover Avenue follow is “wonderfully weird,” which the hand sculpture extending through the wall to hold the soap perfectly captures. “The space feels not only more fun but also much more personalized to our clients!”

During the renovation, Van’s mother passed away, so Tollett transformed this almost-forgotten area into an homage to the matriarch. “We reused her beautiful plate rack and hung all her flawless collections of plates on the walls,” the designer says, officially turning this room, which overlooks the vegetable gardens, into the “plate room.”

They papered the walls in warm neutral grasscloth and hung relaxed Roman shades to softly filter the natural light, furnishing the room with many of the fabulous pieces Van already owned (and loved). “This is a wonderful example of ‘reimagining what you have,’ and finding ways to remember the ones we love in our own rooms,” Tollett adds. “Van’s mom was the coolest!”

“Decades of sleepovers in Van’s childhood bedroom allowed me to execute exactly what she was hoping to achieve,” Tollett explains. Though there are many layers of pattern in this sitting area, the designer managed to prevent it from feeling busy by keeping the same serene color palette for most of the textiles, minus a few pops of red for a warm balance.

“This room is a grown-up translation of Van’s entire design sensibility that began when she was young and was nurtured in her adulthood during her travels,” Tollett adds.

On the other side of the room is a calming wash of warm white paint and natural textures, giving Van a visual place of rest ideal for sleeping. Tollett calls it a “well-collected, well-traveled, layered space that seems to have evolved all on its own—a sanctuary for Van and Sean,” which makes the designer especially happy since she was the maid of honor at the couple’s wedding.

The primary bath really only needed some cosmetic upgrades, but they did a lot of heavy lifting. Airy Roman shades soften the space while Van’s mother’s bench and one of her existing rugs give it personality. By opting for two warmer paint colors and swapping out key fixtures, such as the lights, hardware, and faucets, Tollett created a serene, sophisticated escape that looks completely new, all with the same bones.

Tollett tapped into her childhood memories with her best friend when designing this room. “During slumber parties, Van and I fell asleep across from each other in twin beds, so it felt fitting to create another bedroom that fosters the same kind of childhood chatter,” she says, adding that stepping inside is like stepping into a time machine going back to 1983.

Along with the beds, much of the look was directly inspired by Van’s childhood bedroom, the round green pillow on the bed having been plucked directly from it. The pre-existing Tudor windows were perfect for the vintage vibe.

Tucked away at the far end of the home is a spot connected to the sunroom lounge. Dubbed the “dayroom”—an informal spot dedicated to relaxation and reading—Tollett wanted it to feel cozy and as collected as the rest of the home but still a bit private. They washed the walls in a soft gray-green hue and did the same to the custom day bed, finishing off the room with antique bookcases in a complementary gray tone. It’s easy for small rooms like these to feel overcrowded, but the pale color palette takes away some visual weight.

As founder and principal designer of Hanover Avenue, Anne Hulcher Tollett is intimately involved in every project the Richmond-based design firm takes on, from high-end residential to boutique commercial. Tollett finds a thrill in uncovering her clients’ design styles and endless joy in creating their dream homes that reflect who they are.

#6873. How a Connected Home Supports a Good Night’s Rest

How a wellness content creator uses Hubspace from The Home Depot to curate an environment for better sleep.

As a certified holistic health coach and wellness content creator, Carolina Salazar knows the importance of a peaceful wind-down routine before bed. She trusts Hubspace from The Home Depot that powers many of the smart devices in her home, to help ease her into a good night’s sleep.

Hubspace, The Home Depot’s exclusive smart home platform, powers more than 250 products across select private brands—like Hampton Bay, Husky, and Vissani—as well as national brand partners. It effortlessly connects everyday essentials, including smart security, indoor and outdoor lighting, appliances, switches, watering controls, and more.

Some of the Hubspace app’s most practical features help users personalize lighting and improve home security. Hubspace Connect, for example, lets you control compatible lighting from a single switch, while Vacation Mode is a security feature that turns lights and smart plugs on and off while you’re away. One of Salazar’s favorite features is Circadian Rhythm, which automatically transitions lights from cooler, whiter tones to warmer ones as the day goes on.

“I can set my lights to follow my circadian rhythm, so they shift automatically throughout the day,” says Salazar. “And when they start to turn warm in the evening, that’s my cue to slow it down.” That can mean cozying up with a book, brewing a cup of hot tea, or, in her case, lighting a candle at her bedside and journaling. When Salazar is finished putting her thoughts to paper, she uses the Hubspace app to adjust the color, temperature, and brightness of her lights, dimming them as she gets closer to sleep. This can even be programmed automatically in order to reduce screen time before bed.

“Colder rooms bring better sleep, so I also have Hubspace turn on my AC at night,” she says.

The Hubspace app is free to download and easily syncs with your home’s smart devices. You can enable voice control for screen-free commands—also a plus if you’re trying to reduce your pre-sleep screen time. And the app runs on both WiFi and Bluetooth—so even if the WiFi is down, your nighttime routine can still run seamlessly.

#6872. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6867. The AI Police Are Going After Black Writers

The white powers that be have always doubted our intellect. Now they have a new tool.

Picture a waifish enslaved teen—a girl named Phillis, after the slave ship that Middle Passaged her from present-day Senegal and Gambia—trembling before a room of incredulous, austere white men, the most esteemed thinkers and politicians of colonial Massachusetts. The reason: The gifted girl wrote a book manuscript, and the family that owned her journeyed all the way to London to find a publisher for it. That prospective British publisher, a bloke named Archibald Bell, promised to publish the manuscript if the Wheatleys could prove Phillis had written it herself—an attestation tantamount, of course, to whether an enslaved African soon-to-be American possessed the intellect requisite to producing exceptional creative work.

Present at the trial, which has been described by historian Henry Louis Gates Jr. as the nascent scene of African American letters, was Thomas Hutchinson, the governor of the Province of the Massachusetts Bay; Andrew Oliver, the province’s lieutenant governor; and Mr. John Hancock himself, the future first governor of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Per Gates, almost all the men forgathered to pass judgment on Phillis Wheatley were both Harvard graduates and slaveholders. No record exists of what transpired, but oh how brilliant Phillis must’ve been as she convinced those 18 eminent Bostonians that she’d indeed written the poems. The confirmation was printed in the front matter of Phillis’s poetry collection—the first book published by an African soon-to-be American—Poems on Various Subjects, Religious and Moral (1773):

We whose Names are under-written, do assure the World, that the Poems specified in the following Page, were . . . written by Phillis, a young Negro Girl, who was but a few Years since, brought an uncultivated Barbarian from Africa, and has ever since been, and now is, under the Disadvantage of serving as a Slave in a Family in this Town. She has been examined by some of the best Judges, and is thought qualified to write them.

As my great-grandmother Edith Larkin Jackson, a woman not two generations removed from chattel slavery, used to say, “There’s nothing new under the sun.” Mama Eddie was a schoolteacher, and I imagine she’d be astounded by artificial intelligence. However, she’d have no trouble whatsoever fathoming a world that called into question the creativity or intellect of her people.

Here we are in this brave new world of AI, an age hurtling headlong toward the peril of superintelligence. Here we are in the 21st century, a time when the ivory-tower panjandrums are engaged in a sinister pattern, if not conspiratorial scheme, of incriminating Black and brown writers with even the suggestion that their work is AI assisted.

For example: In March, Hachette Book Group rendered kaput the U. S. publication of Mia Ballard’s Shy Girl over claims of AI usage.

When Trinidadian writer Jamir Nazir’s story “The Serpent in the Grove” was named the regional winner of the Commonwealth Prize in May, Ethan Mollick, a Wharton Business School professor who serves as the codirector of Wharton Generative AI Labs, ran it through the AI-detection software Pangram, touted as the industry standard, and reported that the platform flagged it at 100 percent. Once Mollick shared his findings on social media, several others joined the chorus bemoaning Nazir’s work as a product of AI.

In July, academics studying AI accused H. M. Wolfe of having used a large language model to write her best seller Daggermouth (though its publisher Simon & Schuster has, thus far, stood by the book). Later that month, writer Jerry Falade had a $2 million book deal with Macmillan collapse after his agents withdrew the submission of his debut crime novel citing AI concerns.

In September, Haitian Canadian writer Thélyson Orélien’s novel C’était Ça ou Mourir (It Was Either That or Die) was proclaimed a standout of the French literary season, won France’s Prix du Roman FNAC, and was long-listed for the Goncourt, the country’s most prestigious literary prize. But then an anonymous X account (later revealed to be owned by a conservative columnist) posted that Pangram had identified all 34 chapters of the book as having been written with AI. That claim spurred accusations that Orélien plagiarized earlier work, leading the Goncourt to nix the book from its long list.

In October, The Atlantic accused writer Simbarashe Kundizeza of using AI to write “The Blue Fighter,” which won the Caine Prize, Africa’s most prestigious short-story accolade.

Germane: While Nazir is Trinidadian, Ballard, Wolfe, Falade, Orélien, and Kundizeza are all Black.

Although all those writers have denied claims of using AI to produce their work, I’m not here to dispute whether they did or to what degree they used it, nor the epistemological paradox of using AI to determine whether a writer used AI to write a story. Of equal importance is that, if we ain’t reached the point where all published writing is scanned for possible use of an LLM, what’s the logic informing which writers are investigated for AI use and which aren’t, as well as the attendant issue of which accused writers suffer the most severe fallout, including the scarlet letter of damaged credibility?

The plight of Black and brown writers in the age of AI must be judged in light of what’s being described as an era of postliteracy. Of the specter of AI sloptimism becoming so ubiquitous it overruns human output. Of the cataclysmic doom of superintelligence approximating the creative work of a human brain to such a degree that the difference will be negligible to the undiscerning masses.

The plight of Black and brown writers in the age of AI must be apprehended against the beef between the publishing industry writ large and AI. Against 10,000 authors having copublished an “empty” book in protest of their work being pirated to train its models. Against America’s Big Five publishers suing Meta for copyright infringement. Against the recent unsealed documents that reveal OpenAI executives knew they were training their AI on copyrighted material and, furthermore, that AI-authored books were apt to displace in the market ones authored by humans. “There will be a point where a bunch of artists express worry about what we’re doing here, and we’ll likely ignore their concerns and release anyway,” OpenAI policy director Jack Clark wrote in an internal memo back in 2020. The predicament of Black and brown writers should be weighed against the long and calculated history of portraying them as lesser intellects.

Casting doubt on the possibility of Black literary brilliance is in the spirit of a hubristic Saul Bellow asking, “Who is the Tolstoy of the Zulus?” Of 45/47 portraying beaucoup Black people as “not that bright.” Of attacking DEI to paint us as unworthy or incapable, less we are sanctioned by people in power. The same insidious forces—linked to right-wing Cambridge professor James Orr and conservative billionaire Peter Thiel—stoked the allegations that ended in Cambridge professor Jason Arday dying by suicide. (Arday has been posthumously cleared of plagiarism.)

In stark contrast, best-selling novelist and screenwriter Anthony Horowitz has admitted using AI for his work. Best-selling biographer Katie Prescott extolled AI as invaluable in writing her book on Mike Lynch, the late British tech entrepreneur and AI pioneer. And while she’s been called among other things a “grifter” and a “villain,” South African writer Coral Hart has published more than 370 books using artificial intelligence since 2025 and defended her use of LLMs despite criticism and threats. While she later clarified she used it only for research, Nobel laureate Olga Tokarczuk caused a ruckus revealing she queried AI for writing help. Other than Santiago Schnell, a white Latino, resigning as the provost of Dartmouth for using AI in his published articles and The New York Times cutting ties with Alex Preston for an AI-assisted book review, beyond vocal to tepid criticism, white writers aren’t suffering material consequences on par with those of their Black peers.

Poor Phillis Wheatley never published a second book. Despite advertising six times in the newspaper to find a publisher. Despite announcing she’d dedicate it to Benjamin Franklin. Despite General George Washington extolling her as “favored by the muses.” In fact, in 1784, Phillis died at 30 years young, destitute, having placed the last advertisement for her second manuscript just a few months prior.

Because Big Tech is big-time thriving and race is evermore an oppressive tool, critics are bound to keep casting the work of Black writers as AI assisted, in effect electing themselves nouveau versions of those privileged 18th-century Bostonians. Best believe their indictments are a tacit way to portray the work of Black people as unworthy of patronage, acclaim, awards—aka inferior. But be forewarned: If Big Tech succeeds at AI turning the creative work of human brains passé if not obsolete, it’s near foregone that most writers will lose their ability to earn a living. Indeed, the sad-ass upshot is that if AI prevails, all writers—race be damned—will be fated a Phillis.
