#6865. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6864. Yes, Katie and Victor Were Flirting on ‘The Traitors: New Blood’—and They Dished All About Their First Date

Nothing says romance like trying not to get murdered on reality TV.

The Traitors may be a game of deceit, but Victor Vollbrechthausen and Katie Fites’ flirtation? That was apparently very real.

As the first civilian-only season of The Traitors: New Blood came to a close, host Alan Cumming asked Katie, “Is there a possible date on the cards for you and Victor?”

She seemed tainted by the fact that Victor had banished her, jokingly saying, “No date for you,” until he kept the banter going and claimed that he “earned it” after finding out she was a Traitor. Now, the star-crossed Faithful and Traitor romance has seemingly made its way out of Alan’s Scottish castle.

During a joint postseason interview with Entertainment Weekly, Victor and Katie revealed they followed through on their plans and actually went on a first date.

“We have gone on that date. After incessant begging, let the kids back home know that persistence works,” Katie joked, before revealing that it was “great.”

“It was fun to get to know each other outside of her trying to murder me, me trying not to get murdered. It was nice to take a moment just for us,” Victor, who played as a Faithful while Katie was one of the season’s Traitors, told the outlet.

As for what their first date entailed? Well, there was certainly no discussion about their time on the show, since Victor banned any mention of it. And, honestly, who can blame him? After all, he was onto Katie’s strategy to potentially target him for murder.

While her crush on Victor helped her game, Katie admitted that she genuinely enjoyed hanging out with him and even grew close to him throughout the season. She also revealed that she didn’t immediately kill Victor off because she wasn’t aware of his suspicions toward her—though he had been side-eyeing her earlier on than she realized.

The pair remained coy about whether they’ll keep their showmance going offscreen. When asked if they had plans to go on a second date, Victor shared, “Let’s see. Listen, man, it’s gonna... I don’t know. Getting that first one was an uphill battle, but I think there could be.”

“I think there could be,” Katie added. “But we’ll see. Gonna have to keep persisting.”

#6858. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6857. A Roundtable of Men in Their Nineties Taught Me the Secrets of Longevity

Every single thing I learned is something you can start doing right now.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Alan Patricof, 92, is one of my idols. The legendary venture capitalist was an early investor in enterprises as varied as AOL, Apple, and New York magazine. More important to me, aside from his incredible career and philanthropic work, he is a man who believes in always moving forward into the future. He co-launched a fund called Primetime Partners when he was 85. At 88, he wrote his first book, No Red Lights: Reflections on Life, 50 Years in Venture Capital and Never Driving Alone. He was the oldest participant—and finisher—in the New York City Marathon at 88 and got remarried at 89 to the globally recognized art advisor Barbara Guggenheim.

So when Patricof—who has also become one of my friends—called me with a request to join in on a lunch he was hosting for 12 men in their 90s, I was intrigued. I have been reporting on longevity for several years now and lead a company called ROARforward focused on the subject. Patricof asked me to come and talk about what I had learned about longevity and the possibility of the 100-year life. I agreed, but I immediately thought of it differently—as a master class in wisdom and experience where I stood to learn at least as much from these men as they could learn from me.

When I saw the list of guests, it was a bit intimidating. It included a former CEO of a Fortune 100 company, a vice chairman emeritus at one of the largest banks on the planet, a major real estate developer, and a man who has his name on one of the most prestigious health systems in New York. And those are just the ones who agreed to be identified—some asked to remain anonymous.

You get the idea: This was not an everyday group of 90-somethings but rather a gathering of highly accomplished, successful men who continue to thrive in a variety of industries and disciplines. That only made me more interested, though I imagined it might take some work to translate their habits and the ways they were spending their time into things more accessible to everyday people.

Luckily, I would turn out to be wrong about that.

As the men assembled in a private room at Patroon, a popular Manhattan restaurant, I saw that many of them already knew each other, some from as far back as grade school. As Patricof went over ground rules, including no talk of politics (both parties were represented) and no talk of sex (though Viagra did find its way into the conversation), they moved around the room, shaking hands, asking about wives and kids, and explaining what was keeping them busy.

That was my first clue that the behaviors that helped these men stay robust into their tenth decade had little to do with wealth. Research has shown that cultivating deep, healthy relationships is one of the best things you can do to increase your chances of having a long, healthy life. These men were living examples of what that looks like, and it’s free. (Though not if you have your lunches at Patroon.)

These men took care of three major aspects of their lives: health, head, and heart. First, they were committed to a fitness regimen and healthy eating habits. Second, they were highly engaged and curious individuals who valued a strong sense of purpose. Third, each of them had deep relationships with loved ones, whether a spouse, family, or friends.

You may notice that these are all universal virtues. They’re also things that can give you a sense of fulfillment if you’re only in your 80s, or 70s, or even younger. They aren’t solely investments in the future. You do them now, and they benefit you now. Which is a good feeling and precisely what makes longevity worth it in the first place. As one of the men said, “If today is the last day of my life, I’ve had a helluva run.”

“At 90, I tried boxing at a gym near my home in Harlem,” said George Hirsch, 92, before adding, proudly, that he went a full round. Hirsch was the longtime chairman of the New York Roadrunners running club. While he is no longer out doing road races, he is on a treadmill four or five days a week in a walk-run regimen. He supplements that with rowing, the elliptical machine, weights, and, apparently, the occasional prizefight, in a 60-minute routine.

For a gathering men who were 90 or older, this was a remarkably healthy group. It’s true they all have had access to the best health care, but none had problems with issues that come for everyone, like cognitive decline, diabetes, and heart disease. That word routine seemed to be the key. Routine, regimen—take your pick. These men make a point of committing to physical activity rather than just dabbling in it. One told me, “I think the secret to my life physically is that I’ve worked with the same trainer three days a week for 29 years,” adding that “even when I travel, I take my iPad and work out with him wherever I am.”

In some cases, these routines would be the envy of any younger man! The men had all played some type of sports when they were younger and carried that behavior deep into their adult lives. Since movement and exercise are the top natural medicines for a long, healthy life, these guys were on it. Activities included walking, weight training, stationary bicycling, swimming, pickleball, tennis, and downhill skiing. Two of them have a regular tennis game, and one of the oldest in the group is still an avid golfer. Most of them go to a gym or have a personal trainer. While a few admitted that they were slower as they got older—a sign of remarkable maturity in a man, to admit that—they were all active.

They are also conscious of what they eat. They agreed that they consume less food than in the past and drink very little, if at all. As Hirsch said, “I never drank much, maybe a beer or glass of wine with a meal. But I don’t drink anymore—it just doesn’t mean that much to me.”

Their attention to eating habits stood out. One thing that especially interested them was GLP-1’s, because of their impact on diet but also other potential benefits. As I shared what I’ve learned about longevity, including the frontiers of research on the topic, they asked question after question about the emergence of GLP-1’s and other classes of drugs that may enhance individual longevity. That kind of constant, engaged questioning was a hallmark of the group.

Only one of the men at the lunch defined himself as “retired.” The others said they were, at most, “semiretired.” While they are not necessarily working full throttle in what was their main career, to a man they all have very busy days—and that busyness, I found, is driven by a voracious curiosity, which is one of the best ways to strengthen and maintain brain health.

Ken Langone is a great example. He came from a working-class family in Queens and is still in awe of his good fortune. He’s taken it to mean that, as long as he’s able, he should be doing everything he can not to squander what he has. “I won big, and I can’t tell you how grateful I am,” he said, adding, “We should be giving, not taking.”

He is in his office three days a week and works from home two days a week, engaged in a public board, three private boards, his family office, and other investments. His commitment to public health is incredibly important to him; he serves as chair emeritus of NYU Langone Health.

“At 91,” Langone says, “my biggest issue is boredom, sitting around with nothing to do.”

Jim Freund, also 91, was a high-powered attorney into his early 60s, did a stint as a mediator after that, and now has a robust life as a writer, photographer, and musician.

He’s written 20 fiction and nonfiction books and has produced 25 albums of music featuring him at the piano. For six years, he has circulated a monthly blog for more than 500 friends and colleagues that includes the details of various projects he’s working on—an essay or short fiction, a piece of music, a portfolio of photos.

One expression of this curiosity is that all the men want to keep current. They follow the news, are dedicated readers, and have a deep curiosity about emerging trends. They don’t just read print; they’re on their phones, Kindles, Audible—anywhere there’s action. And AI—they’re on ChatGPT like everyone else. Some even have digital assistants.

But being current doesn’t just mean trendspotting. It can also mean having hobbies and projects that force you to engage with the world as it is. All of these men had ambitious new endeavors in the works.

Hirsch now has a bimonthly podcast with the running legend Amby Burfoot called “Running: State of the Sport.” Dan Rose, 97, former president and chairman of Rose Associates, the company that built Pentagon City and One Financial Center in Boston, is writing a new book. Patricof and his wife are taking Yiddish lessons. At least one man reported that he was finally learning the piano.

For all of them, it is important that their activities, whatever they may be, connect to deeper meaning and purpose.

Larry Leeds, 97, the former CEO and chairman of the apparel company Manhattan Industries, now works at Buckingham Capital Management five days a week buying and selling securities. It allows him to keep up with trends and investments. But it’s far from the most important thing to which he’s dedicated his energy.

Leeds hopes the leading line in his obituary is the $27 million he raised to launch the Cary Leeds Center for Tennis and Learning in the South Bronx in 2015. Dedicated to athletic instruction and academic enrichment for underprivileged youth—health and head—the center is also an expression of the most important thing in Leeds’s life: his family. It is named after his son, who competed in six US Opens and five Wimbledon championships before dying at the age of 45.

Sitting there in Patroon, I heard stories of how these men had committed to keeping themselves healthy and how their curiosity led them to continue to engage with the world at a point in their lives when they could just as easily have settled into insular routine. But it was the lunch itself that testified to their commitment to their hearts. Ninety years in, they were still making a point of getting together, speaking, laughing, and growing closer to other people.

Upon arriving, as we sat and introduced ourselves, Patricof explained that he wanted to do this lunch not only to learn from one another at this stage of life but also to simply enjoy socializing with peers—like many women do on a regular basis. Women live five to six more years than men; maybe it is their commitment to deep relationships, he suggested, that is one of the key factors of their greater longevity.

Women came up in another way too: Everyone attributed their health and longevity to having a loving partner.

The majority of the men have had incredibly long marriages. Nine of the 12 had been married for more than 60 years. Two had been married for 70 years. Then there was Freund, who joked that he, too, has been married for 60 years—just not all to the same woman. (He is married to his second wife, and they are at 41 years and counting.)

They all also have children and grandchildren, whom they are in touch with more than a few times a week—something any of us who are parents can do.

Rose has a unique ritual. “We have a family video conference every Sunday night with family from all parts of the country. It’s life enhancing and a time to connect and give everyone a chance to share accomplishments and disappointments with each other,” he explained.

At this stage of their lives, many have lost longtime friends, but they all strive to make new friends, some young, some old. In fact, there’s already a plan in place to replicate the lunch so that a bunch of other 90-somethings can be invited into the conversation. Here’s the thing I noticed about this: What got these guys excited about another lunch wasn’t simply that they enjoyed each other’s company or having a laugh with peers about Viagra. They were also inspired by one other. That must count for something.

I walked away inspired, too, and in awe of how these men live. They have to walk a line that most of us don’t yet, and maybe never will. Part of being alive—especially for this group—is thinking about the future, trying to figure out what’s next, planning another pursuit, but now they all admit that their horizon of looking out, say, 25 years is no longer a reality.

In the face of that tension, they’ve found a way to be optimistic about their future while at the same time savoring the present. None complained about getting older. None talked about their aches and pains. Yes, they faced growing limitations, but they all seemed to take them in stride.

“I do my best to love my life and try to have the same intensity and diligence that I had in my younger years,” said Leeds.

Langone said, “When I can no longer take care of myself and I’m totally dependent on someone else, then that’s the time to go,” adding that “God has his plan.”

And Patricof has his. He’s already publicly stated that he plans to live to 114, which would be 22 more years. I pointed out that the world’s oldest verified living person, Ethel Caterham in the UK, just turned 117, and I challenged Alan to break that record. He gladly told me that he accepts the challenge, and I don’t doubt he’ll make a real run at it. I have to admit I have an ulterior motive: I look forward to my lunch with centenarians. And supercentenarians.

#6854. The Latest PlayStation Is 6 Years Old and Twice as Expensive as When It Debuted, What’s That About?

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Thanks to AI and the RAMpocalypse, it’ll cost you to play the new ‘GTA VI’.

Grand Theft Auto VI is here, and it’s going to shock people heading into the holidays. No, we’re not moralizing about whatever violence, sex, or illegal activity is in the game. We’re talking about sticker shock.

Do you have any idea how much a new entry-level PlayStation 5 costs now? It costs $600, yet it was only $400 when it debuted in 2020. And the entry-level Xbox? It’ll also run you $600, though you can get a model with less storage for $500. Also having launched in 2020, the lower-storage Xbox Series S used to retail for $300. Both those consoles are more than half a decade old, and they have increased in price by 50 percent.

So what does a consumer do? I’m imagining a guy—perhaps this is you, reader. The last time he bought a console was a PlayStation 3 in 2013 for the previous Grand Theft Auto, or even a PS4 in 2018 for Red Dead Redemption 2. He thought about getting a PS5 for EA Sports College Football in 2024 but ultimately passed.This guy doesn’t pay attention to video games—or he only pays attention to the odd AAA release—and he is expecting to pay $400 for the key to experiencing this generation’s only game that matters. He might not even know that the game itself is going for $80, a rarity that only Nintendo has tried, with Mario Kart World. When he walks up to the counter, this hypothetical man will be paying nearly $900 to be able to play GTA VI. And the worst part? He’s not hypothetical. Starting Thursday, there are going to be thousands of real people in this very situation. Here’s why and what you can do—can you do anything?—about it.

GTA VI has taken over a decade to make (that’s longer than it took Rome to build the Colosseum), and estimates put the budget somewhere north of $1 billion. Rockstar has pushed back, though, with head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe telling Bloomberg that the rumor of the budget surpassing the $1.5 billion budget of the Burj Khalifa is just that—a rumor. Still, it’s not a stretch to call this the biggest media release of all time.

It’s also the biggest test of gaming’s resiliency to price hikes—price hikes that (like everything else, it seems) tie back to AI.

“When [EA Sports College Football] came back a couple of years ago, there was an influx of people who hadn’t bought a console yet,” said Mat Piscatella, a video game industry analyst and advisor at Circana. “Those folks at the time mostly chose either the Xbox Series S or the PlayStation 5 Digital, so the least expensive options to get in.”

The main cause is that the multibillion-dollar gaming industry is in a crisis, a RAM crisis. The RAMageddon, or RAMpocalypse, is a global supply shortage of memory chips that is primarily caused by the post-2024 boom in generative AI. Cleanview, a market-intelligence company, reported that the total number of data centers rose from 361 in 2016 to 1,278 in 2026, with more than five hundred more being built. Data centers are giant warehouses full of computer systems, complex computer systems requiring unprecedented amounts of RAM.

RAM stands for random-access memory. Memory, in this instance, doesn’t mean storage space. RAM is your computer’s “short-term memory,” essentially how many different things your computer’s brain can process at once. The more RAM your computer has, the more it can handle simultaneously. And according to JP Morgan Global Research, prices have risen more than 400 percent in the past two years.

That gets us back to GTA VI and the world of video game consoles. Modern games, with their massive open worlds, highly detailed graphics, and complex physics simulations, require a ton of RAM. As computer parts become scarcer and more expensive, the price of a console goes up. Thus, consoles that—besides the higher-tier PlayStation 5 Pro—have not had meaningful updates in half a decade are now hundreds of dollars more expensive than they were at launch.

Because contrary to what people know about the old gaming business models, the machines themselves still need to turn a profit. It didn’t use to be this way. In the PS2 era, consoles were sold at a loss, with games being the moneymaker. Things changed, audiences grew and moved elsewhere, and—somewhere between microtransactions and Xbox Game Pass—platform holders needed (or preferred) to make money on hardware again. So if a console is more expensive to manufacture, Sony and Microsoft aren’t going to eat that price. Consumers are seeing that increase directly, perhaps more directly and more acutely than we’ve ever seen any increase in technology prices.

Here’s our advice: Don’t wait. The AI push shows no signs of slowing down, and it’s likely that this RAM pipeline bottleneck won’t fix itself for a year or two. This holiday season is getting underway with a supply and demand squeeze similar to what we saw in 2020, when these consoles originally launched.

If you can swing it, you’ll probably want to buy a new console, but the PlayStation 5 Pro is already impossible to find brand-new. It might come down to shopping for renewed and refurbished consoles. These give you a very slight discount, but really they just give you a console that you can plug in and fire up GTA VI on. Welcome to the future of console gaming: refurbished six-year-old consoles.

#6853. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6851. I Am the Wildest Dream of Every Black and Brown Trans Woman Before Me

And it is my duty to honor their legacy while making a way for the next generation of trans girls, just like our luminaries did for me.

As an influencer, a lot of times I'm the only trans person—or the only trans person of color—in the room. It’s a lot of pressure, and there have been times where I have anxiety before going to certain spaces because I will question "Do I belong here?" But I’ve learned that that’s just the patriarchy and society trying to make us feel like we're not worthy.

That’s especially true in this current political and cultural climate. The trans community, especially the Black and brown trans community, gets so much hate—that’s why I’m very offended when people say being trans “is a choice.” Like, living with and navigating these stigmas and mistreatments isn't something that someone would willing choose.

It’s been particularly challenging, too, as a trans person in the public eye; there's been many instances where my safety has been jeopardized. When I get looked at, I'm thinking, They're looking at me either because I'm pretty or because I'm a viral trans person online. The differentiator is literally if I'm being chased home, which has happened multiple times. Since I speak out for trans women that can't speak for themselves, I'm also putting myself at risk. But at the same time, I think I can also make it safer for the next girl down the line.

It's so rewarding being that person others can look up to. I love reassuring other trans women that they can do certain things or just live their lives authentically, loud, and open. But sometimes I'm like, Oh my god, how nice and easy would it be for me to just be silent and be in the background? But I don't think I was born to be a background girl. I'm front and center.

And I think that the trans women before me would want me to be. The hard work that those women put for girls like me now, I have to show up for that. I think about trans women on the front lines of trans rights, like Marsha P. Johnson or Sylvia Rivera—they were front and center at Stonewall. They were the voices for many then, and there was so much more violence. The community was pretty much hidden at the time, too.

There's so much trans history lost, and Trans Day of Remembrance resurfaces those stories. A lot of times things get pushed under the rug because trans people are seen as not important to the media, or people in general, especially after all the propaganda that's been pushed out by certain administrations. But every year on this day, I learn about a different girl, especially outside of the U.S. I’ve learned so much about how some surgeries started because certain trans women needed gender-affirming care, and they paved the way for girls down the line.

Education is the number one thing. I want people to know that your understanding of trans identities is not what you think and that there's a lot more to learn. There are times when I get frustrated because I see the stigmas and the rhetoric online that's really just misinformation, rumors, and lies that people make up about trans people. A lot of trans women have died because that ignorance can lead to trans violence. It's really sad to see. But we made it back then—and it was hard then—so we'll make it now. We're a strong community.

The trans community, we show up for ours (for the most part). There's a couple spaces made for Black trans queer people, like the Black Trans Kitchen by Queen Jean—it’s really beautiful. And Gia Love created the annual Celebration of Black Trans Women Cookout. Trans women that make spaces like that really inspire me because they remind our community that we matter. And we as trans people need spaces where we can just be ourselves, and really show care to one another. Those spaces not only keep me humble, but also remind me of everything I've been through. It makes me feel so grateful and privileged to be alive today because a lot of girls don't make it.

With the work that I do and everything I create, I want other trans women to see me and know that they belong and that they can be in those spaces too. I want to see more, especially more trans women of color, in positions of power and in rooms where you rarely ever see them.

I hope other trans women see that they can be their most authentic self, and that they can feel confident regardless of what anybody says. If you believe you're an amazing, beautiful person, it will reflect on the outside. I want people to feel that way and do that inner work because it's important, especially for our survival as trans people.

#6847. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6845. Movado’s New Tempo-Matic Watch Is Inspired by an Iconic Piece from the ’70s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Proof once again that it’s hip to be square.

Movado was founded in 1881 in La Chaux-de-Fonds, Switzerland, which isn’t a particularly odd backstory for a heritage Swiss watch brand. But it has been American owned since 1983 and has a distinct quality tied deeply to its long history in the U.S. market—which puts it slightly outside the predictable purview of many watch collectors.

Right now, a growing band of horological nerds is finding rich pickings in the Movado back catalog and pushing up vintage prices considerably—especially for its mid-century chronographs and its legendary Museum Watch (which celebrates 80 years in 2027; more of that to come) in the process.

It’s not only the collectors who are digging into Movado’s past, however. Last month, the brand released the Heritage Tempo-Matic ($995–$1,095), a re-creation of a watch that merges sporty and retro design in a neat and slinky package. The Tempo-Matic, Movado’s first automatic movement, was launched in 1945 and went on to power a broad range of Movado’s wristwatches. But the rounded yet boxy “TV” shape of the new Heritage Tempo-Matic is unmistakably 1970s, when watch design took a giant leap forward into sexy new territory.

The 2026 version is available in three executions, each powered by the reliable Sellita SW200-1B movement and fitted with integrated steel bracelets that positively scream seventies. Two of them come in brushed steel with vertically brushed burgundy or blue dials. A third—which, we must admit, is our favorite—is gold-tone PVD with a warm gray dial. You’d be hard-pressed to find such an elegant, authentic, and affordable homage to the 1970s—a decade that’s currently a very hot motif in watch-collecting circles—anywhere else.

For further Movado inspiration, you might enjoy a glimpse into the brand’s rich watchmaking history at the Horological Society of New York, a fascinating nonprofit organization—effectively a guild created to support immigrant German watchmakers—that since 1866 has promoted the art and science of watchmaking in America. In the guild’s expansive watchmaking library, until July 2027, a dedicated display charts the Movado brand’s achievements in watchmaking, advertising, and design from as early as the ahead-of-its-time, art-deco-style Polyplan of 1912. Visits to the Movado exhibit are free, but we advise you to call ahead to check availability. And if you really get the bug while you’re there, you might like to sign up for membership, regular lectures, and even a series of watchmaking workshops where you get hands-on watchmaking experience under expert tutorship.

#6844. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

#6843. The MacBook Neo Is on Sale for the First Time Ever

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Grab this early Black Friday deal before it sells out.

I am not the first person to point out that MacBooks are expensive. I will not be the last. If you monitor this stuff like we do, you’ll have seen the irony that Apple released the budget-conscious MacBook Neo earlier this year—only for the global computing shortage to almost immediately raise the price from $599 to $699.

But the Neo is still cheap. You could buy it for full price and get your money’s worth—although scoring it on sale makes the whole thing even sweeter. Just in time for Black Friday, Amazon shaved $50 off of the buzzy MacBook. Chalk it up to a Christmas miracle.

Designed as Apple’s answer to the lower-budget laptops running Windows, the Neo’s target audience is technically students—hence the good branding and cool colors. That being said, if you need a personal computer for watching Netflix and doing your taxes but don’t want a full desktop, it’s a perfect choice.

The Neo runs on Apple’s A18 Pro chip—the same one that powers the iPhone 16 Pro and Pro Max. If the idea of buying a laptop that runs on a phone chip gives you pause, your stress is better channeled elsewhere. While this isn’t the right device for someone who is planning to run heavy-duty software, it can handle standard Internet browsing, video calls, and the like with ease. Just keep in mind that it has only 8 GB of RAM, so it’s best suited to basic workflows.

Of course, this computer has the added benefit of four jazzy color options: blush and citrus for the bold, indigo for those more subdued, and silver for that classic MacBook aesthetic. Wrapped in an aluminum body with a 13-inch Liquid Retina display, the Neo weighs in at just 2.7 pounds (same as the MacBook Air) and is 0.5 inches thick. (For reference, the iPhone 17 Pro Max is 0.35 inches thick.)

Despite our best efforts, we don’t quite know how Apple determines its deals for big sale events, so there’s no telling if this $50 discount is as good as it’s going to get for Black Friday. In all honesty, we were hoping Amazon would shave $100 off to get the Neo back to its pre-increase price. In our wildest dreams, we’re gunning for a deal that would make this the first-ever sub-$500 MacBook.

We’re not giving up on a deeper discount once Black Friday proper arrives, but for now the Neo is undoubtedly a smart buy for anyone who wants a laptop that can handle all of the basics. At $649, it’s still a fraction of the cost of the MacBook Air and Pro.

#6841. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6838. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6836. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6832. The Surprisingly Wholesome Life of a Professional BDSM Practitioner

I don’t fit the gigolo stereotype of washboard abs and silky hair. Plus, I’m married with a daughter. But when powerful women want to experience their most private fantasies, whether in person or online, they hire me.

Have you heard of BDSM? The acronym refers to the sexual acts of bondage and discipline, dominance and submission, and sadism and masochism. While those acts might sound strange to the uninitiated, they’re not uncommon. A 2020 study published in The Journal of Sex Research found that more than 40 percent of people have BDSM fantasies, while about 20 percent have engaged in BDSM. That’s a pretty big client base for people like the subject of this week’s edition of “The Secret Lives of Men.”

Sir Dax, 53, is a professional BDSM practitioner in central England. His clients, who include OnlyFans models and CEOs, pay him for everything from sexy centaur fantasies to submissive experiences in which they go down on him and cook him dinner. The sexual nature of his work doesn’t affect his private life, however. The happily married father, called a “teddy bear” by his daughter, has a normal, everyday life outside the role-play and sex toys. Here’s how he balances it all.

I never dreamed of becoming a professional dominant. I didn’t even know what kink was until I was far into adulthood. But now there are women from all walks of life, including high-profile women who could have any men they want, who pay me—or a version of me I call “Sir Dax.”

I do my sex work both online and in person. I take my video calls in my cabin behind the house I share with my wife and daughter. Before I start a call, I place a black backdrop to cover up my Star Wars and Avengers Lego sculptures. I keep my tools there, in a wheeled case, so I can easily bring them to my sessions. I have everything from vibrators to suction cups to floggers and butt plugs. In-person sessions happen at hotels and rental homes. Sometimes women even pay me to travel with them. I’ve been to America and the Netherlands. Next year I’m going to Iceland.

I’m not a gigolo stereotype. I don’t have washboard abs or silky hair. I’m a five-foot-seven, 53-year-old, married dad who is a bit overweight and balding with a big, bushy beard. My wife isn’t kinky, and we don’t have an open relationship. She’s very much monogamous. But she knows what sort of things I do with my female clients. They like me because I’m confident in myself, and they like that I’m married because they know I won’t catch feelings for them. It’s purely business. And honestly, as much as I enjoy it, if I wasn’t being paid for it, I wouldn’t be doing it.

I grew up very religious. I was taken to church every Sunday and had the Bible beaten into me. It took me a while to outgrow that.

I was kinky before I realized the word for it. In my 20s, I would ask dates to wear button-down blouses and tell them to undo a button at a time. By the end of the evening, her bra would usually be in my pocket. Quite often, her panties would be used as a scrunchie in her hair. Later, I would tie her to the bed.

I was working in IT at the time. I was having a conversation with a few of my male colleagues about sex, and I asked them a question that was bugging me: “Why do women always say daddy and sir in bed?” And they were like, “What are you talking about? No one does that.” It was the 1990s—this kind of talk was less common.

Eventually, someone clued me into BDSM. They told me I was a dom.

When I was in my mid-30s, I met my wife on an online dating site. I wasn’t looking for a kinky partner then, and I knew that my wife had bad experiences with an ex, so we never explored it.

Then, in the early 2010s, my whole immediate family died, and it basically broke my brain. Depression hit hard. Therapy helped me make it through the tough time. But I had changed. I’d gone from being quite an extrovert to being something of an introvert. I soon found my way into a BDSM chat room. I started conversations about kink, and I felt lighter. My wife even noticed that the cloud of my depression was lifting.

I told some of my female friends about the BDSM chats, and they said I should charge for them. I thought they didn’t really mean it; they were just being nice. But more people keep telling me to. When enough women tell you to do something, you start to think, Maybe I should listen.

I knew about dominatrices, so I looked into whether there was a market for men to do that with women. And there was. It was small, but it was there. So I had another conversation with my wife and asked, “Would this be okay? This goes beyond me talking to people online. This is me touching another woman’s naked body and putting things inside her.”

“If you’re helping them, then that makes sense,” she said.

I affirmed that she is my forever home and that I would never leave her for a client. The relationship between my clients and me would be of dominance and submission, and that’s it. No romance.

My clients now include directors, CEOs, health-care professionals, models, mothers. Many of the women who hire me are submissives. Some are in high-powered jobs and don’t want to make decisions in their off time. They want somebody to hand over control to, somebody who’s going to tell them what to do.

Most of my clients are in their late 20s to early 30s, but they range from 18 years old to 60-plus. Most are in relationships. I don’t judge them, but I do make sure to ask, because some of the things we could do would be noticeable to their partners—like writing “Property of Sir Dax” on their boobs.

The majority of my clients either have no experience with BDSM or have had bad experiences. They turn to me because they feel I’m trustworthy. Peacock has just released a first-look teaser, taken from the opening of the new series. Plot details are being kept under wraps for now, but the Jackal is back in action, and there should be plenty of globetrotting, elaborate schemes, and unfortunate encounters for anyone who gets in his way.

The Day of the Jackal season 2 arrives on Peacock in March 2027.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set to shop. After all, when Apple gadgets are this cheap, they tend to go like hotcakes, so don’t say we didn’t warn you when they inevitably sell out.

Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made sweeter when it’s on sale for Black Friday.

For October Prime Day, this brand-new watch surprisingly dropped to just $350. We would have been pleased with that same discount for Black Friday, so this $70 discount is all the more impressive.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This Black Friday discount brings the SE 3 back down to the record-low $199 we first saw during October Prime Day, making it by far the most affordable Apple Watch on the market.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, pick it up. It’s incredibly likely that the deals you see here are the best ones you’ll see for the rest of the year.

Back in the old days of Black Friday shopping, you had to fight other members of your community—your kids’ bus driver, the people that sat next to you in church, a friend’s personal trainer—to score a good TV on sale. The idea of dropping a power-draining, water-gulping ten-building campus on a place as small as Sutton is exactly why data centers have become the hot-button local issue all over the country. It’s good to be rich in this country. You may not have noticed.

This has resulted in some hilarious testimonial follies and at least one near hooley between the two senators from Texas. #6823. Jack O’Connell on McQueen, Ink, and the “Heavy Hitters” of Sinners

He may not be James Bond, but the actor is still dressed to kill.

Jack O’Connell may not be James Bond, but he’s still dressed to kill. At the 17th annual Governors Awards, hosted by the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences, the Sinners and 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple star rolled up in a mono-black suit sourced from McQueen’s Spring/Summer 2027 pre-collection: a tailored jacket with ultrawide lapels, black wool barathea trousers, and polished Jermyn brogues. If anyone at the awards exuded main-character energy, it was O’Connell.

“It’s a real lovely, beautiful chance to congregate and celebrate,” O’Connell tells me days later. “You come away from them feeling really inspired. There’s a lot of love in that room.”

He adds, “It is my mission now to binge Glenn Close [movies].” (The 79-year-old legend received an Academy Honorary Award for her many decades of contributions to the industry.)

But O’Connell’s all-black eveningwear, courtesy of McQueen, is award worthy on its own. No stranger to events like these, “sometimes you don’t wanna look uniform,” O’Connell says. The now grown-up James Cook from Skins opted for a double-breasted jacket with distinctly large lapels that he says were “almost a winged tip.” He felt it was something Alexander McQueen himself might have imagined. “Just a dapper bit of Lee McQueen—it’s nice, isn’t it? To push the boat out a little bit.”

He credits his stylist, Luke Day, for pointing him in the right direction. “We pick our moments. Sometimes we want to toe the line; sometimes we don’t. You don’t always feel like slapping a dicky bow tie on. It’s great. It mixes it up.”

When O’Connell isn’t mixing it up in front of flashing cameras, he likes to dress to his surroundings—as any man ought to. “If I’m in the countryside, it’s boots. If I’m in the city, it’s kicks. I really like a lot of Axel Arigatos on my feet. But Reebok was always the staple. I love a Reebok.”

The man could use a pair of Reeboks, because he’s had quite the year running ahead. In 2025, he was a most memorable menace in Ryan Coogler’s vampire scorcher Sinners. He kicked off 2026 in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple as Jimmy Crystal, the bleach-blond cult leader of butt-kicking devil worshippers in the zombie apocalypse. He will soon star in Ink (which hits select theaters December 11 before it lands on Netflix on January 8), Danny Boyle’s screen adaptation of the stage play about the British tabloid The Sun. If it bleeds, it leads, indeed.

All the while, his name got thrown into the mix as the next James Bond. Though O’Connell had public support from friends like Boyle, he downplayed how much he was actually in the running (as in an October interview with Entertainment Weekly). Now that Robert Pattinson is 007, he doesn’t have to answer those questions anymore. “He’s one of the best in the biz, isn’t he? I love his work,” O’Connell says of R-Patz. “He’s a great set of hands. Anything he turns to, he just kinda nails. Really interesting actor, makes very unobvious choices. I’ve been a fan of his for a long time.”

Without Bond looming over him, O’Connell is free to focus on what he’s actually cooking up. Lately, he’s been hard at work on two big blockbusters for 2027: Godzilla x Kong: Supernova and A Quiet Place Part III. “It’s been one of my favorites for sure,” O’Connell says, reflecting on the past year. “Tied for going to the Oscars and watching my friends win trophies, it is up there as one of the best experiences of my life. I hope we keep pushing.”

So what can audiences expect to see in Supernova? Besides monster wrestling, of course. “We follow Kaitlyn [Dever]’s character up the ranks,” he explains. “She’s studying these monsters. Her brother’s part of a new organization. I can’t give too much away about it. And Trapper’s just being Trapper. You know how Trapper is.”

The project, with its green screens and CGI kaiju, is the biggest O’Connell has undertaken yet. But the large-scale endeavor brings him back to his childhood. “I remember kids running around the playground pretending to be Godzilla,” he says. “It’s omnipresent, isn’t it?”

He adds, “It’s not every job that I’m doing that type of thing. It was great to explore it to the maximum. You know who the main characters are. It’s not the people. It’s great supporting these pixelated legends.”

O’Connell can say even less about A Quiet Place Part III. “It’s in the aftermath of this horrific apocalyptic event,” he says. “It’s a tale of trauma, loss. It’s stuntier than any of us anticipated. But at the center of it is this very beautiful human story where we’re saying, What is the cost of all this fucking chaos and carnage, you know?”

But in some ways, O’Connell is still riding the high of Sinners. “People responded to it in such a way,” he says about the Oscar-winning thriller in retrospect. “It was great to see people unite at the cinema en masse. It’s great when one film can do that. I put it down to Ryan Coogler, our cast, and our entire team across the board. Everywhere you looked, it was packed with heavy hitters.”

Next time you catch O’Connell, bet he’s dressed in McQueen. “I really love what Alexander McQueen, as a person and as a creative, stood for,” he says. “We’re from similar backgrounds, which is rare for me in this world. It makes it meaningful. I celebrate his story, so any opportunity to work with them is a really heartfelt one.”

He swears you’ve seen nothing yet. “I don’t feel like I’ve done my best work yet. Far from it.”

Clothing by McQueen Spring Summer 2027 Pre-collection, wearing a sharp lapel tailored jacket and trousers in black wool barathea, a harness shirt in black cotton poplin with safety pin embroidery, and Jermyn brogues in black polished leather.

#6821. Chocolate Strawberry Nails Are the Moody-Sweet Manicure Trend to Try Next

Think mocha, pinky brown, and deep berry tones with a just-dipped shine.

We’ve had espresso nails, almond milk nails, and now we’re savoring chocolate strawberry nails. So what exactly are they? Think of anything in the family of mocha shades, pinky browns, and deep berry tones. It’s the color you get when a ripe strawberry takes a dip in melted chocolate.

The look works because it balances warm and cool. The rich brown base keeps things grounded and neutral, while the berry undertones add a soft flush of color that feels fresh rather than flat. It’s a shade that flatters nearly every skin tone, and it’s subtle enough for the office yet moody enough for a night out. For an extra touch, try a glossy top coat to mimic that just-dipped chocolate shine, or add a thin chrome or sheer pink accent nail for a little dimension.

Below, we’ve rounded up some fun designs to get you inspired for your next chocolate strawberry manicure.

Like a strawberry dipped fully in chocolate, this dark shade hides a subtle red glow that peeks through almost like a cat-eye effect.

This dark berry shade has a high-gloss, mirror-like shine, just like a strawberry fresh from a dip in silky milk chocolate.

From dreamy cotton-candy pink to a deep, jammy berry, these rosy shades capture every stage of a juicy strawberry, from first blush to fully ripe.

This nail set looks like creamy milk chocolate glazed under a frosty chrome finish.

The silver studs on this rich berry shade mimic a strawberry’s tiny seeds, with a glam twist.

These short nails are neatly filed and glossed to perfection. The rounded shape keeps them practical for everyday wear, while the mirror-bright finish gives them a polished, just-left-the-salon look.

This mani pairs a lustrous chocolate base with a sweet pink French tip. The soft pink line adds a playful pop against the deep brown, giving the classic French a fresh, dessert-inspired twist.

Soft pink polka dots float across a deep burgundy base, a playful nod to retro charm with a ripe, berry-rich finish.

This manicure features DND’s Strawberry Wine, a moody fall shade that’s hard to beat.

Another DND color, Fudgsicle, is a rich chocolate brown with a hint of red, the ideal base for chocolate strawberry nails.

Like real strawberries, the black dots melt into the base so completely they’re nearly invisible, until a closer look reveals the quiet detail hiding in plain sight.
