#6818. Lafayette 148’s Emily Smith on 30 Years in Fashion and Finding Her Uniform

In ELLE’s Office Hours series, we ask people in powerful positions about their first jobs, worst jobs, and everything in between. This month, we sat down with Lafayette 148 creative director Emily Smith as the New York-based fashion house celebrates its 30th anniversary. Smith has spent more than two decades at the helm of the brand, working her way up from a part-time freelancer to creative lead. But her fashion ambitions began long before she arrived in New York. Growing up in Atlanta, she ran her own neighborhood art camp before discovering designers like Donna Karan and realizing fashion could be a career. Now, Smith is celebrating the women who have shaped Lafayette 148 through its “Uniform” campaign, which highlights accomplished New Yorkers and their individual approaches to personal style. Below, Smith reflects on her unconventional start, why she’s stayed at the company for over 20 years, and the career advice she wishes she’d followed sooner.

I ran a camp in my neighborhood because, at the time, I thought I wanted to be an art teacher. It was basically babysitting all the neighborhood kids, but I created a flyer, got paid for it, and did the whole thing. I started in second grade, and we did it every year for about eight years. I even started hiring people so we could have two classes going at once. We had swimming lessons. The local news even came and did a segment on it. I loved decorating my classroom and setting everything up. My parents were so nice to give me the garage and basement to do it all. Not necessarily an official job, but my first money-making moment.

I was a bagger at Kroger. It just was not for me.

I was always creative growing up. At a certain point, I discovered Donna Karan. It’s kind of crazy when you see somebody doing something and suddenly realize, Oh, that’s a career. I can do that. I became obsessed with everything in the ’90s, all the New York fashion at the time. That was when I decided I wanted to pursue it. When college applications came around, my mom really encouraged me to go to art school. I started in fashion, and that became my focus. Growing up in Atlanta, I wasn’t necessarily surrounded by a big fashion community. But my dad’s office was across the street from the art museum at one point, and I remember going there and having that exposure. My mom was always crafty and creative, too. She really encouraged us to be who we were.

There’s a language creative people have that’s a little different from the way business people communicate. When I went to art school, I felt like I’d found my people. It wasn’t just fashion people. It was photographers, fine artists, and all these different kinds of creatives. Being around people who thought about things differently was really inspiring. Even today, I thrive around people who have creative insight. There’s always a little more depth to the way they see things.

I interned at Bill Blass for about six months. I moved to New York in July 2001, and we had our fashion show on September 10. I remember Marc Jacobs had his show that day, too, and everybody was celebrating. People were saying, “Just come a little late to work tomorrow.” And then, obviously, September 11 happened. A week later, the entire team was let go, from the top down. What I learned from that experience was the fragility of everything. Don’t take anything for granted. Anything can come and go in a moment. But I also worked with Lars Nilsson and Hervé Pierre Braillard, who were just the kindest people. This industry has a little bit of everything, but I loved that they led with kindness, even when they were under so much pressure. As an intern, I was literally carrying ironing boards down the street and doing a sketch here or there. It was very much about doing whatever needed to be done.

After my internship, I had a brief stint of freelancing. Those were some long months. I remember coming home from one job and my roommates asking how my day was. I said, “Well, I was putting pigs on socks.” I knew I wouldn’t be going back. Freelancing is hard. It’s a grind, but you meet a lot of great people. I actually made some really good contacts during that time. I eventually met Deirdre [Quinn], Lafayette 148’s founder. She met with me on a Saturday, which I thought was incredible. I was really just looking for advice. I wasn’t looking for a job, and I didn’t even know much about the brand. It was only five years old. She was so kind, full of knowledge, and passionate. After I spent a year freelancing and meeting people who seemed so over the industry, her enthusiasm reminded me why I wanted to do this in the first place. She offered me a part-time freelance position, and the rest is history.

In the beginning, security was really important to me. The idea of losing a job and having to pay rent was scary, so that was definitely part of it initially. But really, it was the growth. Within a couple of years, they were sending me to the factory. It was a small, tight-knit company, so I was able to wear a lot of different hats. I had a mentor named Barbara Gast who was traveling back and forth from Italy, and I was learning so much from her. I’d compare notes with friends in the industry and realize how much hands-on experience I was getting. I understood the value of that and really loved being so close to the product. It never got boring. And whenever things did get slightly boring, I’d ask, “Okay, what’s next?” And there was always something. It’s such an entrepreneurial place that you can almost create your next opportunity. That’s what made it so exciting.

I think if you asked Deirdre, she’d have a different answer because she has her own experience of building the company. For me, it’s about having started at a company that was really a factory and being part of the journey of building its identity and what it stands for. I was the kid running up and down the factory floors collecting samples. That hustle and bustle was so innate to who the brand was. To me, it was really exciting to build more of a brand story around that. Thirty years later, we’re still celebrating those roots and making sure we haven’t strayed too far from them, while continuing to evolve. Being part of that journey has been really exciting.

The constant has always been this idea of an accomplished woman. She’s always been at the forefront of who we’re designing for. At the time, it was the career woman. But now, what does that even mean anymore? What does that look like? There are so many different versions of that woman and so many different ways people dress. At the heart of it, she’s a woman who knows what she wants. She’s busy and has a really full life, whether she’s starting her career or ending it. They can all find something in the clothes.

For us, it was about choosing people who inspire us. Number one, they had to be New York women. That was the base. We wanted women who make us want to be better ourselves. From someone like Deborah Berke, the architect, to women who have built incredible careers in other fields, it’s important to shine a light on them. I think back to when I was a kid and how much you need that kind of inspiration. Even at a certain stage in your career, when you’re wondering what the point is, it’s nice to have people who inspire you. These are women who have really built something for themselves, and I think that’s worth celebrating.

Sophie de Rakoff said something during the project that I really liked, and I’m going to steal it: “Dressing with intention.” When you know yourself, you know what works on you. It’s no longer about dressing to impress. There’s a confidence in knowing what works for you and making it work. We’re all so busy. Who has time to overthink getting dressed? You know what works, and you build a system around that. You’re able to create a wardrobe that really works for you. Everybody has a different definition of a uniform, and that was the whole point of the project.

When I first started in the industry, I was a total hippie. I was making my own clothes, and they were terrible. Absolutely falling apart. It was what it was. I think part of the change comes from not having as much time as you did when you were younger. But it’s also about simplicity and comfort. I know that if I’m going to be on the floor pinning something but also have to give a presentation, there are certain pieces that work for both. I can be comfortable and still feel appropriately dressed. I’ve found a handful of things that work for me, and I don’t have to think too much about them anymore. A crisp, oversize white cotton button-down is half my closet. I also love a soft skirt and a big chunky sweater. Those are the pieces I always go back to and interchange depending on the day. I even wear sweaters in the summer. It’s cold in the Navy Yard!

Our pants fit incredibly well. The moment somebody gets into our pants, they’ll love us forever. Then it’s the jacket and the white shirt. Those three things are our gateway pieces, and everything else follows. You need a good blazer and a good leather piece, too. Once you find those foundational pieces that really work for you, everything else becomes easier.

I really want to see more women in our clothes. It’s not just about growing the business. It makes me happy when I see people put on our clothes and feel really good. We work so hard to make sure things fit the way a woman wants them to fit. I just want more people to enjoy that.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6815. The 27 Best Video Games of 2026 (So Far)

With Grand Theft Auto VI finally in gamers’ hands, 2026 might go down as one of the greatest years in the medium’s history.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Will 2026 go down as one of the best years in video games? There just simply aren’t enough hours in the calendar year to play through all of the amazing games that came out before Thursday, November 19, and now we have to add on hundreds of hours of Grand Theft Auto VI.

Of course, the industry’s economic woes cannot be overstated. It’s a dark time when even prolific studios downsize, slash budgets, and lay off talented developers with invaluable institutional knowledge. Even the GTA VI designers—whose game is already heralded as a masterpiece—are still fighting for better working conditions. So it’s hard to actually call 2026 a great year in gaming when it’s also been a year of heartbreak and uncertainty for the future.

But the games are still great. Even just a few hours into GTA VI, it’s obvious that it’s a massive achievement for Rockstar Games that’s well worth the wait. And just this past month, Nintendo dropped a remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time that features beautifully upgraded graphics from the original 1998 classic. Elsewhere, Resident Evil Requiem nailed the franchise’s decades-long identity split with a game that’s half third-person action and half first-person survival horror. Plus, action games like 007 First Light, Control Resonant, and Onimusha: Way of the Sword let loose the power fantasies we’ve only dreamed of.

But it’s not all blood and guts and guns and swords. Earlier this year, the coming-of-age Mixtape caused a ruckus among gaming circles, though anyone who’s played it and loved it will tell you just how much it’s a love letter to bygone summer days. Oh, and a spin-off Pokémon title about building adorable towns and protecting the environment is currently the highest-rated game in the franchise’s thirty-year history.

So, unless you’re already dedicated to spending all of Thanksgiving and Christmas in GTA VI’s Vice City, some of the best games Esquire’s editors have played through in 2026 so far.

GTA VI hadn’t even been out for twenty-four hours yet and critics were already calling it a masterpiece. To be fair, video game critics call a lot of titles masterpieces, but it was clear from everyone’s immediate reaction to Vice City that Rockstar Games spent the last decade making something truly special. Sure, there are hundreds of hours of side quests and town-foolery to get lost in, but GTA VI also features a Bonnie & Clyde-esque love story between its two playable protagonists—Jason and Lucia—which breaks up the beloved smash-and-grab chaos of the Grand Theft Auto franchise and makes the gamer question just where the point between a video game and real life begins.

At its most basic, Nintendo’s The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake isn’t all that different from the classic, 1998 original. The game certainly looks a lot better on the Nintendo Switch 2 than it did on the Nintendo 64, now that the polygonal characters and sharp outdoors have been converted to more living and breathing graphics. But at the end of the day, you’re still playing the same game that you played in 1998. Don’t take that as a knock, though! Ocarina of Time still tops “Best Video Games of All Time” lists for a reason.

There are times playing Star Wars: Galactic Racer when you will feel like you are going too fast. And yes, the rate at which it will feel like that will shock you. But that’s pod racing! And better than any racing sim, let alone a Star Wars video game with voice acting as good as Galactic Racer, it truly feels like you’re speeding around in a hunk-a-junk that you just cobbled together with space garbage and jet engines.

Following up Fire Emblem: Three Houses was already a tall task, let alone having to follow Fire Emblem: Engage. Both titles combined the best of Japanese RPGs with the new slice-of-life school relationship activities introduced in Three Houses. And now Fortune’s Weave, something of a trilogy-capper for the series, is the most maximalist entry yet. With dozens of characters, choices, a vast map, and four unique story quests that all overlap, there’s so much to do in the latest Fire Emblem game that they could wait another five years before the next entry.

The Blood of Dawnwalker is the debut game from Rebel Wolves, a new studio started by former creatives from The Witcher 3: Blood Hunt. So if Dawnwalker feels eerily like a Witcher game, you’re not crazy. That being said, the vampire game’s controversial timer—which forces players to make quick decisions with big impacts on gameplay—fully separated the RPG and proved that the new studio was ready to try out exciting ideas all its own.

Remedy’s Alan Wake 2 topped Esquire’s Best Video Games of 2023 list three years ago, so you can bet we were excited for the studio’s next game, Control Resonant. The latest is a sequel to 2019’s Control, which now finds its former protagonist Jesse Faden missing. So, support character Dylan Faden, now in the spotlight, takes control to deal with the foreign entities and save his estranged sister.

Can video games have too much of a good thing? Apparently not. Phantom Blade Zero, the umpteenth samurai RPG since Sekiro: Shadows Die Twice and Ghost of Tsushima, dazzled gamers with its combat and fluid martial arts combos. And while it firmly isn’t a soulslike—the developer explicitly rejects the genre, actually—that still doesn’t mean Phantom Blade Zero is a walk in the park either.

How about a game that’s a little less stressful? If you need a break from the fast-paced action, check out Big Walk, a co-operative puzzle game about, well, going on a long walk. The charming game allows players to communicate only if they’re close to one another on Big Walk’s big map, providing a bit of a challenge as you progress on your journey while also adding some intimacy to the digital experience. Plus, the characters are cute as hell.

Star Wars fans ate good this year. If racing isn’t your thing, check out Zero Company, a turn-based tactics game that falls somewhere in between Fire Emblem and XCOM. Though the game isn’t all that challenging on easier difficulties, players have found a lot of fun and repeat plays with the game’s permadeath mode that doesn’t allow characters to return after they die.

Rock out with your claws out. Insomniac applies its winning formula for triple-A superhero games to flesh out the most popular X-Men member of all time. The five-foot-four berserker stars in his own solo adventure in a universe where mutants live in secret. While piecing together his own memories, Wolverine meets a telepath named Jean Grey. Together they must stop a powerful industrialist from unleashing a weapon that could kill all mutants. A hyper-violent tour through some of its title antihero’s classic comics, Wolverine doesn’t quite reach the same heights as Spider-Man, but the way it lets players truly feel like “The Best There Is” is gold.

If you could rewind to your last day of school, what would it look like? Who are you with? And most importantly, what’s on the stereo? At the start of summer, developers Beethoven & Dinosaur released the gorgeous narrative game Mixtape, a loving homage to coming-of-age cinema with a tastefully curated soundtrack. (We’re talking Joy Division, Silverchair, B.J. Thomas, and more.) Set in the summer of 1995, the game sees three California teens relish their final hours together before going their separate ways. While Mixtape kicked up a storm among gamers over what defines a “game”—you can’t die or fail, nor are any of the things you do in Mixtape remotely challenging—it’s still a special experience that I argue illuminates how special games can be as a storytelling medium. Your adolescence probably didn’t look exactly like Mixtape, but the game can nonetheless trigger old memories.

As the world waits with bated breath to see who will play the next James Bond on the big screen, video games have a Bond to call their own in actor Patrick Gibson. From developer IO Interactive, of the smash Hitman series, comes 007 First Light, a high-octane espionage action game that follows a new James Bond in the early days of his career at MI6. Unlike the Hitman games, which are mired in the darkness of Agent 47’s deadly profession, 007 First Light revels in the fantasy of Bond’s perilous and pleasurable lifestyle. It’s basically the closest any of us will get to feeling like James Bond short of renting an Aston Martin for a test drive.

It’s been 12 years since Yacht Club Games released Shovel Knight, which was deemed by many to be one of the leading retro-inspired indie hits of the 2010s. Now Yacht Club has done it again with Mina the Hollower, which mines plenty of inspiration from gothic dungeon crawlers like Castlevania and Game Boy–era Legend of Zelda games, with modern flourishes à la Dark Souls. Players control Mina, a genius 19th-century mouse who must slay monsters and restore power to her home. Don’t let its cute looks fool you: Mina the Hollower is a challenging game that will have you sweating and smiling all the way through.

Remember dropping quarters into Marvel vs. Capcom 2 back in the day? The legacy of Marvel’s 2D-fighting heritage lives on in Marvel Tōkon: Fighting Souls, a shiny new team-based fighter with an anime-inspired take on the Marvel universe. Heroes like Captain America, Iron Man, Spider-Man, Storm, Deadpool, and Blade—and even villains like Doctor Doom—team up to save the world from an alien menace. The game is developed by Arc System Works, arguably the modern masters of the genre, boasting titles like Guilty Gear, BlazBlue, and Dragon Ball FighterZ, and the studio applies its decades of expertise to one of the finest and most nuanced fighting games we’ve ever had the pleasure of throwing our controllers at.

There’s no shortage of killer samurai games nowadays. Which is perhaps why Capcom saw fit to resurrect its dormant Onimusha franchise with the exceptional Onimusha: Way of the Sword. Essentially a reboot of the series (whose last title was released all the way back in 2006 for the PlayStation 2), Way of the Sword follows legendary swordsman Miyamoto Musashi as he comes into possession of the Oni Gauntlet and must cleanse the world of a supernatural evil. While not quite a Souls-like, the game offers an adequately complex combat system that ensures sword slashing never gets stale. And slicing demons in half feels oh so satisfying.

If you, like me, have fond memories of Initial D, Forza Horizon 6 is an invitation to awaken the sleeping gearhead within. The open-world racing series finally makes its way to Japan, the birthplace of drift racing, to let players roam dense city streets and wide-open mountain roads in tricked-out Hondas, Nissans, and exotic supercars. The Forza Horizon series has reached its peak with this sixth installment, with photorealistic graphics that can fool you into believing you’re actually speeding through traffic halfway around the world.

After almost 30 years of Resident Evil games, it’s no surprise that the ninth installment is another polished thrill ride. Capcom plays the hits to great success in Resident Evil Requiem, a survival-horror game that hits the right notes even when it’s serving up Leon S. Kennedy fan service. But in the roughly 10-to-18-hour campaign (a great length!), the gameplay of FBI newbie Grace Ashcroft is more horror focused and full of fresh twists on the Resident Evil formula. Though we won’t spoil it here, Esquire’s review in February also teased that the latest Resident Evil game contains “one major set piece in particular that rivals some of the best action-movie moments in recent years.”

Don’t let Pokémon Pokopia’s cozy gameplay fool you—the latest spin-off of the cutesy monster-collecting phenomenon is actually one of the deepest games in the franchise’s nearly thirty-year history. Sure, you will spend most of your time in Pokopia building houses for your Pokémon to live in or meticulously gardening. But underneath the casual novelties of building a perfect little Pokémon town lives a surprisingly emotional story about climate-change disasters, grief, and the importance of taking care of Earth’s natural environment.

Saros is a gameplay-first, story-second action game that feels incredible. Much as with the developer’s previous game, Returnal, the third-person shooting is inspired by arcade shooters where bullets fill your screen nonstop. Unlike Returnal, it’s not brutally difficult. Gameplay is fast and exhilarating—and sometimes overwhelming—but it doesn’t take long before you know exactly which projectiles to absorb, which to dodge, and which to parry. The PlayStation-exclusive title also has one of the best gameplay loops of the year, and it does more with the DualSense controller’s advanced haptic feedback than any other game on the Sony system. Merging the run-based progression of Hades with a more traditional level-based structure to enormous success, Saros is difficult yet approachable, rewarding players with a ton of resources to invest in upgrades as it slowly unfurls its mystery-box story bit by bit.

With the release of No More Heroes III in 2021, Grasshopper Manufacture finally put the saga of Travis Touchdown to rest and kicked off its next era with Romeo Is a Dead Man. The studio’s latest game retells Shakespeare’s famous love story mashed up with 1980s sci-fi, Japanese tokusatsu, and grind-house cinema. The game puts players in control of Romeo Stargazer, a small-town deputy who falls in love with an amnesiac named Juliet. But when Romeo dies, he is reborn as a half-man, half-machine superhero who embarks on a quest to find his missing love. Romeo Is a Dead Man feels like immersing oneself in an obscure Troma film, and it’s as madcap and magnificent as it sounds.

Remastered video games are always a tough sell—though with every Nintendo fan having no issue coughing up $30 to play Pokémon FireRed & LeafGreen on the Switch 2 this year, it seems times have changed. Luckily Dragon Quest VII Reimagined is much more than a port to a newer system. The remake, which includes many quality-of-life improvements, was rebuilt from the ground up with glorious 3D animation. If you’ve never played the classic Japanese RPG before, the charm of this latest remake is enough to make you a Dragon Quest fan for life.

If you’re too scared to play horror games alone, let Reanimal’s multiplayer gameplay bring people together. Set in an island town overrun with monsters and mysterious creatures, Reanimal follows a nameless brother and sister who must survive the island’s horrors if they want to save their friends. With a story as cryptic as some of its puzzles, Reanimal carries the baton of other memorable platformers like Limbo and Little Nightmares to deliver an otherworldly experience. Although Reanimal can be played alone, it is a journey best taken with someone by your side.

Video games are all about rewarding the player for progression, and there may be no better equivalent to this feeling in the real world than rock climbing. Cairn, a new indie title that focuses on this extreme sport, captures the similarities beautifully. As the player works their way up the mountain, juggling the character’s stamina and gauging the next best foothold, it is amazing how intense and immersive Cairn feels compared with its relatively beautiful visuals. But as with any good climb, the surprisingly difficult journey is more than worth it when you finally reach the top.

If you thought Doom had too many pixelated demons and not enough rubber-hose whimsy, Mouse: P.I. for Hire is your game. Heavily inspired by classic Disney, Fleischer, and MGM cartoons—and clearly taking some cues from the 2017 hit Cuphead—Mouse: P.I. for Hire puts players in control of an anthropomorphic mouse detective on his latest case in Mouseburg. The game’s fast-paced first-person-shooter action is delightfully complemented by its (literally) cartoonish antics. Hand-drawn pistol and shotgun blasts and reloading animations are as much a treat to watch as the game is fun to play. If gaming today feels more complicated than ever, Mouse: P.I. for Hire brings it back to basics with a novelty coat of paint.

Nintendo should have called the new Mario Tennis game Mario Tennis Chaos, because the player needs to understand right up front that skills on the court can carry them only so far in Mario Tennis Fever. Much like the 24 drivers per race in Mario Kart World, a calamity of new power-ups renders tennis into a zany, uncontrollable farce of a sport that becomes one of the best Mario sports titles in Nintendo’s history—once you learn to let go and embrace the nonsense.

The decades-old Marvel vs. Capcom series still reigns supreme as the best superhero tag fighter in video games, but Invincible VS is set on taking the throne. Based on the hit Amazon series (with some of its stars reprising their voice roles, like Oscar-winner J.K. Simmons as Omni-Man), Invincible VS is a 3v3 tag-team fighting game featuring playable characters from Robert Kirkman’s comic-book universe. The game tells a stand-alone story adjacent to season 3 in which Mark must stand with his allies to save Earth from the Viltrumites. (Hence the fighting.) Although its mechanical controls are relatively simple compared with those of other fighting games, it’s no less elegant and fun, and the game features some gnarly cel-shaded 3D animation that’s simply gorgeous. Besides, it’s another way to pummel your buddies to a bloody pulp. Talk about threatening us with a good time.

Dead Space? More like Dad Space. Pragmata is Capcom’s latest original game and a potential franchise in the making, its story centered around a systems engineer named Hugh and his foster-father relationship with an adorable android nicknamed Diana. With a rogue AI overtaking a research station on the moon, Hugh and Diana must work together to take the AI offline and make it back to Earth. Between its breakneck gameplay involving grid-like puzzles (which must be solved on the fly) and its endearing story about parenthood, Pragmata is what you might get if you asked ChatGPT, “What if Alien and Marvel’s Logan could be a single video game?”

#6810. Is The Rings of Power Coming Back for Season 4?

There’s One Ring to rule them all, but two seasons still to go.

There is One Ring to rule them all, but there are still two more seasons to go. The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power on Prime Video is fast approaching the end of its third season, which means some fans are now asking what the future has in store for the prequel series. While there aren’t definitive answers just yet, Tolkien fans can rest assured in knowing that a fourth season is more likely to happen than not. Here’s what we know so far.

On November 25, The Rings of Power will release the final two episodes of season 3. So, is season 4 a shoo-in? Almost. Per The Hollywood Reporter in a May 2026 story, preproduction was, at the time, “eyeing a schedule that includes preproduction on a fourth season this fall, with shooting to begin in early 2027.” As far as anyone outside the production knows, this work for season 4 has been underway.

In 2017, Amazon secured the rights to produce a Lord of the Rings television series for an eye-watering price tag of $250 million. In 2018, The Hollywood Reporter wrote that negotiations between Amazon and the Tolkien estate included a five-season commitment, while Amazon maintains authority to greenlight or cancel the series at any time. In October 2025, The Ankler reported that Tolkien has a $20 million per season “kill fee” if Amazon ends the show before its fifth season.

As of right now, Amazon has not formally greenlit The Rings of Power for season 4. But some signs point to it as inevitable. Amazon has not released viewership numbers for the current season, so only Amazon knows how the show is performing on Prime Video. Whether the audience is strong enough to keep the show going or Amazon simply wants to avoid kill fees (and a negative reputation in the industry for failing to finish a pricey Lord of the Rings show), will decide the future. For now, fans should enjoy the sunrise in Rivendell while there is still one.

#6809. Hailey Bieber Brings New Life to an Archival Carolyn Bessette Kennedy Look

It was pulled from the Yohji Yamamoto Spring/Summer 1998 collection

As one of the leaders of modern-day minimalism, it makes sense that Hailey Bieber would wink to the woman who arguably started it all: Carolyn Bessette Kennedy.

This afternoon, Mrs. Bieber attended the 2026 DealBook Summit in Lincoln Center—a conference that unites some of the biggest leaders in tech, business, and politics—to discuss her mega-successful beauty brand, Rhode.

At the summit, she pulled out a supreme fashion reference, with an archival set from the Yohji Yamamoto Spring/Summer 1998 collection, once worn by none other than serious Yamamoto enthusiast CBK.

The finely tailored look consisted of a black Mandarin-collar jacket with a pinched waist and a split hem, plus a row of matching buttons dotting the front.

It was paired with a cream linen midi skirt that hugged Bieber’s legs, and for accessories, she matched the jacket with inky patent-leather pumps and one of CBK’s favorite finishing touches: an acetate headband.

Bessette Kennedy famously wore this look back in June 1998, when she attended the Robin Hood Foundation benefit with her husband, John F. Kennedy Jr., at the 26th Street Armory in New York City. For the occasion, she tied her hair back into a ponytail and went sans jewelry.

We were all reminded of CBK’s chic style earlier this year, when FX released the smash Love Story: John F. Kennedy Jr. and Carolyn Bessette miniseries. Stars like Bella Hadid and Sarah Pidgeon (the latter of whom portrayed Bessette Kennedy in the show) all seemed to take a page out of the former Calvin Klein publicist’s styling book for their own outfits. And Bieber also joined in on the fun.

But while many have tried to recreate the late muse’s look, few can really capture the true essence of CBK. Clearly, Bieber is one of them.

#6807. The Car Coat Is Back and Cooler Than Ever

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Looking to score one for yourself? We rounded up top-notch options from Dunhill, Percival, and more.

You don’t necessarily need a car coat. If you’re in the market for a new coat and not, like us, learned in the world of 20th century menswear, it just might be one of the few styles you’ve heard the word for—peacoat and trench coat likely being two others. All three are great if you’re looking for something a bit grown up than your average off-duty jacket. A trench coat would give you gravitas, a peacoat versatility; the car coat, though, strikes a nice middle ground. Find the right one, and it can replace 90 percent of your other coats.

At its inception, the car coat was a heavy outer layer. Cars were still open to the elements, so the rich who owned them needed floor-length fur jackets to keep themselves warm as they went motoring and did a lap around the neighborhood on Christmas. As cars became more more enclosed and more part of daily life, the car coat became more practical. The length got shorter—eventually settling around mid-thigh—to be comfortable when you sit in the driver seat but with enough coverage to be considered a coat. Materials shifted away from furs to leather and suede, then to even lighter-weight gabardines and weather-resistant materials. They

But current iterations of the car coat don’t exactly follow a formula. As we’ve seen with Harringtons, brands just start to use the most widely-known phrasing to get guys into buying a coat. We, in the interest of keeping some amount of historical accuracy, will stick to a formula. A car coat has that mid-thigh length, a turn down collar, and a practicality for someone getting in and out of a seat. They’re great during fall, and they can be worn through winter if you live somewhere that doesn’t get consistently below freezing. Here, you’ll find our favorite picks.

We’re starting on the high end, but it’s only because if you’re looking for a by-the-book garment or accessory tied to motoring history, you go to Dunhill. In its original form, Alfred Dunhill’s company sold motoring accessories. Overcoats, goggles, picnic sets, horns, lamps, dashboard clocks—the tagline was “Everything but the motor.” It was a luxury men’s brand for the premier luxury hobby of the time.

The company has gone through several iterations, but has always maintained a bend towards that luxury man. Now, under the creative direction of Simon Holloway, it’s a standard-bearer for a slightly more fashion-forward vision British menswear. Take this car coat for example. It calls back to the house’s original business model, and it is classic in its finishes: turn down collar, natural shoulders, cuff straps, and totally un-adorned through the torso. But the fabric is a gorgeous wool, silk, and cashmere blend. It’s a genius balance between tradition and fashion, one that only Dunhill is well-positioned to put out.

Length: mid-thigh Material: 84% wool, 13% silk, 3% cashmere

Now that we’ve gotten the formalities out of the way, let’s talk value. For the money, Percival is the best on this list. Hell, it is really just the best on this list overall.

The fabric is the same you’d find on a trench—a tight-weave polyester and cotton blend. The cut is simple and easy to wear. The length, crucially, is more suited to the modern driving experience. There’s a bit of bum coverage, but if you were out running errands and getting in and out of the car, you can keep the coat on. We also love the classic colors and patterns the team cut this in.

All of that for an attainable price, it’s one of the best items in the Percival lineup.

Length: just over your rearMaterial: 65% polyester, 35% cotton

Short of finding an Etsy seller, this is probably the closest you’ll get to the genuine car coats of the ‘50 and ‘60s. The fabric flutters just around your knees just like they did way back when, while a classic collar and navy blue hue adds to the retro nostalgia. Made of 100% cotton, the fabric is finished with C0 water repellency and lined with cotton to give the fashion even more function. We’d recommend wearing and styling like you would a trench (though, if you want something more substantial, J.Crew also makes the Rivington in cozy wool for those who in search of a new winter coat.)

Length: mid-thigh, grazes the kneeMaterial: 100% cotton

#6804. What to Eat for Healthier Skin and Slower Aging

A dietitian breaks down the foods, nutrients, and habits that may support skin from the inside out.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

On the quest to age smarter and slower, there’s a lot that your skin care can do. But perhaps one of the greatest contributions lies in the foods you consistently eat. Of course, diet and exercise are the pillars of good health, proper body function, and longevity, but a recent study done by scientists at the German Center for Neurodegenerative Diseases (DZNE) also reveals that 10 specific diets are linked to slower aging. Experts unpack the findings for ELLE and break down which foods you should incorporate or ditch from your diet to help improve skin health.

To understand the connection between diet and skin health, Caitlin Hosmer, MS, RD, LDN, a registered dietitian and co-founder of MenoPantry, says it’s important to define the notion of slowing down aging. She points out that this particular DZNE study didn’t look at wrinkles or how old people feel. “It looked at biological aging, measured with epigenetic clocks. These read chemical marks on our DNA (methylation) that shift as our cells age. In about 7,500 adults, the researchers found that following any of 10 healthy eating patterns more closely was associated with slower biological aging,” Hosmer explains. The 10 eating patterns or diets included in the study results are Mediterranean, Nordic, DASH, MIND, and plant-based. Hosmer says that while the effect was modest, this kind of study shows an association, not causation—proof that diet is the reason for the positive outcome—so it is still a meaningful signal.

Additionally, Mark Gilbert, nutritionist from The 1:1 Diet by Cambridge Weight Plan, tells ELLE that no single food has been satisfactorily studied and shown to be likely, on its own, to slow aging. “Of all the predictors of a long, healthy life, two of the best, which can be improved with diet, are muscle mass and strength, but this leads to a controversial conclusion,” says Gilbert. “The best nutritional intervention to increase muscle and strength is eating a high-protein diet. However, many scientists argue that high-protein diets have the opposite effect on longevity. So, if we can’t even agree on that, you can see that science has a long way to go to try and figure this out.”

One stat Hosmer found particularly striking was that over 70 percent of the biological pathways affected were the same across all ten diets. “I interpret that to mean that no single superfood is doing the work, and it affirms that [a combination of] food, diet, and nutrition impacts us on the cellular level. The foods these patterns share are what matter,” says Hosmer, who, along with Gilbert, breaks down the standouts:

Alongside individual foods, Hosmer says certain diet components have the greatest impact on aging. For starters, protein is a must. “As we age, our muscles respond less efficiently to the protein we eat. This is called anabolic resistance, and in women, the drop in estrogen makes it worse,” she explains. Building and retaining muscle isn’t only about strength; Hosmer says it’s our largest site for handling blood sugar and a strong predictor of independence later in life. “Many women eat less protein just when they need more.”

Gilbert adds that while protein is an important part of a balanced, longevity diet, there are some considerations to keep in mind before adding it to your meal. “I believe that because muscle and strength are so strongly associated with longevity and that protein, particularly later in life, supports them,” he says. The caveat, however, is that there is a potential mechanism (which has to do with the amino acid methionine) whereby too much protein could have negative effects. “Many scientists argue that, because in insects and some other animals, less protein along with calorie restriction increases lifespan, humans would similarly benefit. Also, some cite the so-called ‘Blue Zones’, alleged to show that plant-based, low-protein diets are superior. In my opinion, all of these have been shown to be invalid,” says Gilbert.

Fiber is another essential nutrient that you’ll reap from plant foods and mushrooms. Meat, fish, dairy, and eggs contain no fiber, and because our enzymes can’t break them down, Hosmer says that fiber travels intact to the large intestine to assist. Once there, fermentable fibers become food for the gut microbiome; other fibers mainly add bulk and keep things moving. “As gut bacteria ferment fiber, they make short-chain fatty acids, which nourish the cells lining the gut, help keep that barrier strong, and keep inflammation in check. Fiber also steadies blood sugar and helps the body excrete estrogen the liver has already processed, supporting healthy hormone balance. Yet more than 90 percent of American women don’t reach the recommended 21 to 25 grams a day,” Hosmer explains.

Plant diversity matters, too, and not just quantity. Hosmer points to research from the American Gut Project that found people who ate 30 or more different kinds of plant foods a week had more diverse gut microbiomes than those who ate 10 or fewer. “That’s because each plant brings its own mix of fibers and polyphenols, and some bacteria have preferences, so a varied diet feeds a broader community of microbes. A broader community does more jobs. It makes short-chain fatty acids that protect the gut lining, helps regulate immunity and inflammation, turns plant compounds into forms the body can use, and even helps process estrogen.” The variety of plants Hosmer adds seems to matter more than any single “perfect” plant.

You’ve likely heard of healthy fats, meaning omega-3s from fish and the polyphenol-rich fat in olive oil. Adding these to your diet can support cell membranes and help settle inflammation.

Also on the list for Gilbert is vitamin D, which he says studies have shown to be inversely related to the risk of mortality. “This does not prove that low vitamin D is the cause, but it is compelling evidence. Although some results are controversial, low [vitamin D] levels have been linked to all-cause, cardiovascular, cancer, and infection-related mortality,” he adds. Vitamin D is intimately involved in immunity, too. It protects T-cell immune response.

Although Gilbert says, “The only thing all good nutritionists agree on is that there is no one ‘best’ diet or ‘best’ food,” he warns that for most people, minimizing the intake of sugar and refined, starchy grains and increasing protein intake is, in my opinion and experience, the best approach, but, again, different people can thrive on different diets. “As in the examples I gave, many of these foods contain phytochemicals—plant chemicals like polyphenols, flavanols, cyanidins, and others – which affect disease risk or support systems in the body that are likely to serve you well in old age.”

Hosmer notes several other red flags as contributing to unhealthy skin: Diets dominated by refined carbohydrates and added sugars, heavily processed meat, and regular alcohol. “Each of these drives the chronic, low-grade inflammation researchers call “inflammaging,” and blood-sugar instability.”

Having seen so many gaps in these important nutrients in her practice, and understanding how women’s bodies change in midlife, led Hosmer and her team to build Menomix at MenoPantry. “One serving adds meaningful protein along with ground flaxseed and psyllium fiber, plus a range of plant ingredients. It’s made to help close the protein, fiber, and plant-variety gaps that so many women are carrying,” she adds.

To dive deeper into some of those gaps or bad habits that can send skin aging off the rails, Hosmer offers some eating patterns that push biology in the wrong direction:

The information can be overwhelming, but instead of purging everything in your fridge, Hosmer suggests first focusing on adding good foods instead of restricting less healthful options. “In my experience, that’s more sustainable, both because it feels less like deprivation and because the “good” foods can displace the ones that speed aging up instead of slowing it down. You don’t need to pick the “right” diet and follow it perfectly.”

She highlights that the DZNE study found few people scored well on all ten diets at once, yet better adherence to any of them helped. What matters is the common ground. Mediterranean, Nordic, DASH, MIND, and plant-based diets vary on the plate, but they have a common core: more fruits, vegetables, and whole grains, and less red and processed meat, and added sugar. “That shared core is where I’d start,” says Hosmer, offering this meal planning guide:

Increase your familiarity with, and use of, plant-based proteins. Plant-based proteins offer an excellent package of beneficial nutrients, including fiber (not found in animal products). If you aren’t already familiar, learn how to include whole soy foods (tofu, tempeh, edamame) in your diet; add beans and peas and lentils to dishes here and there. Nuts and seeds and nut and seed butters count here also.

Build meals around vegetables and fruit. Fill half your plate with them, and choose deep colors like leafy greens, berries, beets, and cabbage-family vegetables. Those colors come from polyphenols, plant compounds that act as antioxidants and feed beneficial gut bacteria.

Make your grains whole and varied. Oats, rye, barley, brown rice, and whole-wheat bread are staples of the Nordic and DASH patterns, the two most consistently linked to slower aging in this study. Try a grain you haven’t eaten before—quinoa? Farro? Millet or teff? They are all cooked the same way—in boiling water, just with different grain-to-water ratios. Note: raise fiber gradually, with plenty of water, so your gut has time to adjust.

Count your plants, not your calories. Aim toward 30 different kinds of plant foods a week. Veggies, fruits, nuts, seeds, whole grains, beans, peas, herbs, and spices all count. A homemade trail mix, an interesting salad, or a smoothie with a handful of fruit and veggies can add three or four at once.

Choose your fats deliberately. Most of these patterns favor unsaturated fats over saturated ones. Use extra-virgin olive oil as your everyday fat, and eat oily fish two or three times a week.

Keep red and processed meat occasional, and keep added sugar low. Every one of these diets limits them.

Quality matters. Note that “plant-based” isn’t automatically healthy. A version of the plant-based diet built on refined grains, sweets, and sugary drinks tended to be associated with faster aging, as did a pro-inflammatory eating pattern overall. Food quality matters, not just food category.

Diet is the foundation the other efforts rest on. Collagen supplements and skin care can help, but they can’t make up for a body that lacks what it needs to build and protect tissue. According to Gilbert, collagen supplements contain very high levels of the amino acid glycine, which he says can balance the potential harms of proteins that are high in methionine.

Hosmer further explains that collagen peptides break down during digestion into amino acids and small peptides. Your body then decides where to use them. “To actually build collagen, it needs enough total protein, plus vitamin C. Vitamin C is a required helper for the enzymes that stabilize the collagen structure. It also needs minerals like zinc and copper. So a collagen supplement taken on top of a protein-poor, micronutrient-poor diet is not going to be optimized,” Hosmer says. “Collagen is also an incomplete protein: it’s low in leucine and lacks tryptophan entirely. It can add to your protein intake, but it isn’t a substitute for complete proteins when it comes to muscle.”

On the surface, what we eat affects the skin from the inside. Hosmer notes that polyphenol-rich foods and healthy fats support the skin’s own defenses against oxidative stress, and high blood sugar leads to glycation, which stiffens collagen fibers. The gut matters here, too. “Researchers call the connection the gut–skin axis. When the gut lining is healthy, and the microbiome is well fed, inflammation throughout the body tends to stay lower, and the skin reflects that. External products can only do so much if we aren’t supporting our skin from the inside,” Hosmer says.

Diet plays a major role in overall skin health and slower aging, but Hosmer argues that it’s not the full picture. To optimize more youthful-looking skin, consider other factors that may be equally as important as diet.

#6802. Quilted Liner Jackets Are the Swerve Your Wardrobe Needs. Here Are 10 Stellar Picks.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Layering is the name of the game in fall and winter. For the next few months, you are not a person; you’re a living, breathing Russian nesting doll. You are encased in strategic layers of t-shirts, sweaters, scarves, light jackets, and big jackets that you divest yourself of over the course of a day (or night). Like a knight donning his armor, you will spend this season dutifully putting on and taking off each of these individual pieces as you fight the seemingly endless battle against winter.

A few pieces are a given. A good tee certainly isn’t going anywhere, nor is a sweater. The specific kind of Big Coat you use varies depending on the guy and his style, but it’s nevertheless a guaranteed winter staple. Those middle layers can be a bit of a toss up, though, which is how you might encounter something like a quilted liner jacket.

Once upon a time, these padded jackets served as the interior protective shell worn under World War II era flight jackets before expanding into more mainstream outerwear. The quilted padding traps body heat, but doesn’t make the jacket so heavy that it just feels like another cumbersome layer. You can still wear it as a liner jacket—but lowkey, if it’s the right temperature, they also work pretty well on their own. So if you’re in the market for a new versatile transitional weather jacket, you’ve come to the right place.

If we had to pick one from this entire group, it would be this jacket from Buck Mason ... And it’s really not that close. Inspired by old-school mountaineering outfits, the nylon is woven in Japan, and the down fill has an exceptional warmth rating. Add a elastic cuffs and a fit that works layered underneath or overtop, it’s perfect.

Look, layering’s great and all—but no one talks about how annoying and awkward it is when you get hot enough to take off one layer, but then are stuck just kind of holding onto it until you get the chance to put it down or hang it up. That’s not a problem with this packable Paul Smith shirt jacket. Let it do its magic to keep you warm. When you’re ready to take it off, simply collapse it down, zip it up, and stuff it away into your pocket until you need it again.

Rains has a well-established reputation of balancing fashion and function with their outerwear, whether it’s their Esquire-editor favorite Fishtail Parka or this bomber liner jacket. Nothing beats a classic leather bomber...except maybe the rain (leather + rain = no bueno). That’s not an issue with Rains’ Banja bomber liner. You get that same edgy look, but with a slightly more practical construction. Plus there’s something sleekly minimal about the channel quilting. Layer it beneath your actual bomber or heavy coat of choice for optimal warmth all season long.

Look, we love a good Dickies moment. They make some of our favorite pants of all time, not least of which because they’re mad affordable—though that’s a pretty big element to it. So naturally, if someone comes up to us asking for a high-quality and stylish quilted liner jacket at well under $100, we’ve got one word for ya: Dickies.

Barbour’s been making sturdy outerwear that can withstand the rainy U.K. weather since the 1800s. And while they don’t strictly make a liner jacket, this lightweight—or “Flyweight,” as Barbour calls it—quilted jacket gets pretty close.

With a sleek, collarless design, this Fieldworth jacket can easily slide under any heavy coat. It falls more into “middleweight jacket” territory, meaning it’ll probably keep you toastier than some of the other options on this list—both on its own and when layered up.

If you’re looking for more of a typical fall/spring jacket, J.Crew’s PrimaLoft® jacket is where it’s at. The quintessentially Americana brand uses the down alternative to create a well-insulated transitional weather jacket you can layer with, or wear on its own.

Look, all these other brands and designers are great and all—but Alpha Industries is the real deal. No like seriously. When the company was first founded in Tennessee in the 1950s, it was as a clothing contractor for the United States Department of Defense. These were the guys making those sexy flight jackets long before Tom Cruise shrugged one on in Top Gun. Their Deco Liner nods to the OG liners of yore, featuring a slim, collarless design that will integrate with additional layers. Wear the jacket by itself when you start feeling warmer, and let the whole world see the Alpha embroidery on the back so everyone knows this is some top of line shit.

If you’re in the market for something lighter and less padded, opt for this cotton hunting jacket. The silhouette takes inspiration from vintage Czech military liners and traditional hunting gear to create a layer of outerwear breezy but still warm.

Get two jackets for the price of one with this reversible number from Cos. You get all the benefits of a quilted coat, with the versatile sleekness of a classic collarless jacket.

#6801. A New Exhibition Celebrates the Mantilla in Fashion and Art

The garment finally gets its due credit at The Hispanic Society Museum & Library.

Few garments have been as imitated, reinterpreted, and misunderstood as the mantilla. The iconic lace veil with Spanish roots has endured for centuries. What started as an object traditionally worn for ceremonies has had a renaissance via Madonna, Rosalía, and Dolce & Gabbana, just to name a few. That’s just one of the reasons why The Hispanic Society Museum & Library in New York is opening its new exhibition “Mantilla: Fashion Unveiled” beginning November 20. The museum calls the mantilla the “most distinctive garment in Spanish women’s fashion with origins dating to the 16th century.” Curated by Spanish-born scholar Carlos Usabiaga López, the show merges fashion, art, and archival material to shine a light on an iconic garment that has been donned on screen, on runways, and on red carpets for generations.

“The understanding of the mantilla in all its complexity is often limited, either by a superficial reading of the symbol itself or by its association with an old-fashioned, rather conservative image of Spain,” López tells ELLE. “This exhibition challenges the perception of the mantilla as an anachronistic relic of the past and reclaims its continued cultural relevance and subversive potential. From Spanish countercultural movements such as ‘La Movida Madrileña’ to drag performance, the mantilla has repeatedly been appropriated and reimagined in ways that reveal its capacity to carry new meanings.”

The exhibition is the second installment in the Hispanic Society’s multiyear fashion initiative, which focuses on reinterpreting its own renowned collection through a new lens. The museum was founded in 1903 by American philanthropist Archer Milton Huntington and has many visual examples of the garment, including Francisco Goya’s famed portrait of María del Pilar Teresa Cayetana de Silva y Álvarez de Toledo, the 13th Duchess of Alba (1762–1802). “By bringing together works of art, fashion, and material culture, the iconography of the mantilla activates a collection of such an extraordinary diversity, through unexpected dialogues between the museum’s different departments and international loans,” adds López.

The new exhibition pulls from many cultural moments new and old, including the “Spanish Craze,” which was a period of cultural fascination with Spain in the United States spanning 1890 to 1930. “During this period, the mantilla became part of a highly recognizable visual language through which Spain was imagined and represented in America,” says López. “Spanish performers who travelled to the United States achieved remarkable success: Raquel Meller, for instance, appeared on the cover of Time magazine in 1926, while Concha Piquer starred in From Far Seville (1923).” He also points to the reinterpretation of the mantilla in The Devil Is a Woman (1935), starring Marlene Dietrich.

Fashion naturally plays a role in the show, especially through the imagination of emerging designers, like Arturo Obegero. “Throughout the history of fashion, major designers such as Cristóbal Balenciaga and Óscar de la Renta have drawn inspiration from this [Spanish] visual language, including the mantilla,” adds López. “However, it was a curatorial decision to bring contemporary designers, including emerging voices, into direct conversation with the masterpieces in order to make justice to the mantilla’s enduring influence on fashion to this day.”

The mantilla has been loved (and reinvented) in contemporary fashion at least since the 1940s, when it appeared in numerous fashion magazines. Today, the references are endless and people of all cultures and backgrounds are embracing it. “I think there is something particularly beautiful about the fact that those who have historically been excluded from the dominant narrative, who may have felt unrepresented or even oppressed by these national symbols, can now feel encouraged to revisit this folklore and expand its boundaries to make room for others to do the same,” says López. As the exhibition makes clear, the mantilla’s compelling story is still unfolding.

“Mantilla: Fashion Unveiled” is on view at The Hispanic Society Museum & Library from Nov. 20, 2026, through March 28, 2027.

#6795. Where Is Caroline Ellison, Portrayed by Julia Garner in The Altruists, Now?

The cryptocurrency executive spent 11 months in prison.

This story contains spoilers for The Altruists.

Netflix’s The Altruists tells the true story of FTX, the celebrated cryptocurrency exchange, and the trading firm Alameda Research, which both collapsed amid accusations of wrongdoing by executives. Anthony Boyle and Julia Garner play Sam Bankman-Fried, who founded the company, and Caroline Ellison, the co-CEO of Alameda, respectively. The two, who met while working for the New York City firm Jane Street, are said to have been a couple at one point. Both were sent to prison on charges of fraud, with Ellison having been released this year. Here’s what you need to know about where she is now.

A native of Massachusetts and a Stanford grad, Ellison joined Alameda as a trader in 2018. She was promoted to co-CEO in 2021.

Ellison was sentenced to 24 months in prison in 2024 after pleading guilty to seven charges, including wire fraud and money laundering. Prosecutors praised Ellison for her cooperation, which included testifying against Bankman-Fried. “In her many meetings with the government, Ellison approached her cooperation with remarkable candor, remorse and seriousness…and she persevered despite harsh media and public scrutiny and Bankman-Fried’s efforts to publicly weaponize her personal writings to discredit and intimidate her,” they wrote.

Ellison served about 11 months in a Connecticut prison before being moved to what was referred to as community confinement, which typically means being under house arrest or living in a halfway house. She was released in January 2026.

While in college, Ellison became interested in effective altruism. The movement, which is popular within tech circles, urges people to use evidence to make decisions that will yield the most good for society. That can mean choosing to take a high-paying salary rather than volunteering, believing that the money earned will provide more benefit.

The nonprofit Manifund, a grant-funding organization that was inspired by the effective altruism movement and had deep ties to FTX, hired Ellison. The nonprofit’s CEO, Austin Chen, announced in September that she had begun working over the summer. “I believe in redemption. Caroline has admitted her faults, worked to make creditors whole, and served her time in prison. I’d like to give her the opportunity to contribute back to the world; I hope that the world will concur,” he wrote.

Chen quoted Ellison in his announcement. “I’m deeply sorry for what I did, and I completely understand why people may not want to work with me. But I’m very grateful to Austin for giving me a second chance, and judging me on my current work performance rather than my past. I feel very lucky not only to have a job, but to be working somewhere that I think is doing great work and has a mission I believe in,” she wrote.

#6793. We Really Need to Talk About the MobLand Season 2 Finale

A certain character’s fate hangs in the balance.

Nothing is simple in MobLand. Two seasons in, the plot has racked up more lines than Eddie put up his nose the night he stabbed Tommy Stevenson in season 1. Actually, that’s not strictly true. One thing is simple in MobLand: Eddie. He is a simpleton of truly Homeric (as in Simpson) proportions: a smarmy, sneering, simpering Freudian mess, channelled with gimlet-eyed precision by Anson Boon. All he wants is his God-given right to be respected.

Trouble is, when you’re an anti-Midas who turns everything he touches to shit, respect is a dish that never leaves the kitchen. Eddie is, in Kevin’s words in season 1, “a knifey little twerp.” That stabbing set the whole bloody saga in motion, so it’s only right that two seasons later we end with Eddie the Incompetent. Full circle, then, like a roundabout made of corpses.

MobLand opened with Eddie turning a stabbing into a gang war, so it seems fitting that we end with Eddie turning an assassination into an own goal. But we’ll get to that.

It seems Conrad’s chickens are coming home to roost, and his marbles are jangling around his head like loose change. We open the episode with his card being declined at a petrol station. In frustration, he shoots the till boy dead. But he knows what it means.

“We’re getting lifted,” he tells Maeve in the car. Police are raiding the entire business: bank accounts are frozen, warrants are out, and Tattersall is telling reporters the Harrigans have links to terror groups across the globe. The family convenes at a dingy old safe house out of town.

“We’re terrorists,” says Seraphina. “We’re looking at life in Guantánamo. It’s now or never.”

Her plan has been bubbling for a while. She calls it Harrigan 2.0: going global.

Remember the diplomatic suitcase of diamonds Seraphina collected from her billionaire stepdad Charlton in the last episode? It was meant to fund her and Bella’s takeover of the Harrigan operation. Now, it seems, Harry has it. He takes her aside to let her know he knows. “How did you know?” she asks, incredulous.

Then he says the three words he says more than any others in MobLand: “It’s my job.”

Inside the safe house, the family are all there, and everyone has a plan to pitch. Kevin wants to crawl back to Kat and hand over Conrad, Maeve, and Eddie. “Clean the house,” he growls to Harry, out of earshot of the others.

Conrad, meanwhile, isn’t sure what to do. “What are our options?” he asks Harry.

“We don’t have any,” comes the terse reply. “Either we run seven ways and it’s every man and woman for themselves, or we go back to Kat and try to strike some kind of deal.”

Kevin proposes offering her 50 percent and first working out who’s a liability. “And right now I’m seeing a lot of liabilities. Right, Harry?”

Bella has had it up to ’ere with Kevin’s pusillanimity. “Oh, you nearly got to the end of a sentence without saying ‘Harry,’” she taunts. “I know he saved you from a bumming once but… Jesus.”

Kevin pulls a pistol and fires, missing her ear by millimeters. Conrad draws. “Put the gun down, boy,” he hisses. Bella steps forward. “Go on then, big boy.”

You could cut the tension with piano wire.

“Fuck this, I’m getting us out of this mess,” says Seraphina, defusing the standoff by fetching a fistful of diamonds from the car. “Fifteen Type IIa brilliants. North of ten million euros,” she tells Conrad, placing them in his hand.

The plan: offer Kat a cut of the empire. “This time tomorrow we’re back in business.”

Remember that glorious, long-winded speech Conrad gave in Spain about the good old days, how he came from nothing, and how Maeve has been his compass through the carnage of his life? “We were born outlaws and we’ll die outlaws,” he bellowed.

Well, now he knows the jig is up. Looking balefully at the floor, he grunts: “Do it.”

So how will Harry play this? He slips out to his 4x4 and rings Kat. “At 5 p.m. I will meet your diplomatic courier. I will pass to said courier €10 million in untraceable diamonds. Upon authentication, I will give you the location of the Harrigan family.”

A double cross? It must be a ruse. He simply MUST have a plan… Then, hanging up, he mutters: “Fuck this, I’m done.”

He returns to the family and lies: “Kat will take the ten million and 50 percent.” Too easy by half.

As Frankie put it two episodes ago: “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

We’re more than halfway through and haven’t heard a peep from Frankie, our laconic, crocodilian villain. Ah, but here he is, getting the news of Harry’s volte-face from Tattersall. “How do we know it’s not a bluff?”

“He’s not stupid,” says Tattersall. “He knows when the game’s up.”

“Well, you know me,” leers Frankie. “I can’t resist a gamble.” Guns are grabbed from car boots, and off he pootles with a gaggle of tooled-up goons to send the Harrigans back to Hell.

It’s all unfolding very quickly now, as denouements always should. Back at the safe house, Conrad is still seeing the dead, including the petrol station lad he just murdered. Maeve says he’s been saying their names in his sleep.

The old loon’s marbles are now departing his head like passengers from a cancelled flight. But there’s one thing that’ll straighten any man out: a black pearl of Maeve wisdom. “We’ve been stuck up tighter holes than Kat McAllister’s,” she reassures him. “But when you’re stuck up someone’s hole, there’s at least two ways out.”

Quite so. And who better to lead the way than Harry? As ever, he picks the exit that leads straight back to where he came in. Unbeknownst to us, he has gone up Kat’s backside, rummaged about, and come out the other end holding her entire career. The “betrayal” was a ruse from the start.

So when Frankie’s goons arrive armed to the teeth, it’s a trap: flash grenades, smoke grenades, poison gas, and Zosia on the exit with a machine gun for anyone daft enough to run. “Oh, Harry,” concedes Frankie, before a mighty gunfight erupts and Harry tears through the house like Keyser Söze in a gas mask. “So this isn’t the beginning of a beautiful friendship,” smiles Frankie, choking in the corner. Harry shoots him in the knee.

Then Harry rings Kat. Those “untraceable” diamonds? Very traceable indeed, straight to a terrorist organization. He has bribed a low-ranking diplomatic courier to deliver them along with a “little present, just for you.”

She opens the box, ashen-faced. It’s Donnie’s blue and rotting head. “This one you might find harder to justify,” he tells her.

“My family are gonna hunt you down and feed you your balls,” she spits.

“Probably,” says Harry. “But not before they feed you yours.”

Checkmate, Kat. The black queen has no more squares to go.

But wait … Harry can offer her one square. “I can fix this,” he says.

Here’s what she’s going to do. She’ll resign. She’ll kill the police investigation. The Harrigans’ accounts will reopen and they’ll come off the terror list.

Cut to a London corridor of power, where a faceless civil servant informs DS Kavanagh that “continuation of your terrorism financing line will interfere with an active joint intelligence operation between British and American security services. You will stand down at the close of business.”

And so, full circle. Maeve is electrocuting Frankie’s balls with a car battery. Kevin is building bridges with Bella. Conrad is going madder by the day. And Eddie?

The Semtex he blackmailed Paul for was meant for Harry, at Maeve’s behest. In the hands of any other Harrigan, this would be a doddle. But not Eddie. Not the knifey little twerp, a man who could trip over his own foreskin in an empty car park.

And he does. But first, the hints. Something strange has been going on with Jan all episode: the soppy Damien Rice soundtrack, the wistful dream sequences of the good times she shared with Harry. Her Harry. The one she fell for in the first place. She seems to have forgiven him. They’re even taking a family holiday.

But there’s one last stop before they go: Harrigan HQ, where Eddie is lying in wait. While Harry negotiates the terms of the holiday with Conrad, Gina and Jan stroll over to the lake. It’s Eddie’s moment, his chance to shine. He plants the Semtex in their car, hides around the corner, arms the bomb, finger hovering over the detonator … then a phone call.

By Finn McCool’s cobblers, it’s Kelly “Mad Dog” O’Connor, the lunatic son of the Medusa, whom Maeve sent Eddie to kill. “I’m coming for you,” growls the voice. “The Mad Dog is coming.”

Gina, Jan and Harry return to the car. Only Jan gets in. In his quivering, fearful state, Eddie trips and drops the detonator.

That’s Jan up in smoke, along with Harry’s plans for a quiet life. It may spell the end of Jan and all her problems, but it’s only the beginning of Eddie’s. He now has Mad Dog O’Connor to answer to, and Harry.

Then the credits roll, and what a gloriously cruel place to leave it.

The only other loose end is Frankie. We’re left to assume he met a long, painful death at the business end of Maeve’s car battery, but we never see a body. I truly hope he does a Harry and wriggles out of one last tight hole—Maeve’s hole—with one good knee and a long memory.

Season 3 can’t come soon enough. When Harry works out whose finger was on the detonator, Eddie’ll wish a knifey little twerp is all he could be.

#6792. The Best Shearling Shoes for Cold-Weather Style and Comfort

Cozy, polished pairs to wear all winter long.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a reason shearling keeps coming back every cold season. It’s the rare material that manages to be cozy and stylish at once, with a plush, cloud-like lining that wraps your feet in warmth and a rich texture that instantly makes an outfit feel more intentional. What began as practical gear for sheepherders and pilots has become a fixture of modern closets, showing up on everything from chic boots to sleek slippers, ballet flats, and fashion-forward sneakers.

Part of its staying power is function. Shearling is naturally insulating, breathable, and moisture-wicking, so your feet stay warm without overheating. Versatility is another reason why shearling has become so popular. The same material that elevates a pair of jeans and a sweater on a weekend errand run can make lounging at home feel like a small luxury.

Whether you’re hunting for the perfect slip-on to kick off at the door, a boot to carry you through winter, or a statement piece that turns heads, below are the must-have shearling shoes that earn a spot in your rotation this season.

Worn with tailored trousers, a slouchy knit, or bare ankles and a long wool coat, the shearling ballet flat moves effortlessly between the polished and the pared-back. The easiest way to make winter feel a little more cozy and luxurious.

The loafer, softened for winter. A shearling lining lends quiet warmth to a classic silhouette, proving that heritage tailoring and true comfort can share the same shoe.

#6790. James Bond’s Favorite Luggage Brand Has Broken Its Own Rules

Globe-Trotter has spent more than a century perfecting its signature suitcase. Now it's trying something different.

James Bond has always travelled in style. And while Daniel Craig's 007 was most immediately associated with his Aston Martins, Omega watches, and Tom Ford suits, his luggage of choice was Globe-Trotter, the Hertfordshire-based manufacturer whose cases have also found favor with everyone from Queen Elizabeth II to Kate Moss.

Now the company has done something completely left-field. After 129 years of making essentially the same kind of suitcase, it has changed the formula.

The new GT897 is Globe-Trotter's lightest case yet, and a significant departure from the design that made the brand famous. Out go the traditional rigid frame and locks, replaced by a zip-around opening and softer, more streamlined silhouette.

That said, some familiar details keep it firmly on-brand: the vulcanized fiberboard, leather trim, nickel hardware, and those trademark exposed rivets. And the cases are still handcrafted in Hertfordshire.

Currently available in two color combinations—bland-and-black and green-and-brown—the GT897 is priced at $1,395. A black-and-oxblood version follows in February.

It's an interesting move for a company whose USP has always rested somewhat on its refusal to change. Whether the new James Bond will follow Craig's lead remains to be seen. But at least there's now a new option for packing his tuxedo.

#6788. The Best Gingerbread Perfumes to Shop for a Cozy, Elevated Winter Scent

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These are the festive, spicy perfumes worth wearing now.

’Tis the season to embrace all things cozy, and that includes gingerbread. The scent isn’t just wafting from our ovens and favorite coffee shops anymore. It’s showing up in our everyday lives, too, as gingerbread fragrances grow more popular.

In perfume, gingerbread is a gourmand accord: warm ginger, molasses, and cinnamon, usually rounded out with vanilla or woods. Ginger is a clever ingredient. Perfumers often use it as a top note, where it adds sparkle and energy, and it pairs beautifully with pepper, citrus, rich woods, and amber. That’s why a good gingerbread perfume can feel cozy without turning into a candle.

Leave it to Squishmallows to create the cutest gingerbread fragrance, complete with a vintage-inspired Cam the Cat bottle. This cozy scent blends ginger, coffee biscuit, and caramel for a sweet, snuggly treat that smells like a warm bakery on a chilly morning.

Jo Malone’s Ginger Biscuit is back for the holiday season. This warm, spicy scent blends ginger, cinnamon, and roasted hazelnut, giving your fragrance wardrobe a cozy, bakery-fresh addition that’s perfect for chilly days.

For on-the-go delight, this travel-size spray delivers warm sweetness with a hint of citrus zest. Slip it into your bag for a quick spritz of holiday cheer whenever you need it.

A scent that’s sure to put you in a festive mood, Baked Gingerbread & Brown Sugar makes holiday spritzing feel extra sweet, cozy, and celebratory.

Although it’s not entirely inspired by gingerbread, this Dior Spice Blend fragrance feels luxuriously rich, with notes of fresh rum, black pepper, and a ginger accord.

Freshly made gingerbread dough comes to mind with every whiff of this unisex fragrance. Described as a golden embrace, the scent feels familiar yet unexpected. It’s heartwarming yet sensual, and it lingers on the skin long after the holidays are over.
