#6786. Who Will Be the New Bond Villain? We Nominate These 5 Actors.

Robert Pattinson has been confirmed as the new 007, but every Bond needs a worthy adversary.

As Alfred Hitchcock put it: “The better the villain, the better the picture.” And nowhere is this truer than for James Bond—a character whose movie is only as good as its baddie.

Jaws, Blofeld, Le Chiffre, Goldfinger—they are the daddies of all baddies; they put the “fun” in funeral, the “ooh-er” in evildoer. They all have a thing, too, because a good baddie always needs a thing. Jaws has the teeth, Le Chiffre the bleeding eye, Blofeld the needy cat, and Goldfinger, well, he’s a kinky old bastard who paints women head to toe in gold to feel like he’s bonking his own bullion.

Which brings me to the question tugging at every Bond fan’s cufflink. Now that Robert Pattinson has been confirmed as the new 007, he’ll need a gold-plated villain to go about “expecting” him.

So which actors would you like to see as the next Bond villain? We have some ideas.

Not famous enough, you say? Not got the chops for such a big-ticket role? Hasn’t earned it, has he? Pah, I’m here to say the actor-slash-musician has already played the best villain on television this year in MobLand’s Frankie Cooper. I’ve gushed about that performance on this website before, and I will again. He plays Cooper with the creeping, glassy-eyed energy of Jimmy Savile—all tracksuits and mirthful threats. But into that he stirs a dollop of Ben Kingsley’s Don “Yes you fucking will” Logan in Sexy Beast. There’s a light nod to Heath Ledger’s Joker there too. In all, it’s a monumental performance—laconically camp, cockney, impishly insane, and properly cackle-out-loud funny. For copyright reasons, I suppose we can’t expect a carbon copy of Frankie transplanted into Bond. But he offers the same vibe: a villain at once unsettling and amusing, which is the hardest trick in the Bond playbook.

There was something in the way he played Pennywise in the adaptation of Stephen King’s It that crawled under my skin like a hookworm. I think it’s the eyes. Bill Skarsgård has a mild lazy eye, which he uses in certain roles to create a disorienting swivel-eyed gaze. He used it for Pennywise. He’s a uniquely physical actor, too, well versed in roles that make children cry. He uses his body like a prop, twisting, deforming, looming, leering as the part demands. He played Count Orlok in Nosferatu, Eric Draven in The Crow and Marquis Vincent Bisset de Gramont in John Wick: Chapter 4. A small snag: he recently said he was “spent” playing bad guys. But he added that, if the right Bond villain landed on his desk, he’d be tempted out of baddie retirement for one last job. “[I] just want to go in and do one Mads Mikkelsen villain,” said the actor, referencing Mikkelsen’s character, Le Chiffre, in Casino Royale.

It’s time for a female lead villain. Sure, Bond has known plenty of miscreant women and seductresses, from Xenia Onatopp (GoldenEye) to Pussy Galore (Goldfinger). But few outright lead villains—hat tip to Lotte Lenya (Rosa Klebb in From Russia with Love) and Sophie Marceau (Elektra King in The World Is Not Enough). It’s a part for a proper actor, a doyenne of screen and stage. A dame, no less. Judi Dench played M across seven films as the one person Bond couldn’t charm, scold, or seduce his way past. Imagine that same withering, headmistress-like authority pointed the other way. Helen Mirren has the gravitas and the twinkle. She’s an Oscar winner who has played a queen, a gangster’s mum in the Fast & Furious films and, of course, the foul-mouthed matriarch Maeve Harrigan in MobLand. She would hardly break a sweat as a Bond villain.

Look at that face: the heavy brow, the gaunt cheeks, the dark, brooding eyes. It’s a face that looks like it was carved by candlelight—a mug made for lurking in the shadows. He’s got a bunch of baddies in his locker, from M. Bison in Street Fighter (2026) to Gareth, the Gemini Killer, in Dexter: Resurrection (2025). But perhaps his biggest ace in the hole is his turn as Piter De Vries in Denis Villeneuve’s Dune (2021). Villeneuve likes to work with actors he knows.

Oh, and the other thing: he really wants it. Dastmalchian explained why in a 2023 interview with /Film: “Trust me when I say that I can bring something to an enemy of 007, whether he or she or they be played in a way that no one has ever seen Bond brought to life before, which is, of course, the way that you guys always do it. I think there’s no one to push Bond to the limits of their capabilities the way that I could, and I would love to do it. So there’s my pitch to them. I can’t wait for them to read this.”

She’s already won an Oscar for playing a corporate villain in Michael Clayton, and she’s never met a prosthetic nose, fur collar or killer blow-dry she couldn’t weaponize... see Snowpiercer’s Minister Mason, Okja’s Lucy Mirando or the White Witch of Narnia—tick, tick, tick. She’s a chameleon with a stare that could freeze a martini.

More than that, Swinton is a master (mistress?) of the in-between, able to embrace and blur lines of gender, age, and species at the click of a slender finger. She possesses that rarest of powers: to change her physical presence, voice, and demeanor so completely as to render herself unrecognizable. Give her a lair, great hair and a long cigarette holder (maybe a Strawberry Ice vape—it’s 2026), and you have a thing of beauty and terror. Because if every great Bond villain needs a thing, Swinton’s is that she can be anything.

#6784. All the Details on Hailey Bieber’s Viral Blue K-Beauty Sheet Mask

The soothing mask is packed with PDRN, blue azulene, and mucin—and it’s coming to Amazon soon.

Hailey Bieber does not play about her skin care. The Rhode Skin founder recently posted a now-viral Instagram carousel showing the many masks she uses to treat her skin. Among the mix of traditional sheet masks, LED devices, rubber masks, and traditional creams was one standout—a blue sheet mask that has sent the internet down a rabbit hole. But of course we’re skin care sleuths, and we gathered all the details, including its benefits and where to buy it.

Jennifer Seo, head concierge at Aneue, a bespoke beauty and wellness travel concierge, immediately ID’d the mask and knows the brand well. She tells ELLE the mask is from Korean skin care brand Pyderin and that its PDRN Lift Cream is the most reordered product among her clients.

Bieber is wearing the Pyderin Hydrovuster PDRN SOS Hyal Soothing Mask, which is part of the brand’s Hydrovuster line. Seo says the lineup was developed as a hydrating, calming collection to soothe post-treatment skin or sensitive, reactive skin. The blue tint isn’t just for aesthetic purposes either; it comes from blue azulene, an anti-inflammatory and calming ingredient that also reduces redness. Dr. Chang Son, a board-certified dermatologist based in NYC, says that its main benefit is hydration. “There may also be an immediate soothing benefit, particularly for skin following sun exposure or experiencing irritation, along with a potential instant cooling effect that leaves the skin feeling refreshed and comforted,” Son explains.

What sets this mask apart from other sheet masks is that it’s soaked in a pharmaceutical-grade serum packed with PDRN in its highest purity. “[The treatment] is highly concentrated and synergizes well with blue azulene and mucin to heal and soothe the skin,” says Seo. The mask uses domestic PDRN extracted from Korean salmon, versus other brands that may use internationally sourced PDRN not from salmon, but from other types of fish, and may contain contaminants. “We have seen other sources of PDRN versus the raw PDRN that is used in Pyderin’s products, and there is a difference in the texture, scent, and color of this active ingredient,” Seo notes.

Son adds that the sodium PDRN used in this mask is more water-soluble, making it better suited for topical applications and easier to incorporate into skin care products such as creams and sheet masks. “Its primary role in topical formulations is hydration, although it is generally not considered as biologically active as injectable forms of PDRN,” says Son.

In addition to its purity, Pyderin uses a 99 percent pure source of PDRN that is also a small enough size, at 97 kilodaltons, to penetrate the skin. “If the particle size is too large, the ingredient will merely sit on top of the skin, whereas this PDRN product will penetrate the epidermis,” Seo explains.

Son and Seo agree that the combo of pure PDRN and blue azulene can be used regularly and is suitable for all skin types and skin tones, especially after skin treatments when skin can be sensitive or irritated. “I think it would be most beneficial for patients with chronic dry or inflamed skin like rosacea,” Son notes. “I generally do not recommend facial masks for patients with conditions involving significant epidermal barrier damage, such as severe eczema or cystic acne, as these may further irritate compromised skin.”

The mask is currently sold out, but according to a Pyderin IG post, it’ll be available via Amazon in mid-to-late November, so any day now.

#6781. How The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping Showcases Effie Trinket’s Fashion Evolution

Costume designer Bina Daigeler shares the inspiration behind the fashion in the new film.

If you follow red carpet fashion, these past few weeks, it’s been impossible to ignore Elle Fanning’s method dressing madness as Effie Trinket. From toy ballerina music box headpieces by Robert Wun fit for a fairy tale to makeup compact dresses (and matching headwear) from Givenchy, the fashion story is strong. All roads lead back to her character as young Effie Trinket from The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, which is making its theater debut on November 20. Based on Suzanne Collins’s 2025 novel of the same name, it is set 24 years before The Hunger Games and 40 years after The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes. It begins the morning of the 50th Hunger Games, with a 16-year-old Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada) and Effie Trinket as a young stylist in the Capitol.

Bina Daigeler is the costume designer behind the new film, and with her team, she oversaw the creation of every single outfit from scratch, with a few helping accessories pulled from vintage specialists. She began with a mood board that focused on the aesthetic differences between the 13 districts while keeping in mind the personalities of the actors. Drusilla Sickle (Glenn Close) had a devoted board full of Alexander McQueen, John Galliano, and Vivienne Westwood, for instance. “It was really like old couture fashion,” Daigeler tells ELLE. “But pieces that, for me, would age very well, you know, that would keep up in time, because I think that is very important when you work on such a famous franchise; that you design for the future. That it’s still interesting in 10 years.”

“Working with Glenn Close, she really came to me and said, ‘I need to find a thread through all the costumes that are made for me, so that I can understand it,’” says Daigeler. “That was super important for her.” Daigeler wove everything together with “cool accessories to give her the strength,” and one very special finale look. “She wanted to have a big finale, you know, like a finale where she really feels like ‘I’m the winner. it’s nothing about Haymitch, it's all about me.’” Think: lots of red.

Daigeler also referenced Woodstock, Studio 54, the late ’60s and early ’70s. “I tried really to have a strong concept that has to do with the personality of each character,” she says. Unsurprisingly, given Effie Trinket’s predilection for fashion as a stylist, she was a big focus for Daigeler. “For example, young Effie Trinket, how did she become the Effie Trinket that Elizabeth Banks was? Here she is younger, she is fresh, she doesn’t have a lot of money or a fashion background. So it must be something that looked a little bit vintage that could be done at home, a mixture of fabrics, of textures, of color, something very young and fresh and fun.”

Early on in the film, we see Effie Trinket’s fashion as improvisational. But Daigeler intentionally showcases her ascension from fashion fan to fashion icon throughout the film. Daigeler says that Effie’s outfits all had to have fun elements. “The black kimono with the flowers and patchwork little waistcoat with a flowery blouse and all the color combinations were very strange, but it worked together,” she says. She also mentions her blue denim, Vivienne Westwood-inspired piece with huge sleeves as being the epitome of young Effie Trinket style.

“The most important thing was, for Effie, that she looks fun, but also free, because she is in the Capitol, but is still the freest and the most positive and optimistic character,” says Daigeler. “She doesn’t look like damaged and depressed and she had to come in and cheer them up and she definitely came with a solution. Our heroes somehow could trust her and they were so happy to see her. All the other characters from the Capitol, you really don’t know who you can trust.” Therefore, their aesthetic is darker while Effie is lighter and brighter.

Look out for the fashion Easter eggs throughout the film, like Effie Trinket’s butterfly crystal rings, which were just some of the pieces that Daigeler sourced vintage from one of her favorite specialists from around the world. Elsewhere, Haymitch has his own fashion revolution in a quieter way. “The way also how Haymitch wears the clothes and how they were aged and the colors, everything just comes together,” says Daigeler. “I think the audience will find, or I hope, that they find that there are things that resemble and they recognize it.”

Likewise for Caesar Flickerman (Kieran Culkin), the longtime Capitol television host, there’s an emphasis on fashionable suits. “It was very, very important that he can move because he is a very physical actor whom moves a lot, so he needed space in his suits,” says Daigeler. “Suits also had to give him power.”

And for Snow (Ralph Fiennes), too, suiting is used to convey power. “He’s very particular about fabrics, about the cut. So I went to a tailor in London, who I have known for many, many years,” she says. “Snow’s suits we developed really like a little bit, World War II military cuts, with a very strong shoulder and a very strong silhouette and these heavy fabrics, and the right colors. But then we gave him the fantasy through hand-embroidered lapels. It had also the hint to his rose that he always wears, and he has somehow always loved flowers.”

Fashion is both spectacle and a survival tool in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping. The odds are forever in fashion’s favor.

#6780. All of the Easter Eggs You Might Have Missed From Taylor Swift’s CMA Performance

And why fans are convinced a re-recorded debut album is on its way

Last night, Taylor Swift made her grand return to the Country Music Awards. Taking the award show’s stage for the first time since 2013, the singer-songwriter performed a mashup of her Single of the Year-nominated Toy Story 5 track, “I Knew It, I Knew You,” and one of her OG country classics: “Our Song.” Released in 2006 as the third single off her debut, self-titled album, the song choice was an emotional tribute to her Nashville origins—reminding the crowd that before she was touring the world as a global pop star, she was writing lyrics in math class and knocking on the doors of Music Row to try to get a record deal.

But the inclusion of “Our Song” wasn’t the only nod to her roots. Throughout the performance, which followed Swift from a backstage dressing room through the Bridgestone Arena hallways and eventually to the stage, the Easter egg professional sprinkled in many references to her early days. And the Swifties are convinced the hints might mean a re-recorded version of her debut album is on its way. (When announcing she bought her masters in May 2025, Swift revealed that she had already recorded an entire “Taylor’s Version” of her first record—though she has been quiet about it ever since.)

Ahead, we break down all the Easter eggs you might have missed from Swift’s CMAs performance.

When the performance kicked off, we saw Swift getting ready in a dressing room backstage. At first glance, the whole place was covered in dresses—with several gowns draped on the plush couch and even more hung on a clothing rack. But once we looked closely, we realized that each of the pieces came from the star’s archive. There was the BCBG halter-neck dress worn to the 2006 Academy of Country Music Awards (ACMs), the sea foam-green maxi from the “Teardrops on My Guitar” music video, and, most notably, the champagne beaded Sandi Spika gown worn to the artist’s first CMAs. It was here that Swift was awarded with the 2007 Horizon Award at the age of 17, definitively declaring it to be the “highlight of [her] senior year.”

From the dressing room, Swift walked through the arena hallways, interacting with a handful of other artists (see here)—from Eric Church to Rascal Flatts to Sugarland to Keith Urban. Every person she encountered she had either opened for, collaborated with, or had a connection to from her early days. One exception was Lainey Wilson—making people guess that the country musician might be featured on the (unconfirmed) forthcoming re-record.

#6776. Wait, Did Hailey Bieber Buy CBK’s Jacket on the RealReal?

Well, no, but there’s a lot to unpack here

Yesterday, Hailey Bieber took her obsession with ’90s minimalism one step further with a reference to the ultimate ‘90s minimalist: Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy. For a talk at the 2026 DealBook Summit here in New York, she and her stylist Dani Michelle pulled a Yohji Yamamoto look from Spring 1998, the one with a black, high-necked jacket with a line of delicate buttons paired with a white linen column skirt so famously worn by CBK in June 1998. It did what it was supposed to do: Stop us in our scrolling. But it jogged something else in the collective consciousness of fashion lovers everywhere. Hadn’t this jacket just made the rounds on our feeds?

A few weeks ago, savvy shoppers spotted the look for sale on the RealReal. Bessette-Kennedy’s reverence for Yohji Yamamoto is one that fashion’s acolytes tend to share, so it didn’t take long for the listing to go viral. The description identified its starry provenance: “From the Spring/Summer 1998 Collection; as worn by Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy in 1998 and worn by Winona Ryder for the season 5 premiere of 'Stranger Things' in 2025.”

TRR listed the jacket for $7,025. It sat on the site long enough to make the Instagram rounds, but someone snatched it up before the site had time to mark it down. The jacket sold for full price on November 6, less than two weeks before the DealBook Summit.

Could it be that Bieber scrolls for vintage Yohji on the RealReal like the rest of us? Does she have saved searches? Is she watching her obsessions with the eyes of a hawk? (Or has she outsourced this job to Michelle or someone on her team?) Bazaar reached out to the RealReal, and a spokesperson revealed that while all customer data is private, they know that Bieber’s look did not come from them. Well, it was fun to imagine. But just the possibility that this could have happened is enough to make you think.

Michelle’s post of the look does not reveal her source, though days later, she confirmed the source to be Tab Vintage. The vintage resale platform Shy mentioned in comment sections that it recently sold the same piece. Another one of the jackets, also without a proven link to CBK’s personal wardrobe, sold via the Fashion Auctioneer in March for $12,500. That’s the thing with ready-to-wear: It’s not one-of-a-kind. Bessette-Kennedy bought her jacket off the rack, which means there are multiple versions floating around out there.

If you scroll down the jacket’s sold page on the RealReal to the “Related Items” section, you’ll find multiple listings of the Cruise 2005 Chanel jacket Andy Sachs famously wore in The Devil Wears Prada, priced at roughly ten grand. “Similar style seen on Anne Hathaway in 'The Devil Wears Prada,'” the description reads. “We are seeing more and more archival pieces with incredible cultural provenance come in on consignment,” says Kristen Naiman, chief brand officer at the RealReal. “And we love helping these shards of fashion history resurface in pop culture.”

It’s become common practice for major stylists to mine specialty vintage stores for these megawatt, nostalgia-driven red carpet references. Just look at Law Roach’s Instagram account, where he documents pulling 1950s Balenciaga for Kim Kardashian from Lily et Cie, a source that specializes in museum-quality vintage. But as this thought experiment exemplifies, these transactions are becoming more normalized.

Naiman continues: “Whether you know Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy or not, there is now an entire new generation coveting this Yohji Yamamoto look on Hailey Bieber, and it has a renewed desirability because of that. And that’s the fun of being a fashion hobbyist and TRR enthusiast. You not only love the piece, you know the love story behind the piece. It’s a way to participate in fashion history. You can spend years coveting a jacket that has left an indelible mark on culture and then, one day, find it in your size.”

Which brings us back to that Yohji jacket that sold on November 6. We know Bieber wasn’t the one who purchased it, but some lucky person did, and now they own a piece of fashion history. Wonder how many other people have the same one.

#6775. 20 White Elephant Gifts Everyone Will Fight Over

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Perfect for that unnecessary work exchange.

If you’re reading this, there’s probably a holiday gift exchange on your calendar coming up. Maybe your boss organized one for the office, or maybe it’s the doing of that one friend who loves hosting so much they’d throw a party for Arbor Day. Either way, you’re probably wondering: What the heck do I bring to this thing?

There are a few rules to this game. First, there’s likely a price cap, so we’re keeping everything under $100. (If your white elephant involves luxury gifts, we’d love to be a fly on that very well-appointed wall.) Second, your gift should be something just about anyone would be excited to unwrap. That rules out clothes and shoes with specific sizes, phone cases for whatever iPhone model is currently in circulation, and franchise merch that assumes your giftee has seen every Marvel movie or follows football.

Luckily, that still leaves plenty of fun, affordable, crowd-pleasing gifts. We’ve rounded up our favorites below, so pick one, wrap it up, and take all the credit when everyone starts fighting over it.

Who doesn’t like waffles? A waffle-maker that’s easy to use and easy to store is for sure going to be a slam dunk at the gift exchange.

A cute felt ornament is a great gift that’s sort of holiday-themed, but also makes a fun gift for any occasion. It will take their Christmas tree to the next level—and even if they don’t celebrate Christmas, they can hang this little guy on a doorknob, at their desk, etc.

Jigsaw puzzles have always been a popular pastime, but people (ahem, my mom and sister) have especially taken up puzzling since the pandemic. Piecework makes some of my favorite puzzles, like this quirky peppermint-themed one.

Whether they drink coffee or tea, this leakproof tumbler will no doubt become their go-to drink vessel. Coffee for the morning commute? Check. Hot tea for an afternoon pick-me-up? Check. A glass of wine with dinner? Sure, why not. The Strova has all that covered (literally).

In these cold-weather months, hand cream is a must. This luxurious one from Homecourt smells like cedarwood smoke, sweet cardamom, cinnamon, and white leather.

Everyone wants a home that smells good. Aera’s aroma diffuser will make that happen. Yes, it’s at the upper limit price-wise, but it’s bound to be the gift everyone wants.

Just any old liquor won’t cut it, but Fords Sloe Gin is perfect for the holidays. Whoever gets this bottle will be making Sloe Gin Fizzes all winter long, and if you’re lucky, they’ll invite you over for one.

This rechargeable candle lighter is both practical and artful—it eliminates the need for matches altogether, and it looks classy next to a candle.

Yes, a candle is a classic gift exchange option, but don’t just go for your run-of-the-mill Yankee Candle; get one that will make their home smell like a winter wonderland.

Who wouldn’t want a tiny printer that can instantly print photos from your phone? This one comes with a paper roll so your giftee can get started printing as soon as they open this awesome present.

Everyone needs a hat in the winter, so why not make it a nice cashmere beanie from Quince? They won’t know you only spent $35 on it—that’s how luxurious it feels.

This set of extra virgin olive oil and balsamic vinegar from Kosterina is a great gift for anyone who cooks, whether they’re a beginner or a professional chef. And the fun holiday packaging is a plus!

Another thing everyone needs? A toiletry bag. No matter where they’re going this holiday season, they’ll need a place to pack their toothbrush, comb, razor, and whatever other bathroom essentials they use. Why not pack them in this cute pouch from Brooklinen?

Everyone is getting into peptides now. Make it easy for whoever gets this gift to start their peptide journey with this duo from Naturium.

Gloves don’t have to be boring. Verloop makes fun, colorful pairs that, yes, are touchscreen compatible. Everyone will want to get their hands on (and in) these.

A tiny record player that can actually play (tiny) records? It’s the dream of any music lover who lives in a small apartment!

Help someone get organized with a fun planner they’ll actually want to use. Burga has tons of cool patterns, so choose one you think will appeal to most people.

A tote bag is the versatile catch-all bag everyone should have at least one of. And these mini ones from Steele are so cute and will last forever.

If you’re going to go with a food gift, make it a Classic Panettone holiday cake from Eataly. Everyone will want a piece of this.

Pretty much anything from the MoMA Design Store is guaranteed to be a hit, and it will probably be the most unique gift at the party. Take these colorful crystal glasses, for example. Who wouldn’t want these in their kitchen?

#6773. Taylor Swift’s Burgundy Wine Manicure Is the Winter Nail Color to Know

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

This week at the CMA Awards, Taylor Swift’s nails were so scarlet, they were practically “maroon.” The singer sported short nails painted a shade of deep red that evoked a tall, thin wine glass, and the manicure went with all three of her outfits of the night. It’s not the first time she’s given us nail inspiration—she recently had “gold fairy dust nails” that stole the show at the 2026 Academy Museum Gala. But this new color is the perfect shade for cooler weather.

“I would describe this as a classic burgundy manicure,” says Vikki Swan, award-winning nail educator and founder of Vikki Swan Nail Academy in Los Angeles. “The deep wine-red shade creates a timeless, elegant look. It’s a great example of how a simple, solid color can make a statement without any additional nail art.”

The color also isn’t completely opaque—but it also isn’t translucent. Jin Soon Choi, manicurist and founder of Jinsoon, describes the polish as a “crelly.” “The crelly finish combines the richness of a cream [nail polish] with the softness of a jelly,” she says. “It gives this classic color a youthful, modern twist while keeping it soft and effortless.”

Swift’s manicure fits right into winter nail trends. “We’re seeing richer, darker colors come forward: black cherry, garnet, burgundy, and deep wine,” says Max Estrada, founder of eNailCouture. “They’re luxurious without relying on glitter or extra embellishment, and they work beautifully with winter fashion. It is the sort of color that gives a simple manicure presence.” Swift isn’t the only one—Jennifer Lopez and Anne Hathaway have both stepped out with similar manicures recently.

If you want to try it yourself, here’s how to re-create it. “Start with a clean manicure, shaping the nails short with softly rounded or soft-square edges and gently pushing back the cuticles,” Choi says. “Apply a base coat, followed by two thin layers of spiced red ‘crelly’ polish to build color without looking heavy. End with a top coat for an ultra-shiny finish that makes the color look lively and fresh.” And if you really want to nod to Swift, you can do it all while listening to “Red.”

#6769. The 5 Types of Coats Every Guy Should Own

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A great winter outfit starts with the right outerwear.

Years ago, I was talking to a very menswear-y buddy at a very menswear-y event, and he proposed a theory of outerwear that’s stuck with me: Your coat is your car. Sounds pretty … menswear-y, right? But bear with me here, because I think it actually makes perfect sense, so long as you’re willing to make a couple (major) concessions.

No, your coat cannot help you travel at 75 miles per hour down an interstate. You cannot fill it up with gasoline or top it up at a charging station. It contains, ideally, zero percent steel.

But if you live in a place where you spend more time walking or taking public transit than you do behind the wheel, your coat does indeed become quite car-like. You ensconce yourself in it when you move about the world. It’s the first thing people see when you arrive and the last thing they see when you depart. It shields you from the elements while simultaneously signaling to friends and strangers alike your combined senses of utility and aesthetics. So, yeah, if you squint and look at the situation sideways, your coat is your car. Even if you live somewhere that demands personal automotive transportation, your coat is your second car—unless you’re one of those kooky types who just dart from the interior of a sedan to the interior of a restaurant in nothing but a sweater—for all the reasons stated above.

The point is: Your coat is the most visible and functional part of your winter wardrobe, and you should approach the buying and wearing of said coat with all due respect for its position. Which is exactly why I created this guide. We’ll run through the key styles that every guy should know, where to get a great example (or three!) of each, and how to wear them with style all season long. You won’t find truckers or bombers here—those are jackets—but you will find parkas, topcoats, trenches, and more. All of them will serve you well until the spring thaw, so let’s get into it.

If you’ve been keeping up with Esquire style guides, you’ll know that we’ve covered a lot of ground over the past few years, breaking down everything from the shoe styles you should have in your rotation to the winter essentials you shouldn’t go without. One story that really resonated with readers was about sweaters. In it, we talked about the fact that you can score very good knitwear from a huge range of lifestyle brands these days. We also mentioned the prudence of keeping a few category specialists on your radar. The same advice holds true for coats.

On the lifestyle side, the brands will look familiar to longtime readers. You’ve got Todd Synder and Buck Mason. J. Press and J. Crew. Ralph Lauren and Drake’s and Alpha Industries and Madewell and Billy Reid and Filson ... the list goes on. You’ve also got fashion behemoths like Burberry, which famously started out with the trench coat. And then you’ve got brands that may not just do outerwear but are truly steeped in it. That could be a heritage player like Mackintosh or Schott or Gloverall, a parka expert like Canada Goose, or an upstart label like Wythe, which makes a wonderful range of products but has a cult following for its exceptional overcoats.

As we’ve warned before: This list is not exhaustive. But it’s a good place to start. Now, on to the coats themselves.

This is the dressiest of the bunch, though it wasn’t always. If you’re a real traditionalist, you might have a velvet-collared Chesterfield in your closet to wear with your fanciest togs. Most of us, though, don’t really need something that formal. The topcoat is similar to and derives from the Chesterfield, but it’s less stuffy.

Still, it looks plenty dressed up to modern eyes. It’ll have lapels, which can be notch or peak (for a little extra personality), and can be single- or double-breasted. If you suit up with any regularity, that means it’s likely the most essential for your wardrobe. Look for one made of wool or a wool blend; this is not the time to overspend on synthetics.

Colors are usually pretty conservative—think camel, navy, gray, and black. That might seem limiting, but remember that this thing should be able to play nicely with your whole wardrobe, so opting for a more subdued hue is usually the right move. As for length: Some folks will swear something that hits above the knee looks more modern. We just think it looks off. Go for at or below the knee.

(Oh, and if you’re wondering if a classic polo coat really counts as a topcoat as opposed to an overcoat, well, you might be right to question us. Technically. But considering its cultural associations—rich New Englanders, Wall Street power brokers—we’re giving it a home here.)

Eschewed by many as a bit too fusty and old-fashioned as recently as a decade ago, the raglan overcoat has come roaring back into the spotlight in recent years. We enthusiastically endorse its return.

The style is defined by its raglan shoulder construction, in which a diagonal seam runs from around the collarbone to the underarm, allowing for an uninterrupted panel over the top of the shoulder. Don’t worry too much about the technical details, though. The main thing to remember is that this shape is more relaxed and tends to layer over other garments (a chunky sweater, a sport coat) more easily and comfortably than a traditional “set in” shoulder seam.

The raglan overcoat also tends to be cut wide and long, so you can bundle up underneath it and never worry about chilly knees. It leans more casual than a topcoat, for sure, but you can still wear it with a suit. And it lends polish to something as simple as jeans and a sweatshirt. One of our favorite subgenres of raglan overcoat is the balmacaan, which features a Prussian collar (which looks like a shirt collar instead of a lapel), a “fly front” with a placket that covers the buttons, and buttoned fasteners at the wrists so you can cinch up to keep out the cold.

Wythe Raglan Overcoat in Diamond Ombre Tweed

If you’re into the warm and wooly vibes of the two styles above but simply can’t stand a long coat, the peacoat is for you. Double-breasted and hip length, it’s a shorter—but no less capable—take on the woolen overcoat that dates back about three centuries.

Originally created for sailors of European (and, eventually, American) navies, the peacoat is tough enough to defend from open-ocean winds and sea spray, which means it’s definitely up to the task of keeping you toasty on your way to work. There are a lot of versions out there these days that are done up in frankly wimpy wool blends that might feel nice at first but don’t do the job.

Go for a heavyweight wool like melton, but make sure there’s enough room to layer up underneath it—and keep an eye on the lapels. They should be big. Big enough that you can pop that collar and shield yourself from an incoming gust without feeling even a tickle of wind touch your neck. Plus, it looks cool.

J. Crew Dock Peacoat in Wool Blend with PrimaLoft

Thanks to a quirky little phenomenon we like to call “global climate change,” there’s a very good chance that some of the winter days ahead will feel a lot more like fall or spring. On those days—especially if it’s raining—you should reach for a trench coat or a mac. The two styles aren’t synonymous, but they’re related in both vibe and history, so we’re coupling them here.

Maximalists will prefer the trench, with its storm flap and epaulettes and double-breasted closure. Minimalists will reach for the mac, with its clean lines and streamlined shape. It’s really a matter of preference, because both fulfill the same roles in your outerwear lineup.

They’ll keep you warmish, especially when combined with additional layers. They’ll help block the wind when it kicks up. And although we can’t guarantee you’ll walk away dry if you stand around for an hour during a deluge, they’ll fend off a shower—or a sprint through a thunderstorm—quite capably.

Burberry Long Kensington Heritage Trench Coat

If it’s really fucking frigid, you need a parka. Make sure it’s properly insulated, whether with a synthetic fill or down. And unless you like freezing your literal ass off, get one that’s hip length or longer. You could get something technical and bright that looks like it just stepped off the ski slopes, but we’re more inclined toward rugged-looking styles that take their cues from the military and outdoor exploration.

The N-3B, or “snorkel parka,” is a burly beast that looks absolutely fantastic with a casual outfit and, if you ask us, can be fun with a suit too. (Though if you’re worried about raising eyebrows or breaking a dress code, maybe opt for a topcoat instead.) Something like Canada Goose’s Langdon parka is a little more pared back in looks but overdelivers on warmth, meaning you’ll be cozy in even the worst weather. The trick is finding one that balances form and function so that it looks as though you intended to wear it on the way to the bar—not like you wandered off some snowcapped mountain and forgot to change.

Like the peacoat, the duffle coat is a part of maritime history. It was adopted by the British Royal Navy in the late 19th century and widely issued to British troops during World War II. Thanks to the sheer number produced and postwar surplus, it quickly became a civilian staple and eventually hopped across the pond to become a fixture of the American preppy uniform. Made of thick wool and equipped with a big hood, it’s very warm. And thanks to the large toggle closures, you don’t even need to take your gloves off to fasten it. Is it a little odd? Perhaps. But that’s what makes it so charming.

#6765. Forget Stainless Steel Appliances: This Designer-Approved Style Is the New Kitchen Status Symbol

Turn your hardest-working appliance into a design moment.

The kitchen has evolved from a hardworking cook space into a multifunctional room for lounging, gathering, and entertaining. So naturally, appliances are changing, too: They need to be as beautiful as they are functional.

Many associate appliances with stainless steel—it has been the go-to choice in kitchens for decades. The material even took center stage in our 1939 Washable House, where it made classic worktables and countertops easier to clean. But today’s design is making room for more colorful choices.

The kitchen designers behind our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston embraced that shift, showing how the room’s most essential appliance—the range—can balance utility with style.

For the kitchen’s centerpiece, designer Susan Ford of Bennett Hofford chose a La Cornue CornuFé range in Quintessential Teal. White-veined marble, creamy cabinetry, and golden hardware frame the deep blue hue, which continues across the lower cabinets to anchor the design.

The color also plays beautifully against the plush terra-cotta stools lining the island. Designer Chloe Legras of Boxwood Avenue, who designed the adjacent great room, selected the stools and other kitchen accents.

Beyond its color, the range features millwork in mixed metals, giving the traditional detail a futuristic edge and adding character to the small but bespoke space. A Parisian-style range hood with gold details completes the look.

If you’re looking to elevate your kitchen, take a cue from our Whole Home. This design is bold enough to impress the design-obsessed and practical enough for the bustle of everyday life.

#6759. Katy Perry and Justin Trudeau Were Spotted in 3 Countries in Just 1 Week

Days after their West Hollywood gala date, the couple traveled to Europe.

Katy Perry and Justin Trudeau had a rare red carpet date in West Hollywood last weekend, but now they’re far from California. The couple has spent the week in Europe, visiting three different countries.

Yesterday, they were photographed outside of the Ritz Paris dressed for late fall. Perry wore a tan suede jacket with black pants, while Trudeau opted for a navy and gray ensemble.

They were also seen earlier that day in Italy, leaving the Four Seasons hotel in Milan.

Two days before that, the former Canadian prime minister and the singer were in Spain, where they attended Atresmedia’s Metafuturo conference on November 17. Trudeau was one of the speakers.

Perry has posted her own glimpses of their relationship on Instagram here and there, though she hasn’t posted any photos from their Europe trip yet.

Earlier this month, she revealed they spent her 42nd birthday together on a different vacation. “Climbed to level 42 this week!” she wrote. “Was feeling indifferent about the inbetweeness of age 42… Then it was explained to me that 42 is actually the answer to everything. Though I still don’t understand, I’m ok with turning 42 as long as I can turn 42 with you ♥️ back to living…”

The singer described Trudeau as the love of her life in a September interview with People. She reflected on the past year, saying, “I learned how to step back into my purpose, and I got the greatest gift from this [Lifetimes] Tour: I got to meet the love of my life.”

Before dating Trudeau, Perry was manifesting their love story. “I always wanted this epic legendary love, and I was talking to my therapist about it: Everything in my life is epic, but this is kind of the missing puzzle piece—then it showed up,” she said.

She praised her boyfriend’s support of her music career, saying, “He knows the words, he’s supportive, and he’s in awe; it’s super healing. He is really heart-centered, and I think that we align in that way. I’m so grateful.”

#6758. The Watches of Grand Theft Auto 6 (and Their Real-Life Inspiration)

From Rolex Day-Dates to Casio digitals, a guide to the timepieces of Vice City.

For one glorious moment, the world appears to be united. Grand Theft Auto VI has finally arrived, to near-universal acclaim, with millions of people abandoning work, school, and presumably all their other responsibilities to explore the streets of Vice City. In these divided times, it's enough to warm the cockles of your heart.

And amid all the fast cars, guns, nightclubs, and criminal capers, there's something else we pay attention to here at Esquire HQ: the watches!

Rockstar Games' latest blockbuster reportedly features 34 "collectible" luxury watches scattered throughout its fictional version of Florida. But that's only part of the story. GTA VI also has its own watch brands, although some of the designs look ... suspiciously familiar.

There's Crowex (a dead ringer for Rolex), Takio (distinctly Casio-adjacent), and Covgari, whose watches owe more to Audemars Piguet than the Italian-o jeweller its name suggests.

From gold Day-Dates to pocket-money Casio digitals, here are some of our favorite real-world watches that appear to have inspired the game's timepieces.

The gold Crowex is a near dead ringer for the Rolex Day-Date 40 (ref. 228238). The fluted bezel, black dial, and day window at 12 o'clock are all present and correct, although the bracelet differs somewhat from Rolex's iconic President. The Day-Date celebrates its 70th anniversary this year. Evidently, it remains the universal symbol of having made it, even in the virtual world.

The silver chronograph worn by a character overlooking Vice City has more than a passing resemblance to the mighty Daytona. The round case, contrasting sub-dials, and metal bracelet suggest that famous racing chronograph. As we know, the Daytona is one of the world's most sought-after sports watches, making it a natural fit for GTA's world of expensive cars, conspicuous consumption, and bling.

Brian Heder, a veteran drug smuggler, wears a metal sports watch that certainly seems to recall Rolex's Sub. There's the substantial case, dark dial, and the bracelet. The Submariner has been a fixture of luxury watchmaking since 1953, and its more ostentatious steel-and-gold versions would be right at home on the streets of Vice City.

Jason Duval, one of GTA VI's two protagonists, wears a chunky digital watch resembling something from Casio's Pro Trek range. The large circular display, prominent buttons and rugged case certainly suggest its something from the range of the Japanese juggernaut. A more practical choice than the gold watches favored by some of his associates.

Not everyone in Vice City is wearing a gold Rollie lookalike. The Takio F-BLAST is an unmistakable nod to Casio's inexpensive digital classics, particularly the bestselling F-91W. It has the familiar rectangular display, black dial surround, and simple, functional design. There's even a more colorful version, with a red case and two-tone metal bracelet, spotted in the game's Hamlet Thrift store.

As spotted by watch Instagram account @geesgshock.

#6754. A Landmark Exhibition Honors Sheroanawe Hakihiiwe’s Yanomami Roots

Presented with Fondation Cartier at ICA Miami, the show spans 25 years of the artist’s work.

It was no small journey for Sheroanawe Hakihiiwe to reach the Institute of Contemporary Art Miami, where a landmark exhibition marks the Venezuelan artist’s first solo museum presentation in the United States. A member of the Yanomami tribe, Hakihiiwe was raised in the remote Poripori community along the upper Orinoco River, where he still lives. It requires a flight, eight days via boat, and three days via canoe just to reach Caracas, the capital of Venezuela. Getting there takes patience; so does the work, which begins with looking closely at the forest around him.

At a preview of the exhibition earlier this week, the soft-spoken Hakihiiwe discussed what he hopes his oeuvre can carry forward. “My work speaks of my ancestors and I would like it to speak of the future too,” he said in Spanish. “Today our rivers, our lands, what we plant and what we eat are threatened by illegal mining, pollution, and climate change.”

The expansive exhibition, organized by ICA Miami and the Fondation Cartier pour l’art contemporain, brings together more than 100 works across three decades. (Hakihiiwe’s first solo U.S. survey is also the Fondation Cartier’s first exhibition to open stateside; previous U.S. presentations followed European debuts.) Designed by Mauricio Rocha’s Taller de Arquitectura, the galleries take cues from the traditional Yanomami shapono, or communal house, with dim, filtered light and natural pigments.

The show opens with Hakihiiwe’s early works from the 1990s: delicate drawings on handmade paper that trace animal tracks in the forest, acting as a visual language that continues to shape his practice. Beginning here was intentional, says Michela Alessandrini, curator at the Fondation Cartier. The early works lay the groundwork for what follows.

“We wanted to share the evolution of his visual language,” says Alessandrini, who collaborated with ICA Miami’s curator Gean Moreno for over one year to survey Hakihiiwe’s work for the show. “Across the galleries, you witness his power of observation, from the forms and signs he discovers in the forest to the delicate relationship between all living things that share it.”

From there, the installation turns to the Amazonian ecosystem, from studies of individual plants to monumental works shaped by Hakihiiwe’s shamanic practice. In “Puu motho shipe” (Stinging Vines), an acrylic-on-paper work, he records a plant species through winding vines set against a graphic yellow backdrop. Like much of the artist’s practice, it preserves traditional ecological knowledge as the rainforest faces mounting threats. “Ushuemi wake u” (Red Bird Water), meanwhile, comes from a body of work shaped by the artist’s recent shamanic training.

“It introduced a way of thinking about art, nature, and other ways in the world that continues to guide our mission today,” Alessandrini said of Hakihiiwe’s work, which the Fondation Cartier first showcased in its 2022 exhibition “Les Vivants”. “What has always resonated with us is the perspective his work offers. It advises to look beyond our own familial ways of seeing and to recognize a world in which humans, animals, and plants are all interconnected.”

Hakihiiwe makes many of his creations in Caracas, where he can work away from Poripori’s humidity and insects. His art may travel, but its focus stays close to home: his community and the land around it. Both anthropological and deeply personal, his pieces offer a view from within a community still fighting to protect its land and way of life.

“We shamans cannot heal the water, the earth, the air,” Hakihiiwe said to close his remarks. “All the shamans are worried. We know that the time is coming, and that if we don’t change, we will disappear.” He continued, “I hope that through my work people can discover the beauty of the forest, but also its importance and how it can raise awareness about this future we see with such concern.”

Sheroanawe Hakihiiwe is on view at the ICA Miami from November 19 to March 28, 2027.

#6750. We Know How Sunrise on the Reaping Ends. That Doesn’t Change Its Impact.

Suzanne Collins’ Hunger Games prequel ends with devastation—and a reminder of what to protect.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Spoilers below for The Hunger Games books, including Sunrise on the Reaping.

Re-reading the original The Hunger Games series in preparation for this review, I started experiencing nightmares. Despite my generally overactive imagination, I would not call this normal. I read a lot of fiction, much of it politically prescient if not outright dystopian; if everything I consumed brought on such sleep paralysis demons, I’d call a doctor. But I shouldn’t be surprised that, now, Suzanne Collins’s young-adult trilogy began seeping into my subconscious, 15 years after I’d first turned its final page—especially considering that it’s now the latest in her series to become a feature film.

The lead protagonist in Collins’s trilogy, Katniss Everdeen, spends the entire series battling such nightmares as the death toll escalates around her. After volunteering as a tribute in the Capitol’s annual Hunger Games, pitting children against each other in a fight to the death, her already difficult life in District 12 becomes outright dangerous. She inadvertently sparks a rebellion. She leads that rebellion. She loses countless loved ones. Those of us who read and adored the series know how it ends, but even those unfamiliar with Katniss’s struggle can take an educated guess. Protest rarely goes unpunished.

I can’t claim to have experienced anywhere near the trauma Katniss undergoes throughout Collins’s trilogy, but I have aged considerably since I first read those books, and what struck me most as I re-encountered familiar passages was how present they still felt. I’d expected some nostalgia whilst leafing through The Hunger Games, Catching Fire, and Mockingjay, and the same when I’d pick up Collins’s latest installment in the franchise, the prequel novel Sunrise on the Reaping. I had not expected to feel so disturbed. Or so furious.

This is the crowning achievement of Sunrise on the Reaping, which succeeds in the near-impossible task of making an well-trodden story feel as intimate and visceral as its predecessors. Reaping is the fifth Hunger Games novel and the second prequel installment after The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes, which chronicled the youth and radicalization of the future president of Panem, Coriolanus Snow. (Like the original trilogy, Songbirds was adapted into a hugely popular Lionsgate film in 2023, and Reaping’s own adaptation landed in 2026.) Reaping surges forward a handful of years, set nearly a quarter of a century before Katniss’s own Hunger Games.

The story centers on Haymitch Abernathy, a beloved figure in The Hunger Games trilogy, in which he plays the role of the embittered if endearing drunk whose previous Hunger Games victory sets him up as mentor to Katniss and her District 12 partner, Peeta, for their own. In Reaping, Katniss and Peeta have yet to be born, but a young Haymitch knows their parents well. Here, Collins does a remarkable job of delivering what fans would call devastating “lore drops,” but she does so, for the most part, without resorting to cheesy or unnecessary Easter eggs. These minor characters—including Katniss’s parents, Burdock Everdeen and Asterid March—serve a clear purpose: to ground Haymitch in a familiar-but-reoriented District 12, where rebellion is yearned for but rarely acted upon.

One of the few characters willing to risk their wellbeing in pursuit of justice is Lenore Dove, Haymitch’s girlfriend and a descendant of the Covey, a group of former nomads whom readers will recognize from Songbirds. Her conviction, her music, and her love (“like all-fire,” she says, one of several Covey expressions employed throughout the book) steers Haymitch to her aid at the annual reaping ceremony, during which child tributes are selected for the Hunger Games. This year is a Quarter Quell, and the Capitol will demand twice as many boy and girl tributes as in an average year. After an unexpected execution takes place in the District 12 square, Haymitch finds himself rounded up with three other tributes and sent to spar for his life in the Gamemakers’ brutal arena.

This is, of course, not a spoiler: Haymitch wins. Collins first described Haymitch’s victory way back in 2009 with the release of Catching Fire, during which Katniss and Peeta watch a recap video of Haymitch’s Quarter Quell. Through Katniss’s eyes, the young Haymitch comes off as “snarky. Arrogant. Indifferent.” But what Katniss doesn’t know is that the footage she’s watching has been heavily edited to reorder the timeline of events in the arena, and to remove Haymitch’s repeated efforts to undermine the Capitol. When he wins the games, he does so not as a ruthless survivor, but as a rebel attempting to break the arena itself. And when he returns home as a reluctant victor, he pays for his revolution a hundredfold.

We already knew from Catching Fire that, mere weeks after the Quell, President Snow orders Haymitch’s mother, brother, and girlfriend to be killed. But it’s something else entirely to experience those deaths on the page. In Sunrise on the Reaping, this mother, brother, and girlfriend are no longer nameless extensions of Haymitch himself; they are characters we’ve come to know (named Willamae, Sid, and Lenore Dove), whose deaths we dread despite their inevitability. Our anticipation does not ultimately erase our pain, just as pre-grieving an ailing loved one does not ultimately erase their loss. Collins understands this well, and she uses our emotions to target our instincts for both ignorance and apathy.

In the end, Haymitch’s own assumed apathy cannot hold. He drives away his friends and few remaining neighbors, turning to “sleep syrup” and rotgut liquor as he’s tormented by violent memories and recurring echoes of “The Raven” by Edgar Allen Poe, which, in the world of Panem, has been passed down through the generations to become Lenore Dove’s “name poem.” In Poe’s famous 1845 poem, an unnamed narrator mourns the death of his lover, his “lost Lenore.” He is caught between two dueling desires: the temptation to forget Lenore and the desperation to remember her. His late-night visitor, a raven who only repeats the word “nevermore,” insists upon remembrance, driving the narrator to conclude that only madness awaits him.

Haymitch makes a similar conclusion—for the time being, anyway—as he wakes half-drunk from nightmares but stays alive, Lenore Dove’s final wish having “condemned [Haymitch] to life.” (She begs that he not let another sun rise on a reaping day, thus giving the book its title.) And though the word-for-word replication of “The Raven” in Sunrise on the Reaping is one of Collins’s few missteps—later eliminated by the movie adaptation—drawing the reader away from Haymitch’s world, the poem nevertheless carries a deeply poignant message.

Thematically, much of Sunrise on the Reaping is about propaganda, and specifically how truth is manipulated to trigger an audience’s emotional response. The Capitol Gamemakers edit footage of Haymitch to confirm what Capitol residents already want to believe about him—that he is a charming but ultimately dangerous rascal—and to ignore what does not suit their “logic.” As Plutarch Heavensbee (another character from the original trilogy) tells Haymitch and his allies, “People have an emotional response to something, then they come up with an argument for why it logically makes sense.” That explains the so-called “necessity” of the Hunger Games themselves: By convincing the Capitol residents to fear and deride the Districts, and the Districts to fear and covet the Capitol, the Capitol citizens are able to stomach the Hunger Games as “for the greater good”—and the Districts ultimately succumb to the resulting authoritarianism. History, as they say, is written by the victors.

But while propaganda is indeed a form of storytelling, there is a chasm of difference between twisting stories with malicious intent and preserving them for posterity. Collins’s use of “The Raven,” in addition to numerous other songs and poems popular amongst the Covey, is a testament to that fact. That a poem from 1845 would exist, in its entirety, numerous centuries in the future—having survived the collapse of North American society and the building of a totalitarian government bent on killing children—is something of a wondrous idea to behold. It is also a nod to the strength and resilience of the Covey themselves, who have taken care to maintain these stories despite the Capitol’s relentless and well-documented censorship.

As in Mockingjay and Songbirds, Sunrise on the Reaping ends with an epilogue. This final chapter leaps decades into the future, taking place in the years after Mockingjay and its siege on the Capitol. When Katniss and Peeta choose to compile a post-war “memorial book,” Collins writes that Haymitch is not initially interested in contributing. “What use?” she writes, from his perspective. “What point? To relive all the loss. But when Burdock’s page came up, I had to mention him showing me [Lenore’s] grave. And I felt compelled to tell them about Maysilee Donner, former owner of the mockingjay pin. And how Sid loved the stars. Before I knew it, they all came tumbling out: family, tributes, friends, comrades in arms, everybody, even my love. I finally told our story.”

Haymitch realizes that forgetting was never really an option. It wasn’t an option for the narrator of “The Raven,” nor is it an option for him. Choosing to forget would be capitulating to a system that manipulates such forgetfulness for power-hungry aims. Intentional amnesia, learned apathy—both would be an abdication of love.

15 years after Mockingjay was released, I needn’t wonder why the series brought on refreshed nightmares. Authoritarianism is not a fictional threat. It has not been dreamed up for our entertainment or titillation. It is here and present and bloodthirsty. As with the entirety of The Hunger Games saga, Sunrise on the Reaping keeps this truth close to its heart, which is why—even for a book for “young readers”—its story is so difficult to absorb.

Yet Collins makes it clear that the war against injustice did not start or end with Katniss Everdeen. Before her was Haymitch Abernathy. Before him was Lucy Gray Baird, of The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes. She learned her political anthems from the Covey, who learned them from their ancestors of yore. Stories, passed down through generations—each of them sowed the seeds of revolution that are sparked during Haymitch’s Hunger Games and ignited during Katniss’s. Dystopian literature like Sunrise on the Reaping is not a manual for effective protest, nor a crystal ball predicting the future. It is fiction and should be treated as such. But it can serve an essential purpose, one that Collins has not lost sight of in her decades of Hunger Games books: Fiction keeps close what we might otherwise deign to forget. It reminds us of what we stand to lose. And, at its best, it stirs us to pick up the fight.

#6749. Do You Always Have to Sacrifice Passion for Emotional Intimacy?

Do you remember the beginning of your relationship with your partner? When you would be turned on by just the thought of your fingers sharing space with theirs? When you’d bust into the apartment movie-style, tossing aside layers of clothes because you just couldn’t wait to get to skin?

Now, your relationship looks a little different. There are no mental gymnastics before grabbing your partner’s hand and you don’t mind putting your clothes away nicely before jumping into bed. It’s a comfortable, cozy phase—one that you worked hard and long to get to.

But sometimes, you might wish you could have both.

We’ve collectively accepted that a long-term relationship somehow means the end of passion. You spend months or even years learning everything you can about your partner. You’ve gained trust, a strong foundation, and yet, the passion seems to have disappeared with the uncertainty.

“The comfort you worked so hard to build may be what’s subtly killing your desire for your partner,” certified sex therapist Dr. Mindy DeSeta, PhD tells Cosmopolitan.

She explains that over time, we unknowingly become complacent about romancing our partner. All of the exciting unknowns of the beginning of a relationship (Do they want to kiss you? Do they want to come home with you?) eventually fade away.

“You may be intimately familiar with your partner's moves after a number of years, and that's a beautiful thing,” Dr. DeSeta says. “But repetition can dull the excitement of physical intimacy.”

But contrary to what we’ve all learned to accept, this isn’t an inevitable diagnosis for your long-term relationship. It is possible to have both emotional intimacy and sexual passion. You just have to work for it.

“Your sex life will suffer if you do not actively continue to learn your partner’s desires,” Dr. DeSeta says.

You might think that you know everything there is to know already about your partner. But as we get older, our desires change—even if our partner doesn’t.

“Your pleasure is constantly evolving and if you want to continue to have a thriving and exciting sex life, a couple must be able to talk about most recent desires,” she says. “What turned you on at 25 may not still turn you on at 40.”

This is why communicating your desires and feelings to your partner is so important. Sure, you and your partner may be pros at working through disagreements and emotionally supporting each other through life’s changes—but does that energy translate into your sex life? Do you share your curiosities? Do you hold space for them telling you they’d like to try something different? If you don’t, it might be time to make a change.

First, in order to feel sexy with your partner, you need to feel sexy yourself. Do something every day that makes you feel sexy and confident,” Dr. DeSeta says. “The foundation of a great sex life starts with each person having a thriving libido.”

Then try to initiate sexy conversation with your partner. Dr. DeSeta suggests planning something that feels tantalizing without the expectation of physical intimacy to follow. Reach each other erotica, talk about new fantasies you’ve had, discuss a trendy sex topic in media.

“Many long-term couples need a prompt to get the sexy conversations started,” she says. They also need reminders to touch each other more.

“Long term couples are notorious for forgetting physical affection,” she says. Dr. DeSeta emphasizes small touches every day. This can look like caressing their back as you pass them in the kitchen or holding hands while you watch your favorite show on the couch. Instead of maneuvering around each other, lean into the touch. It signals to your partner that you see them and want them throughout the day. And just the feeling of being wanted can ignite some of that passion from the early days of your relationship.

Most importantly, you should date your partner every day. Dr. DeSeta recommends “Daily Dates” to couples she sees in therapy. This is when you set aside 20 minutes everyday to make your partner a priority.

“All too often long-term couples put their relationship second, so this tune up is always needed,” she says. “During these 20 minutes the phones are silenced, logistical conversations are off the table, and the time is dedicated to emotionally connecting with their partner.”

By doing this, she explains that you slow down the daily hustle and really learn how to reconnect with your partner. You might even learn a few new things about them in the process.

“If we do not make the intentional effort to keep the passion alive, then you are at risk of falling into autopilot,” Dr.Deseta says. We often think passion and emotional intimacy are at odds with each other, but that’s because we think it’s acceptable to simply fall into our relationship without making an active effort to keep our relationship exciting. And no, you don’t have to be constantly ripping each other’s clothes off or feel nervous in order to have a passionate relationship. But you should aim to feel the same deep desire and attraction to your partner that you did when you first started off—if not more.

#6746. “It Was Never About Fashion”: Emily in Paris’s Costume Designer Reveals the Method to Her Madness

“Emily in Paris: Fashion Without Rules,” a new show at SCAD Fashion in Atlanta, is devoted to the series’s unique style

Emily in Paris premiered when audiences needed it most. The Netflix original, created by Sex and the City producer Darren Star, offered an escapist fantasy: a young girl trying to find love and success in Paris in October 2020. The show functioned as a reprieve from the pandemic, a dreamy alternative in which we could vicariously live through Emily (Lily Collins) as she gallivanted around the City of Light. It was and still is delectably digestible television.

The love for Emily in Paris was anything but unanimous. Critics wasted no time tearing the show apart, with some targeting its depictions of French culture or claiming the script lacked the bite of Star’s previous television shows. The criticism extended to Emily’s idiosyncratic wardrobe, with some writing that the clothing leaned hard on American stereotypes of French fashion or looked cartoonish, with clashing patterns and bright colors. Costume designer Marylin Fitoussi took these criticisms head-on and doubled down. Emily’s outfits weren’t meant to be couture or designer; they were meant to dress a girl finding her sense of self, expressing her desire to explore the unknown.

For six seasons, Fitoussi curated a wardrobe fit for Emily, a bright-eyed young woman unafraid to try something new, to break the rules. This mirrors Fitoussi’s personal philosophy on clothing: that fashion is meant to break rules, to push boundaries, to provide a space of unrelenting experimentation. Ahead of the sixth season, SCAD FASH Museum of Fashion + Film compiles six seasons’ worth of outfits in “Emily in Paris: Fashion Without Rules,” on view through March 14, 2027. The exhibition lives up to its name; for the designer, clothing is a mode of expression that lets anyone feel free, wherever they are. It offers a character study, tracing Emily’s evolution from a doe-eyed expat to a well-versed Parisian resident. (And worry not, there are also looks from Emily’s chic boss Sylvie—played with devastating elegance by Philippine Leroy-Beaulieu.)

Ahead of season 6, Bazaar sat down with Fitoussi at SCAD Fashion in Atlanta to discuss working with Lily Collins, her swan song for Emily in Paris, and how everyone has the potential for creativity.

Marylin Fitoussi: I don’t recall a contemporary TV show being in the spotlight in this way. Generally, when you think of an exhibition, you think of period pieces or couture pieces. The show is really part of pop culture now!

MF: I was surprised when fashion magazines [criticized the show] at the beginning. I’m not a fashion designer, [but] I love fashion and I love clothes. This show was not about fashion at the beginning. [Emily] is a bubbly character with a very strong personality from Chicago; she couldn’t have come dressed in Comme des Garçons or in Yamamoto. I needed to have this cultural shock. She’s a fish-out-of-water character, and we needed something to catch the audience’s eyes. When you want to show people that you love the culture but you can’t express it because you don’t know the codes, you don’t speak the language, the only way you find it is through clothes. It was never about “fashion.” Sometimes, it’s about creativity, individuality, making something not trendy at all. The audience got that, and fashion magazines didn’t get it, so they killed me.

MF: Many times, I’ve asked myself, “Why does this little gingham suit have so much influence?” It’s quite simple. It was the most duplicated, copied, reposted, fan-arted, and used as a Halloween costume. It touched people because it was for them. The French can have their own version of it, their own concept, but for the entire world, it was this one little girly, timeless outfit with the red beret, the red lips, the red soul.

MF: She read the bad press after the release of season 1, and we had a talk. I said they hated it in France; they find it hideous. [I asked her] “Do you want me to change [it]?” She was smiling and said, “No, we can’t, and we won’t please everybody. We need to keep this strong DNA. We need to continue to have this unexpected silhouette because it’s what catches the audience’s eye.” It’s a guilty pleasure. We can say, I love it.

It opens a way for the audience to see fashion in a different way, where you can mix in an awkward way and free people from this law where you shouldn’t [do that]. Coco Chanel used to say, “Before leaving a room, look at yourself in the mirror and take something out.” I always say, “Put something more on.”

MF: I wanted to present a more elevated version, more mature, more evolved, but still true to what she was and what she is, with an eclectic combination and a little bit awkward. She knows the French rules, but as I do, she finds French fashion so boring. In general, I find that fashion is less and less interesting. Why are fashion and celebrity stylists sourcing more and more from archives? What it’s showing is that now everything is the same. Everything is boring, and I understand why. They need to sell the show! We don’t have to sell anything. We have to sell dreams, illusions, craziness, boldness. People watch the show in groups. Some people say, “I love it,” and some say, “I hate it,” and they have a dialogue and fight and have a good [time]. We love that.

MF: A young designer wants to be famous right away. Fame doesn’t happen, and it doesn’t matter. You need to be happy with what you’re doing. With costume design versus fashion design, you can be much freer. You don’t have anything to sell, and you don’t have to design the “It bag.” If your show is successful, it’s a win-win for everybody. To see that, you need to be true to your vision. You need to be true to your gut feeling, to your vision, to your DNA. Talent is not enough; you need to be at the right place at the right time. Being discovered by Darren Star … he changed my life. At some point, you find the right project for you.

You need to work a lot to take care of every detail. You need to be strong and push the boundaries. You need to try to show a different way to see costumes and clothes, to see that everything is possible…. Style begins when fear ends. You don’t want to please everybody, and I’m always attracted to people who dress in a different way, and I’m never judgmental. To create something is to expose yourself. It’s very vulnerable. It’s brave.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6745. Nina Garcia’s Insider Guide to Los Angeles

You’re reading Nina Garcia’s Insider Guide newsletter, exclusively available on ELLE.com to All Access members. This edition was originally published in November 2026. Sign up to receive the next edition here.

There isn’t just one way to experience Los Angeles, a place where glitz and glamour sit next to the natural beauty of California mountains and beaches, and the possibility of a movie star being around every corner brings a thrill to every day. As the iconic American designer Isaac Mizrahi once said, “I like to think glamour was invented in Hollywood,” and I tend to agree. There’s a thread of style in every aspect of life.

I recently headed to Los Angeles for ELLE’s Women in Hollywood event, one of our favorite nights of the year, and had a chance to revisit all this city has to offer. Our seminal Women in Hollywood issue honors the year’s most talented actresses, and each fall, in celebration of the issue, we host a star-studded party at the Four Seasons Los Angeles in Beverly Hills. This year, I am bringing you along for the ride. There is something for everyone, including a world-class dining scene that spans from elite Korean restaurants to a universe of Mexican food. (Many say Guelaguetza has the best Oaxacan menu in the country.) If you’re anything like me, you might spend a day checking out the innovative architecture and vibrant gallery scene or opt for a visit to Rodeo Drive for a sun-soaked day of shopping.

For this month’s Insider Guide, I deliberated with true Angelenos, my friends Heidi Klum, interior designer Michael Smith, and fashion designer Sarah Staudinger, about the places they love the most in this classic West Coast destination.

Any time of year. Summer can be overly warm, and the perfect wintertime weather makes it an ideal getaway for East Coasters, but every season has something to offer. With LAX’s proximity to the city center, Los Angeles makes for an easy trip. I recommend renting a car to be able to visit all the beaches and experience the joy of driving down the Pacific Coast Highway, but Uber works just as well. Just be wary of the infamous Los Angeles traffic.

Want to chat about my Insider Guide to Los Angeles? Send me a note at ninagarciainsiderguide@hearst.com. I’ll see you next month. Until then, follow me at @ninagarcia on Instagram for real-time fashion and travel updates—and don’t forget to check out @elleusa too.

#6743. Why Don’t You … Take Fashion Inspiration from the World’s Great Performers?

Diana Vreeland had a singular take on not only fashion but life. Her eccentricity has become folklore and was best exemplified in an illustrated column of irreverent advice for chic living she called “Why Don’t You?” Resurrected and reimagined for modern life, the column, now written by Bazaar’s executive digital director, Lynette Nylander, will dispense tips on how to live stylishly, decadently, and daringly.

Here’s a thought: Don’t get dressed. Mount a production. Put on a show. Go on tour. And if the reviews are lukewarm, don’t change the costume; blame the critic! For the performance issue, Lynette Nylander takes cues from the stage’s most stylish to arm you with tips for stepping out.

that the “effortless” look took 40 minutes. The “I just threw it on” leather jacket, the smudged eye makeup, the shirt that looks slept in: All of it is stagecraft. Miley Cyrus’s new rock-star look personifies this. She’s not someone to shy away from an “era,” and this one is all tousled hair, loads of lashes, and leather and lace from the likes of Tom Ford, Roberto Cavalli, and Saint Laurent. Effortless? No. Fabulous? Absolutely!

some noise. Taffeta rustles, silk whispers, embellishments jangle, and leather creaks with intent. Performance is not only visual. The swish of a skirt on a staircase is part of the entrance, and the weight of crepe hugging the hips is part of the walk. The late, great Tina Turner was a master of clothes that created a symphony as she writhed around the stage; often, they were the creations of the late, great Bob Mackie. Björk went viral earlier this year at the Venice Biennale in a neon-pink Bottega Veneta dress made of fiberglass shards that jingle-jangled together as she fist-pumped. The lesson? Dress for the ear as well as the eye.

the precision of a uniform. The chef’s whites, the lawyer’s suit, the professor’s tweeds, the tailored black of the gallery assistant: Each is a costume designed with a very particular audience in mind. Even people who wear the same thing every day are making a statement, and the strongest of them curate that repetition with real rigor. Consistency, done well, is a kind of theater. Just don’t make it boring! Consider Steve Jobs, who famously wore only Issey Miyake turtlenecks and Levi’s. Truly the only tech titan to ever have taste!

the art of quick change. For Céline Dion’s fall residency at the Plénitude Arena in Paris, underneath the spotlight wasn’t the only site where a performance took place. Like all true divas, Dion knows that every moment of every day is an opportunity to perform. Outside of the Royal Monceau hotel, her home in Paris, where her faithful fans lined up to greet her, she treated onlookers to a dance, a song, an air-kiss, and always a fashion show: off-the-runway looks from Jacquemus, Loewe, and Phoebe Philo. As they say, all the world’s a stage.

the ordinary garment its moment. Not every act needs sequins. A perfect white tee, a pair of jeans that have taken on the shape of a life, a forever leather jacket worn with confidence: these are the understudies who steal the show. Performance in fashion is not always about drama. Sometimes it is the quiet authority of a simple thing worn exactly right. A disciple of this way of dressing? Kim Gordon, whose effortless style, both onstage and offstage, has made her your favorite celebrity’s favorite celebrity for the past 40 years.
